# Exterior Binding

->[Decorative Leather Cover]<-

This page shows the front cover of the manuscript, a fine example of 18th or 19th-century craftsmanship. The binding is made of polished leather treated with a decorative <term>tree calf<note>A marbling technique where acidic chemicals are dripped onto the leather, allowing them to run and form a pattern that resembles the trunk and branches of a tree.</note></term> effect. 

The edges of the cover are adorned with a <term>gold-tooled<note>A decorative process where a craftsman uses a heated metal tool to press gold leaf into the leather, creating a permanent, shimmering design.</note></term> border. This border features a delicate, repeating pattern of leaves that frames the central marbled panel.

The center of this page features the personal <term>bookplate<note>Also known as an ex-libris, this is a printed label pasted into a book to indicate ownership.</note></term> of Alfred M. Hellman. The plate depicts an engraved scene of a scholarly lectern holding an open book, with more volumes resting on a shelf below. 

The Greek motto surrounding the image, "Concerning the Medical Soul," reflects the owner's professional and personal interests. Alfred M. Hellman (1881–1955) was a prominent New York obstetrician and a renowned collector of rare medical texts. His collection was particularly famous for works related to early obstetrics and gynecology. The presence of this bookplate indicates that this manuscript was once part of his significant private library before entering a public or institutional collection.

## DÜRER (ALBRECHT). Of Albrecht Dürer, the most famous painter and Geometer, on the Symmetry of parts in the right forms of human bodies, books translated into Latin <note>original: "Alberti Dureri clarissimi pictoris et Geometrae de Sym(m)etria partium in rectis formis hu(m)anorum corporum libri in latinum conversi"</note> (by Joachim Camerarius).

Folio 81b: This work was printed in Nuremberg in the summer of the year 1532 from the birth of Christ the Savior. In the house of Dürer’s widow. <note>original: "Norimbergae excudebatur opus aestate Anni A Christo servatore genito M. D. xxxij. In aedib. viduae Durerianae."</note>

Folio, <term>Gothic types<note>A style of type modeled after medieval handwriting, common in early German printing.</note></term>, with Greek passages, (81) leaves, (1) blank leaf with <term>signature<note>Letters and numbers at the bottom of pages used by binders to assemble the book in the correct order.</note></term> <sig>(A-E 6 F 4, G-O 6)</sig>; <term>xylographic monogram<note>A woodblock print of the author's initials, in this case, Dürer’s famous "AD" logo.</note></term> on title-page; 80 full-length woodcut human figures and numerous other diagrams; <margin>contemporary manuscript marginal note.</margin> Fine, large copy.

->For the widow of Dürer, Nuremberg, 1532.<-

FIRST LATIN EDITION of parts 1 and 2 of *Herein are contained four books on human proportion by A. D.... Nuremberg, 1528.* <note>original German title: "Herinn sind begriffen vier Bücher von menschlicher proportion"</note>
Campbell-Dodgson, I, p. 265.

## DÜRER (ALBRECHT). Two books by the most famous Painter and Geometer Albrecht Dürer on the variety of figures, the bending of parts, and the gestures of images, which are now added as a first Latin translation to the previously published books on symmetry. The year 1534. <note>original: "Clariss. Pictoris et Geometrae Alberti Dureri de varietate figurarum et flexuris partium ac gestib. imaginum, libri duo, qui priorib. de symmetria quondam editis nunc primum in latinum conversi accesserunt. Anno M. D. XXXiiij."</note>

Folio 59b: The work was finished in the year 1534 from the life-giving birth [of Christ], on the 9th day before the <term>Kalends of December<note>Following the Roman calendar, this corresponds to November 23rd.</note></term>. Published at the expense of Dürer’s widow by Hieronymus Formschneyder in Nuremberg. <note>original: "Finitum opus Anno a salutifero partu. 1534. 9. Cal. Decemb. Impensis viduae Durerianae per Hieronymum Formschneyder Norimbergae."</note>

Folio, Gothic types with Greek passages, (59) leaves, (1) blank leaf (cut away), with signatures <sig>(a- k 6)</sig>; folios, signatures a 2 and 3; l 5 and 6 and f 1 and 2 remain connected and folded together as extended plates; 60 full-length woodcut human figures, extended and other woodcut figures; a fine large copy in calf, gilt, <term>interior gilt dentelles<note>Decorative lace-like patterns pressed in gold leaf onto the inner edges of the book cover.</note></term>, <term>gilt edges<note>The edges of the pages have been smoothed and coated in gold leaf.</note></term>.

->Hieronymus Formschneyder for the widow of Dürer, Nuremberg, 1534.<-

FIRST LATIN EDITION of parts 3 and 4 of the original German work.

> Campbell Dodgson, volume 1, page 265: "A continuation of the last, being a translation of Books 3 & 4 of the original German edition, with the same cuts."

> Frank-Choulant, History and Bibliography of Anatomic Illustration, page 145: "...These two volumes contain the complete translation of the original German edition, by Joachim Camerarius the elder.... the woodcuts are prints from the original blocks.... This work...is technically interesting because it contains the first attempts to represent shades and shadows in wood engraving by means of <term>crosshatching<note>A technique using sets of parallel, intersecting lines to create the illusion of depth and shadow.</note></term>."

Albrecht Dürer, painter and engraver, born Nuremberg, 1471; died 1528.

<note>Historical manuscripts often contain blank leaves between major sections. These served several purposes: they protected the text from "bleed-through" (ink soaking through from the other side), provided space for future owners to add their own notes, or simply occurred due to the way the parchment or paper was folded into "gatherings" or "quires." The brown spotting often seen on such pages is called "foxing," a result of aging and humidity affecting the paper's fibers.</note>

# The Books of Albrecht Dürer, the most celebrated painter and Geometer, on the Symmetry of parts in the upright forms of human bodies, translated into Latin.

<note>original: "Alberti Dureri clarissimi pictoris et Geometrae de Symetria partium..." Albrecht Dürer (1471–1528) was a titan of the German Renaissance. By identifying himself as a "Geometer" alongside "Painter," he asserts that art is a science rooted in mathematical principles.</note>

### To the Reader.

> If by chance you read these words, produced by a bold German hand
> And now commanded to flow through Latin <note>original: "ausonios" – a poetic reference to the Ausonians of Italy, here meaning the Latin language</note> sounds,
> Grant pardon for any errors, for we do not claim to be without them,
> And let the work be recommended to you by its novelty.
> For this labor lacked a previous model, nor were there
> Any footprints of predecessors for us to follow.
> If, however—for I do not condemn all our efforts—any of these
> Writings please you, let your decorating favor increase them.

->Jo.<-
<note>Jo. is the abbreviation for Joachim Camerarius (1500–1574), a famous humanist scholar. He translated Dürer's original German text into Latin to ensure the artist's theories could be read by the international "Republic of Letters"—the scholars and scientists of all Europe who used Latin as their common language.</note>

<note>Below the text, the page features the iconic **AD** monogram. This emblem, where the 'D' sits inside the legs of the 'A', was one of the first "logos" in art history. It functioned as a legal trademark and a seal of quality for Dürer’s prints and books.</note>

<summary>This is the title page of the Latin translation of Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion," featuring an introductory poem by the translator Joachim Camerarius.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Joachim Camerarius, human proportion, Renaissance anatomy, Latin translation, geometry in art, title page</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>Continuing from the title page, this leaf contains the legal "Imperial Privilege," a 16th-century precursor to copyright. The OCR indicates noticeable bleed-through from the other side of the paper, common in books of this age.</meta>

->It is warned by an edict of the Lord Charles V, Roman Emperor, that no one<-
<note>original: "D. Caroli .V. Imper. Rom." – Charles V (1500–1558) ruled one of the largest empires in history. His "edict" here provides the legal weight to protect Dürer's intellectual property across the Holy Roman Empire.</note>
->may, with impunity, print the writings of Albrecht Dürer, whether in this genre [human proportion] or his<-
->Elements of Geometry, or his works on <term>Fortification</term>,<-
<note>original: "tichismatica" – derived from the Greek word for "wall." This refers to Dürer's 1527 treatise on the construction of city walls and defenses, one of the first major works on modern fortification.</note>
->nor may they sell those already printed;<-
->for those acting to the contrary, a penalty has been decreed<-
<note>original: "multa" – specifically a monetary fine or legal penalty.</note>
->which is contained in the official document of privilege.<-
<note>An "Imperial Privilege" was an early form of copyright. Because there were no international copyright laws, an author or publisher had to pay for a "Privilege" from the Emperor to gain the exclusive right to print and sell a book for a set number of years, usually under threat of a heavy fine and the confiscation of illegal copies.</note>

<summary>This page presents the formal legal protection (Imperial Privilege) granted by Emperor Charles V to protect Albrecht Dürer's published works from piracy.</summary>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Continuing from the Imperial Privilege, this page begins the formal preface to Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion." This introduction was written by Dürer's close friend, the humanist scholar Joachim Camerarius. Signature mark: A ij.</meta>

# To the Fair and Studious Readers of the Liberal Arts:

If I had decided that anything should be said about painting in this place, I would have celebrated it not so much with praises as with lamentation. For who among you can be unaware of the honors belonging to this most lauded art? Indeed, who would find fault with me, rather than thinking I ought to pursue with grief and tears the loss of its flowering and the neglect of its regrowth? But it was not my intention to speak of the art itself now, but rather to discuss, by a certain necessity, the artist who is the author of this book, and our own work. I trust that he has become known through his virtue and merits, not only to his own country but even to foreign nations. I understand well enough that his praises do not wait for our public proclamations, especially since his exceptional works leave him adorned and enriched with everlasting glory. Nevertheless, we judged it appropriate—both because we were publishing his inventions and because the occasion was given to commit to writing the life and character of a most noble man and our very dear friend—to weave together what we have learned of him, partly from the talk of others and partly as eyewitnesses. This will offer some declaration of his dexterity and singular genius, both as an artist and as a man, and will also bring no small pleasure to the readers. 

We have learned that our Albrecht originated from **Hungary** <note>original: "pannonia" — the Roman province roughly corresponding to modern-day Hungary, where Dürer's father's family originated.</note>, but his ancestors moved to **Germany**. Regarding his origin and birth, therefore, there will be no room for a lengthy speech; for even if they were honorable, it is certain that they received more splendor from him than they bestowed upon him. Nature had given him a body conspicuous in its composition and stature, suited to the beautiful mind it contained. In this, **Hippocrates** <note>A famous Greek physician (c. 460 – c. 370 BCE) often called the "Father of Medicine." Camerarius is referring to the ancient idea that a person's physical appearance reflects their inner character.</note>—whose justice nature is accustomed to extol—did not forget him; for just as she wove a ridiculous body for the ridiculously-minded ape, so she is accustomed to surround noble minds with appropriate bodies. 

His head was keen, his eyes flashing, his nose noble and what the Greeks call **well-proportioned** <note>original: "τετράγωνον" (tetragonon) — literally "square" or "four-cornered," used here to describe a sturdy, harmonious, and classically "noble" facial structure.</note>. His neck was somewhat long, his chest broad, his stomach lean, his thighs muscular, and his legs steady. But you would have said you had seen nothing more elegant than his fingers. Furthermore, there was such sweetness in his speech and such wit that nothing was more unpleasant to his listeners than the end of his talking. To be sure, he had not pursued the study of letters <note>A common humanist trope; while Dürer was not a university-trained scholar of Latin ("letters"), he was highly educated in practical and scientific matters.</note>, but he had nonetheless mastered the knowledge of those things which are taught by them, especially of natural and **mathematical** things. Just as he understood the primary principles of these things and knew how to explain them in practice, so he also knew how to declare them in speech. His writings on **Geometry** <note>Dürer’s "Manual of Measurement" (1525) was the first book on mathematics published in German.</note> testify to this; in them, I do not see what more could be required of that science, at least as far as he judged it should be treated by himself. 

He was carried by a great ardor of mind toward embracing all honesty of character and life, which he maintained so well that he was deservedly held to be an excellent man. Yet he was not of a gloomy severity or an offensive gravity; on the contrary, whatever is thought to contribute to sweetness and cheerfulness—provided it was not alien to honesty or right—he did not neglect in his youth, and even as an old man he approved of such things, as are the remnants of **gymnastics** and **Music**. 

But above all else, nature had fashioned him for painting; wherefore he embraced the study of it with all his strength, and he was possessed by a care for learning the works of lauded painters everywhere among nations, and for understanding their methods and imitating what he approved of. Among these, he both experienced the kindness and attained the favor of the greatest kings and princes, and even of **Maximilian** <note>Holy Roman Emperor Maximilian I (reigned 1493–1519), Dürer's primary royal patron.</note> himself and his grandson **Charles, the August Emperors** <note>original: "Caroli AVGG." — Charles V (reigned 1519–1556). "AVGG" is an abbreviation for "Augusti," a plural imperial title.</note>, being honored by them with a non-negligible salary. But once his hand had reached, so to speak, maturity, then you could understand his sublime genius, loving of virtue, especially from his works. For he made all such things grand and of praiseworthy subject matter. There exist his praises of Maximilian, and there exist his immortal works of **astronomical history** <note>Referring to Dürer's woodcuts of the celestial maps and his work on the "Triumphal Procession" for Maximilian.</note>, concerning which things I have mentioned...

<summary>This page contains the beginning of a biographical preface for Albrecht Dürer, describing his ancestry, his remarkably handsome physical appearance, his intellectual brilliance in mathematics, and his favor under Emperors Maximilian I and Charles V.</summary>
<keywords>Joachim Camerarius, biography, physical description, Hippocrates, Hungary, Germany, geometry, Maximilian I, Charles V, character</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Joachim Camerarius to Albrecht Dürer's treatise on human proportion. The previous page introduced Dürer's background and the intent of this biography. This section transitions from a general philosophy of art and artist character to specific technical praise of Dürer's skill.</meta>

...it will be possible to judge—to say nothing of other things—that there is no work among them that any painter, whether from the ancients or in our own age and among any nation, would not wish to be called his own. Indeed, there is surely no surer or truer indication of each person’s nature than that which is provided by the works they have produced according to their craft. Two kinds of these works stand out far beyond the rest, emerging as if from the workshop of Nature herself, and they possess even more power in what we have just said. These are what are called **Representational** <term>Mimetika</term> <note>original: Μιμητικά; the Greek term for art that imitates or represents reality</note> and **Creative** <term>Poietika</term> <note>original: Ποιητικά; the Greek term for the act of making or composing</note>, that is, representing the likenesses of things either through speech or by hand. Of these, the former are properly named "poetic," while the latter—I do not know if they have a general name—are usually understood in four ways: statues, painting, sculpture, and **modeling** <term>plastice</term> <note>the art of molding figures in soft materials like clay or wax</note>.

Now, however, let us speak of painting, as our purpose requires; it is itself the most praised among those four, and was called "vocal poetry" by one of the Greek poets <note>Likely referring to the famous saying attributed to Simonides of Ceos: "Painting is silent poetry, and poetry is vocal painting."</note>. We shall truly believe, therefore, that painters are exactly like their own works, no differently than poets. They say that Polygnotus painted everything on a grander scale, while Pauson painted smaller things <note>Polygnotus and Pauson were ancient Greek painters; Polygnotus was known for noble subjects, Pauson for the grotesque or base</note>. Who does not understand from this that the former possessed a loftier mind, and the latter a humbler one? Martial says that he would not be a **Virgil** <term>Maro</term> <note>original: Maronem; referring to Publius Vergilius Maro</note>, but only a Marsus <note>Domitius Marsus, a contemporary poet of lower stature</note>, even if he had obtained a patron like Maecenas—but he should have looked to himself on that matter. For no one who has read his excellent verses in the genre of epigrams would hope for such a thing, as they reveal not only a strength too weak for greater themes but also a character entirely alienated from them, with many parts even foul and deformed. For this reason, I cannot recall without a laugh that sentiment of Catullus: "it is proper for a pious poet to be chaste himself, but his verses need not be." Or that other poet who wished to prove that his morals differed from his song.

But let us dismiss the poets for now. How many painters have there been—or rather, who among them all has not declared his own nature through his works? I will not mention the ancients now, being content with examples from our own times. To whom is it not obscure that not a few have sought the praise and admiration of the common crowd through the obscenity of their painting? While they published on panels things that cannot honorably be done except in secret—indeed, things which even if done in secret are not free from wicked crime and disgrace—who would believe these men to be modest, whose minds and right hands produced such things? We have also seen the tiny and truly well-colored paintings of many, in which some genius and diligence of the master was declared, but the art was still lacking.

Therefore, in this place, we shall most justly admire **Albrecht** as a most diligent guardian of holiness and modesty, showing himself through the grandeur of his paintings to be well aware of his own strengths. Yet this is so that nothing among his smaller works ought to be looked down upon. In these, you will find no line drawn at random or wrongly, no superfluous dot. What shall I say about the steadiness and certainty of his hand? You would swear that the lines he drew with no assistance—using only a brush, or often a reed or a pen—were marked out with a ruler, a square, or a compass, to the great admiration of those watching. Why should I mention how, with the right hand’s harmony with the conceptions of the mind, he often cast figures of any kind of thing onto paper immediately with a pen or brush, or as they say, "placed" them? In this, I foresee that it will surely seem incredible to readers that he sometimes began with parts of the body that were far apart, not only in the subject matter but on the page, which when joined together agreed so well that nothing could have been done more fittingly. 

<margin>By knowledge?</margin>

Doubtless, the singular mind of the artist, equipped with all knowledge and understanding of the truth and the consensus of parts among themselves, moderated and governed the hand itself, and commanded it to trust in itself without any supports. Similar was his readiness when holding the brush, with which he would write out even the smallest details on a linen cloth or a panel without any preliminary sketch, so that not only could nothing be faulted, but...

<summary>Camerarius argues that an artist's moral character is reflected in their work, contrasting Dürer’s "holy and modest" precision with the obscenity or technical weakness of other artists. He specifically praises Dürer's incredible manual dexterity and his ability to draw perfectly proportioned figures without sketches or mechanical aids.</summary>
<keywords>Joachim Camerarius, Albrecht Dürer, morality in art, Mimetika, Poietika, artistic technique, draftsmanship, Polygnotus, Martial, human proportion</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the praise of Albrecht Dürer’s technical mastery, specifically recounting famous anecdotes involving the Italian masters Giovanni Bellini and Andrea Mantegna. It transitions from general praise to specific examples of his legendary "steady hand."</meta>

<sig>A iii</sig>

...even everything else would find the highest praise. This was especially admired by the most celebrated painters, who, being very well-versed in such matters, were not ignorant of the difficulty involved. At this point, I cannot pass over in silence what happened between him and **Giovanni Bellini** <note>A leading Venetian painter and master of the Venetian school, known for his use of color and light</note>. Bellini was in Venice, and his reputation in this art was excellent throughout all of Italy. When Albrecht had come there, he easily formed a friendship with him, and—as usually happens—they began to show each other their works. While Albrecht candidly admired and placed great value on everything of Bellini’s, Bellini himself honestly praised many things in Albrecht’s works, but especially the **fineness of the hairs** <note>Dürer was famous for his ability to paint individual strands of hair with incredible precision</note>. It happened by chance that they were talking together about art, and when their conversation ended, Bellini said, "Albrecht, would you be kind and do a small favor for a friend?" Albrecht replied, "You shall have it, if indeed it is something I am able to provide." Then Bellini said, "I desire to receive from you as a gift one of the **brushes** <term>penicillis</term> <note>Fine brushes made of animal hair, used for detailed work</note> with which you are accustomed to draw such fine hairs." Albrecht, without delay, brought out many brushes similar to those of others and of the kind that Bellini himself used, and told him to choose the ones that pleased him most, or to take them all if he preferred. But Bellini, thinking himself deceived, said, "I did not mean these, but rather those with which you are accustomed to express several hairs and bristles with a **single stroke** <note>Bellini assumed Dürer used a special "multi-tipped" brush to achieve his realistic textures</note>, which must be spread out and separated by a wide gap. Otherwise, in such great length, such a perfect equality of curves and distances could not be maintained." Albrecht replied, "I use no others than these, and to prove this to you, you may watch." Taking up one of the brushes he had laid out, he produced very long and wavy hairs, such as are mostly seen on women, with a perfectly steady order and method while Bellini looked on in astonishment. Bellini later confessed to many that no mortal man’s word could have made him believe what he had seen with his own eyes.

<margin>Bellini</margin>

<margin>Mantegna</margin>
->The same honor was paid to him with remarkable sincerity by<-
**Andrea Mantegna** <note>An Italian painter and engraver known for his sculptural style and mastery of perspective</note>, who flourished in Mantua. He had recalled painting to a certain severity and law, for which he earned the highest praise, particularly for excavating broken and fragmented statues and setting them up as examples for the art. In his works, everything is hard and rigid, as his hand was not yet accustomed to following the intelligence and readiness of his mind. Yet, in art, nothing is considered better or more perfect than those works. Therefore, while he was lying ill in Mantua and heard that Albrecht was in Italy, he took care to have him summoned to him quickly. He intended to instruct Albrecht's facility and certainty of hand with his own knowledge and theory of things. For he had not infrequently complained in familiar conversation that either he lacked that facility, or Albrecht lacked his knowledge. Albrecht, delaying nothing and putting all other business aside as soon as he received the message, immediately prepared for the journey; but Andrea had died before Albrecht could reach Mantua. Dürer used to say that nothing more sorrowful had happened to him in his life. For although Albrecht was the greatest, his great and noble mind always yearned for something even higher.

->We have gazed in astonishment at a portrait of a bearded man, which, as<-
we have said, he expressed on the linen itself with the brush, with no preliminary sketches arranged beforehand, as is customary. The hairs of the beard are almost a **cubit long** <note>About 18 to 20 inches, highlighting the extreme detail of the work</note>, drawn so exquisitely and skillfully, with such consistent separation and style throughout, that the better someone understood art, the more they would admire it, and consider it incredible that no other aid had assisted the hand in depicting them. Furthermore, no filth or unseemliness exists in his works, as the thoughts of his most chaste mind naturally shrank from all such things. O, an artist worthy of such success! Then, how similar, how infallible, and how true were the expressions of living faces, which they now call **likenesses** <term>contrafacta</term> <note>original: "contrafacta"; a term for portraits that "counterfeit" or exactly mirror nature</note>? He achieved all these things because he had brought back practice to art and reason—a method hitherto unknown and unheard of, at least by our local painters. For which of them was there who could explain the theory of his own work, even that for which he had achieved the greatest fame?

<summary>This page describes Dürer's technical supremacy through his interactions with the masters Bellini and Mantegna, highlighting his legendary ability to paint hair with a steady hand and his desire to combine practical skill with theoretical knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Giovanni Bellini, Andrea Mantegna, Venice, Mantua, portraiture, brush technique, art theory, Renaissance painting</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Albrecht Dürer’s theoretical works, shifting from anecdotes about his artistic skill to the scholarly effort of recording his methods. It describes how Dürer transformed painting from a manual craft into a systematic science and explains the origins of this Latin translation of his German texts.</meta>

...knowledge which one might have believed he had found by chance. But for Albrecht, everything was prepared, certain, and ready at hand, because he had led painting onto the path of instruction and recalled it to the reason of doctrine. Without this, as **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and orator, who argued that art requires both natural talent and systematic study</note> wrote, even if something is done well with the help of nature, nevertheless, because it happens by chance, it cannot always be ready. He first worked these things out for himself. Soon, being most generous and sincere, he set out to explain them in written books for the illustrious and most learned man **Willibald Pirckheimer** <margin>Pirckheimer</margin> <note>A prominent Nuremberg lawyer, humanist, and Dürer’s closest friend</note>, to whom he also dedicated them with a most elegant letter. Since we felt that rendering that letter into Latin while preserving its untouched, native character was beyond our powers, we have left it intact <note>Camerarius chose not to translate Dürer’s original German dedication to Pirckheimer to avoid losing its specific personal tone</note>. But before he could finish everything and publish them in a corrected form as he had desired, he was snatched away by death—a death that was peaceful and even desirable, but certainly, in our judgment, premature. 

If there was anything in that man that seemed like a flaw, it was his unique and infinite diligence, and a self-scrutiny that was often hardly fair to himself. Thus, death took him away from the publication of the work he had begun, which his friends nevertheless completed from his own writings. About this work, and about our version of it, we shall now say a few words. 

The work itself, begun by him to be elaborated in a certain **geometric manner** <term>geometirco modo</term>, seems a little unpolished and lacking the "stain" of literature <note>meaning it lacked the flowery style of a trained scholar</note>, being somewhat rough; but this is easily compensated for by the goodness of the subject itself. A few days before he met his fate, he had asked me to make it Latin even as he was refining it, and I did not begrudge him my labor and study. But death stole from him this care and the opportunity to polish the work—as it steals almost everything. Afterward, his friends obtained my consent—not only through prayers but by their own authority—to undertake the translation into the **Latin language**, so that this task, accepted while Dürer was alive, might be performed for him now that he is dead. 

Indeed, I was not unaware of how great a burden I was taking up in handling a subject that was not entirely clear to me, nor one in which I had anything in the Latin language to set down as an example to imitate. 
<margin>
<unclear>How much more difficult</unclear><br/>
<unclear>it was for [the author]</unclear><br/>
<unclear>from the author's [notes]</unclear><br/>
<unclear>to the people of Nuremberg</unclear><br/>
<unclear>which [was] most [important]</unclear><br/>
<unclear>[...] for Nuremberg</unclear>
</margin>
It also did not escape me how much more difficult it would be, occupied as I am with other ordinary business; I saw that I could not devote as much diligence and persistence to this translation as I desired and as the work itself deserved. But what was I to do? Out of respect for a man who was my dearest friend, and by the will of his surviving friends—who have a certain right to command me—I was swept along against all difficulties to enter upon a path that was neither level nor easy for me. 

Therefore, I have devoted as much leisure as I could steal from my necessary occupations to two books of the entire volume, and I have attempted to teach in Latin, however I could, those things which he had taught so excellently in the **German language** <term>teutonica lingua</term> <note>original: "teutonica lingua"</note>. I trust I shall easily find pardon among you, the good students of the arts, for this deed, as I neither wish nor am able to defend myself from appearing bold. Indeed, I believe that such boldness should not only be forgiven in places, but even supported. If I learn that my industry and study in this part have been pleasing to you, I shall be encouraged to translate the remainder of Albrecht’s **instructions on painting** <term>de pictura præceptionum</term> into Latin, a task that will be as much more refined as it is more laborious. 

However, you should not only expect his writings on this subject, but also his works on **geometry** as well as **fortification** <term>tichismatica</term> <note>from the Greek "teichos" (wall); Dürer wrote a famous treatise on defending towns and castles</note>, in which he described the defenses of cities as the conditions of these times require. These are generally the things about which he composed books. For I hear that some people are promising the public, either in their writings or conversations, a little book by Albrecht on the **symmetry of the parts in the bodies of horses**; I truly wonder where they will produce it from after his death, since he never finished it while alive. Although it is no secret to me that Albrecht began to seek the truth of proportions in this genre as well, the labor of the author will be no less now that he is dead than it would have been while he was living.

He also lists Dürer's authentic works on geometry and fortification while casting doubt on rumored posthumous books about equine symmetry.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the preface by Joachim Camerarius to the Latin translation of Albrecht Dürer’s works. Following the description of Dürer’s death, the text now addresses the integrity of his manuscripts, the problem of unauthorized editions, and the scholarly rigor applied to this specific translation.</meta>

A iiii

**Albrecht**, **Albrecht Dürer**, **Geometry**, **the German language**, <term>original manuscripts</term> <note>original Greek: αὐτογράφων (autographon)</note>, <term>diagrams</term> <note>original Greek: σχημάτων (schematon)</note>

And, what would be most contrary to his nature, I know that it was not a failure to complete his measurements, but rather that whatever had been achieved was lost through the treachery of certain individuals. It was through them that the author’s commentaries were stolen, so that he never again wished to start that work over from the beginning. He did not fail to suspect—or rather, he had discovered—from where the drones <note>original: "fuci"; a metaphor for plagiarists or lazy individuals who live off the work of others</note> had flown in to steal his labors. But that best of men preferred to overlook it, even to his own detriment and pain, rather than forget his own mildness and gentleness by pursuing them. Therefore, we will not allow any of this <note>referring to the stolen or corrupted versions</note> to be blamed on Albrecht the author, as it would be unworthy of such a great artist. 

A few small German papers also appeared some years ago, containing instructions on that subject that were poorly gathered and almost unsuitable. I do not have the heart to speak of them at present, but unless I am very much mistaken, the author of that edition has regretted it more than once. However, those works which we mentioned a little while ago—which are easily proven to be Albrecht’s own, not only because he prepared them for publication himself but also because they are excellent—you will receive from this source in a form much better, I believe, than from anywhere else. 

This is not because we are more prepared in terms of erudition and learning than others, but because we are translating these works in his own workshop. Here, among the additions and corrections of his <term>original manuscripts</term> <note>original Greek: αὐτογράφων</note> and the variety of his drafts, we are working as if in the light of many things that must necessarily seem obscure to others, however learned they may be. His book on <term>Geometry</term> <note>original: "Geometricus"; referring to Dürer's "Manual of Measurement" (Underweysung der Messung)</note> will demonstrate this; a most learned man now has it in hand and will soon release it in a form much more elegant and explained than it is currently read. Indeed, it will contain the addition of no fewer than twenty-six <term>diagrams</term> <note>original Greek: σχημάτων</note> or figures. Furthermore, in rereading the version already released to the public, he had made it better and richer through innumerable corrections and refinements. As if sensing that he would not be the one to publish it again, he had left instructions on what those who would publish it should do regarding both the text and the figures. 

Moreover, we will take care to see it printed in the <term>German language</term> <note>original: "teutonica lingua"</note> just as the volume was composed by the author, as soon as we are able. It is only fair that you receive these things with gratitude, returning thanks to the most burning desire of this singular man to contribute something to the public good through his inventions, and to our own labors and eagerness to spread to all people those things which seemed written for only one nation.

<summary>Camerarius defends Dürer's legacy against plagiarists and explains that this new edition is uniquely accurate because it was prepared directly from Dürer’s original, corrected manuscripts in his own workshop.</summary>

This page continues the preface by Joachim Camerarius to his Latin translation of Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion." Having previously discussed the integrity of Dürer's work and the issues with unauthorized editions, Camerarius now addresses his friend and patron, Christophorus Coler, explaining the linguistic and technical choices made during the translation process from German to Latin.

# Foreword to the Books on Proportions <note>original Greek: συμμετριῶν (symmetriōn)</note> of **Albrecht Dürer**, addressed to the most distinguished and learned Christophorus Coler.

Since, at your great urging, I had undertaken to translate into the Latin language these books by **Albrecht**, the dearest of your friends, it seemed appropriate to give you an account of my entire translation. This was done so that it might be recommended to readers by your judgment, and so that I might enter upon the path of this edition less fearfully, as if with you as my guide. Therefore, when I understood that the effort I had spent on the translation did not displease you, I decided to set forth the principles of my reasoning in this place. This allows me to show that you have approved of what I have done, and in this way, the work might appear more distinguished. I also hoped that this would bring some benefit to readers by sharing something of our spirit and thoughts. If any grateful work has been performed by us in this regard, the readers must credit it all to you, most distinguished sir, who acted not only as the author of the idea but also as the one who drove me to complete it.

## To Christophorus Coler, Distinguished Counselor

Since by your persuasion I took up the task of translating **Dürer's** books on painting, I decided to explain to you before publication what principles we followed in naming things throughout the entire work, so that according to your opinion, those choices might be retained or changed.

The author investigated, in the shaping of suitable and well-proportioned bodies, exactly how far certain parts should be from others. This elegant and beautiful arrangement of limbs is what constitutes beauty; therefore, in translating the title, we noted that **Symmetry** <note>the harmonious relationship of parts to a whole</note> is taught in these books. This was first given to painting, as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author of "Natural History"</note> says, by **Parrhasius**, but was practiced most by **Euphranor**, who also composed volumes on the subject. Although the Latin language lacks a specific certain name for this, we may translate it as "commensuration" or "proportion" <note>original: "commensum commensurationemue"</note>. And so, through art which follows nature, a method is sought by which everything is made in the expression of forms just as it would have been in reality.

In the measurement of bodies, there are two paths: one of height and the other of width. As you know, we have understood width in two ways. For even in profile drawings, as marked on a flat surface, I thought it would be sufficiently understood that this refers to "thickness," so that respect is given to the solidity of the body. To the same point belongs what they call "depth," as when one recedes from the right-hand parts, such as in a head turned sideways—for example, from the eyes to the ears. The measurement of height is indeed that of length. But we have considered the foot or the heel in a particular way. For the foot is long in a transverse sense, and thus the designation for a slanted part is given as a perpendicular height. 

Nor should it escape you if we sometimes simply name "parts"—it should be understood that we mean the beginnings or the tops of those parts, as we have generally added, though I think it was not always necessary and in some places even redundant. However, in working on someone else's book, we decided not to be too bold. For who would require the addition of "top" when "sinciput" <note>the front/top part of the skull</note> is said, especially since this word is formed from "top of the head" <note>original: "summo capite"</note>, unless I am mistaken? Furthermore, since the author sought out the names of the parts of the human body with almost painful diligence so that the measurements might be more certain, and even imposed new names on some, I trust that students of this translation will understand the difficulty even in this part. For I pass over the rest, which themselves could not have seemed easy, especially in a work of this kind which we were to imitate...

<summary>In this preface, Camerarius explains his translation methodology to Christophorus Coler, specifically defining "Symmetry" (proportion) and how he adapted Dürer's technical German terms into Latin architectural and anatomical vocabulary.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Joachim Camerarius, Christophorus Coler, Symmetry, proportion, Pliny the Elder, anatomy, translation theory, Parrhasius, Euphranor</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Joachim Camerarius is explaining to Christophorus Coler his methodology for translating Albrecht Dürer's technical terminology from German into Latin. He acknowledges the difficulty of matching Dürer's vernacular art terms with classical Latin vocabulary.</meta>

...nothing of the ancients would remain. However, we have also sought out with no small care nor over a short time the names by which we might render Dürer’s terms into Latin. As for those names we have applied, it would not be fitting for us to speak in the preface; rather, the judgment will rest with the readers. I would wish you to weigh their quality; if I satisfy you, there will be good hope regarding the judgments of others. Nevertheless, it would be fair, I think, for them to freely forgive our errors with similar goodwill, and for those who correct our work to put forward something better themselves. This will not only not offend us but will bring us pleasure along with utility. So that we hide nothing, we have placed the Latin names for the parts of the human body below, to which we added Dürer's German terms, so that it might be seen what we made from what.

## Names of the parts of the human body mentioned in these books.

**Fore-top of the head** <note>original Latin: Sinciput</note> which the Greeks call <term>bregma <note>the top part of the skull</note></term>. It is understood as the summit of the head, for which the ancients used the term "vertex" <note>peak</note>. <gloss>The crown of the head.</gloss>

**Vertex** <note>the crown</note> where the hair swirls in the middle of the skull. <gloss>The hindmost whorl.</gloss>

**Back of the head** <note>original Latin: Occiput and occipitium</note>. <gloss>The back of the head above the neck.</gloss>

**Hairline** <note>literally: roots of the hair</note>. <gloss>Hair-growth.</gloss> These are understood to be above the forehead and temples.

**Forehead.** <gloss>The forehead.</gloss>

**Eyebrows.** <gloss>Eyebrows.</gloss>

**Eyelids.** <gloss>Eyelids.</gloss>

**Nose.** <gloss>The nose.</gloss>

**Nostrils.** <gloss>Nostrils.</gloss>

**Mouth-corner** <note>original Latin: Bucca</note>. <gloss>Mouth.</gloss> For it seemed best to name the joining of the lips in the mouth thus, which the Greeks call <term>synarmogēn <note>a fitting together</note></term> or <term>symbolēn <note>a meeting point</note></term>.

**Cheeks** <note>original Latin: Genae or malae</note>. Also called "buccae." <gloss>The cheeks.</gloss>

**Temples.** <gloss>Sides of the head.</gloss>

**Neck.** Throat. Gullet. Nape. <gloss>The neck.</gloss>

**Shoulder blades.** <gloss>Shoulder flesh.</gloss>

**Shoulders.** <gloss>Shoulder.</gloss> And in the same place, it is noted that the shoulder bones protrude, that is, the heads inserted into the <term>ōmokotylē <note>the shoulder-socket</note></term>, meaning the socket joints <note>original Latin: acetabulis</note>. <gloss>The shoulder bones.</gloss>

**Collarbones** <note>original Latin: Iugula</note>. <gloss>Little hollow of the neck.</gloss> This is to be understood as <term>hē sphagē <note>the hollow of the throat</note></term>, that is, the pit in the middle of the collarbones. The Latins also called this the "iugulum."

**Chest.** <gloss>The breast.</gloss>

**Wings** <note>original Latin: Alae</note> or Armpits. <gloss>Armpits.</gloss> These are marked on both the front and back of the body.

**Breasts.** <gloss>Breasts.</gloss> **Nipples.** <gloss>The nipples.</gloss>

**Below the breasts.** <gloss>Under the breasts.</gloss> And in the same place, the lower chest is occasionally named.

**Loins.** Sides. Under the ribs. Where we are girded. The waist. <gloss>The flanks.</gloss>

**Cavity of the hips**, which stands above those hollows into which the head of the hip is inserted, which are the <term>kotylai <note>cups or sockets</note></term>, that is, socket-joints. The Greeks clearly call them <term>ischia <note>hip joints</note></term>. <gloss>The hip area.</gloss>

**Top of the thigh or hip.** <gloss>The end of the hip.</gloss> In some places, we have used "coxendices" <note>hip-bones</note>. Which however nearly...

...we have placed for the heads of the hips, that is **the bones in the hips**.

Abdomen. **End of the belly**.

The reverenced parts; the shameful parts; the pubes <note>original Latin: *verenda, pudenda, pubes*</note>. **The private parts**. To this belongs the glans, the scrotum, the little rod <note>referring to the penis</note>, and the testicles. But in women we have called the beginning of the pubes the "beginning of nature," that is, of the cleft <note>original Greek: χίσματος (*chismatos*)</note>; and the "lowest pubes" where it ends, which we nevertheless interpreted in some places with these words: "where the body becomes split." **The woman’s cleft**.

The ends of the buttocks. **End of the hind-parts**. The thigh. **The upper bone**.

The grooves of the thighs. **The indentation of the bone**.

Above the knee. **Above the knee**. Above the knee on the inside: the hollows of the knee <note>original Latin: *poplites*</note>.

Middle of the knee. **In the middle**. Below it. **Under the knee**.

The calf <note>original Latin: *sura*</note>. **Calves**. These are also noted as middle and lower. Likewise, outer and inner.

The shins <note>original Latin: *tibiæ*</note>. **Shin-bone**.

The ankle-bones <note>original Latin: *tali*</note>. **The ankles**. These are also paired, inner and outer.

The mountain of the foot or the convex foot. **The instep of the foot**.

The sole. **The soles**. We called the heel **the foot**. The heel-bone <note>original Latin: *calcaneum*</note>. **The heels**.

The arm. **Arm**. The shoulders. **Shoulder**. The armpits <note>original Latin: *alæ*</note>. **Armpits**. Muscles, that is, the fleshy parts. **The "mice"** <note>original German: *die mause*. The Latin word *musculus* literally means "little mouse," reflecting an ancient belief that the movement of a muscle under the skin resembled a mouse running.</note>.

The binding of the elbow, which in Greek is the *ankon* <note>original Greek: ἀγκών</note>, where the elbow is bent at an angle. **The elbow**.

The joint of the hand, in Greek the *karpos* <note>original Greek: καρπός</note>. **The wrist-joint**.

Hand. **Hand**. Palm. **The open hand**. Fingers. **Fingers and toes**.

Joints. **Joints**.

These are the things which we wished, as it were, to report to you. May you, most distinguished man, consider them diligently, and preserve your goodwill and favor toward us and our studies.

# Helius Eobanus Hessus also composed these for Dürer.

Now, reader, you hold in this small book many new 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Principles of <term>symmetry</term> <note>original: *symmetrica*. This refers to Dürer’s system of human proportions.</note> drawn with noble <term>additions</term> <note>original: *parergis*. In this context, supplementary drawings or ornamental details that accompany the main figures.</note>.
While you happen to read these, you will also perhaps notice 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That he possessed more than just the hands of a painter. 
He understood all things with certain judgment and 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Sharp wit; there was nothing that remained hidden from him. 
Whether a matter had to be managed through mathematical arts, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;No one was more skillful in such judgment than he. 
Or if cities had to be surrounded by suitable walls, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Dürer’s learned hand was able to depict them. 
Nor did he only paint; he also taught things in war 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which even famous generals were often unable to do. 
However, this is not the place to describe his praises, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which the public already possesses written in other poems. 
You, wise reader, should regard Dürer’s immortal honors 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As being like an extraordinary gift. 
And give thanks to his fatherland, the city of Nuremberg <note>original: *Noridos*. A Latinized poetic name for Nuremberg.</note>, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Through which these monuments have been handed down to you.

---

## And these.

How many monuments of his art <term>Apelles</term> <note>The most famous painter of ancient Greece, often used as the ultimate standard of comparison for Renaissance artists like Dürer.</note> left behind 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Is testified by various writings in many places. 
But of that noble artist’s work, none remains 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of which you could say: "this was painted by his hand." 
Am I wrong, or will this same fame belong to Dürer? 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And can the achievements of so great a man perish? 
Indeed, I do not believe it; for the same reason that destroyed 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The former artist allows this one to live on. 
The many things Apelles wrote could not survive 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Because they were written only with a light pen <note>A reference to the fragility of ancient manuscripts or the fact that Apelles' paintings themselves, being on wood or plaster, vanished.</note>. 
We compose many books in bright copper <note>original: *nitido... ære*. This refers to the printing press and the use of engraved copper plates, which allowed Dürer's work to be mass-produced and preserved.</note>, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which no sequence of fates can ever destroy.

->Apelles left behind few examples of his work, as is believable,<-
->Which any harmful hour was able to destroy. <note>original: *quælibet hora nocens*. The poet reflects on a common humanist trope: while the masterpieces of ancient Greece perished through fire, war, or decay, the invention of printing allows modern works to be preserved indefinitely.</note><-
->We produce so many thousands in a short time, as no<-

->Both those who were his contemporaries and those who came before.<-

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the actual treatise by Albrecht Dürer. Following the introductory poems and dedications of the previous pages, Dürer now transitions from the praise of his peers to the technical instruction of his craft. He introduces the "Regula" (measuring rod), the fundamental tool for his system of human proportion.</meta>

# Of Albrecht Dürer, most famous painter and Geometer,
## on Symmetry, Book
### One.

<margin>
Ruler <note>original: *Regula*</note>
Top of the head <note>original: *Vertex*</note>
</margin>

When you are about to depict the human form, follow this method. On a set ruler—which should exceed the length of the figure you wish to make—draw a line of the same height as you have decided the picture should be. Do this so that the first point of the line marks the top of the head <note>The *vertex* refers to the highest point of the skull.</note>, and the final point marks the heel. According to the variety of forms—whether you have conceived them in your mind as tall or short—adapt specific lines to each, and divide them individually. Therefore, whenever you hear the numbers of the parts mentioned in what follows, know that they are to be sought from the stature of the total height, from the top of the head all the way to the heel of the person whose figure is being established. This single line must be marked with the greatest care. This division may range from two parts up to fifty, or a hundred, or however many parts may finally be needed. 

You will refer all those parts, marked with their numbers, to that long line drawn on the ruler, so that they align with its first or initial point, but then differ according to their own variety, as is necessary. In this way, longer parts will be marked with smaller numbers, and conversely, shorter parts with larger numbers. <note>Dürer is using a fractional system: "2" represents 1/2 of the body, which is a large section, whereas "100" would represent 1/100th, a very small section.</note> For example, concerning the total length: the half-line is 2; the third part is 3; the fourth part is 4; and so on. If the work requires it, you may further divide these marked parts at your own discretion into other numbers, whether even or odd. For anyone who desires to measure something accurately, but cannot grasp everything with one certain division, is driven by necessity to the breaking down of parts into smaller units. 

That I have done this will appear later, for you will often see double or triple numbers placed—large, medium, even, and odd—by which I felt I could truly reach the reality of the subject. Moreover, the arrangement of these numbers which I have designated can be changed and used differently according to the talent of another artist. However, so that everything said by us so far and what will be said later may be better understood, I will here depict that ruler with its parts and the marks of those parts, just as I have taught how to make it. For before all else, the correctness of this ruler must be established; without it, immense labor and an infinity of time would be required to find the specific lengths of all the parts within the total height of the stature from the top of the head to the heel.

<margin>
Sole of the foot <note>original: *Planta*</note>
</margin>

<sig>B</sig> <note>This "B" is a "signature mark," used by early printers to keep the large sheets of paper in the correct order when binding the book.</note>

<summary>Albrecht Dürer introduces his mathematical method for drawing the human figure, centered on a measuring rod (regula) that divides the body's total height into proportional fractions. He explains that accuracy in art requires this geometric breaking down of the body into measurable units from the head to the heel.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometry, measurement, artistic theory, anatomy, measuring rod, symmetry</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical instructions on human proportion. Having introduced the "Regula" (measuring rod) on the previous page, he now applies it to a specific model: a "well-built and rustic" man. The catchword "ad genu" (to the knee) signals the transition to the next page's focus on the lower limbs.</meta>

Now I will show how you can measure the figure you have planned. For example, let us say the first male figure to be painted is of a well-built and rustic man, whose head is one-seventh of his total height. Draw a straight horizontal line, and upon it place three perpendicular lines of the same height as the figure you have proposed to make. Of these three lines, the first will be for the profile view <note>original: *obliquæ imagini*, literally "oblique image," referring to a side or three-quarter view</note>, the second for the front view, and the third for the back view. With these parallel lines arranged in the manner I have described, apply the measurement of the height of the limbs—that is, the notable parts—from the top of the head all the way to the lowest part of the sole. In this way, you will observe in our examples that we have tracked the height of the limbs through equally or directly drawn horizontal lines across the perpendiculars. We have marked these with their own distinct numbers, adding a number to the measurement of each part so that the certainty of this entire system might be immediately clear to the viewer.

You will find these principal horizontal lines are often called by several names, though sometimes more or fewer. In the first and highest place is the **top of the head** <note>original: *verticem*</note>; next below this line is the place for the **forehead**; below this, the **eyebrows**; in the lower, the **nose**; in the one also lower, the **chin**. And in this way, one descends to the **top of the shoulders**, the **hollow of the throat** <note>original: *iugula*</note>, the **chest**, the **armpits**, the **shoulder blades**, the **breasts**, below the **breasts**, the **waist** <note>original: *lumbos*, the loins or where one is girded</note>, the **navel**, the **curve of the hips**, the **hip joints** <note>original: *corendices*</note>, the **belly**, the **pubis**, the **end of the male organ** <note>original: *extremam glandem*</note>, the **bottom of the buttocks**, the **furrows of the thighs**, **above the knee**, **above the back of the knees**, the **middle of the knee**, **below the back of the knees**, the **bottom of the outer calf**, the **bottom of the inner calf**, the **instep** <note>original: *montem pedis*, literally "the mountain of the foot"</note>, the **bottom of the outer ankle**, and the **sole**.

We shall add these names in their proper place in the examples on the perpendicular lines by which the length of the limbs is indicated, so that the clarity of this system, which I have followed in the entire work, may be established.

-> With these things thus arranged, you shall measure the length of the limbs in this way. <-

From the top of the skull, which is called the <term>bregma</term> <note>The *bregma* is the anatomical point on the skull where the coronal and sagittal sutures meet.</note>, all the way to the top of the middle of the collarbone <note>original: *iuguli*</note>, let there be one-tenth and one-eleventh part. To the top of the shoulders, two parts. To the bottom of the chin, one part of seven <note>Dürer's "7" means 1/7th of the total body height.</note>. The very center of the top of the head is between the skull-top and the forehead. From the chin to the roots of the hair, one-tenth.

If you divide this <note>The face</note> into three equal spaces, the first will designate the **forehead**, the second the **eyes and nose**, and the third the **mouth and chin**. <note>desingnabit: original spelling for designabit</note> From the collarbone to the top of the chest, one-thirtieth. Under the armpits, one-thirteenth. To the breasts, one-tenth. Below the breasts, one-eighth. For the waist, two-elevenths. From the waist to the navel, one-fortieth. For the curve of the hips, one-thirtieth. For the bottom of the hip joints, one-tenth. For the private parts, one-eighth. For the end of the male organ, one-sixth. For the bottom of the buttocks, one-tenth and one-eleventh. From the bottom of the buttocks to where the thighs are, as it were, furrowed—that is, to the middle of the thigh—one-eighteenth. From the sole to the bottom of the ankle, one-twenty-eighth. From the sole to the instep, one-twentieth.

And having thus measured the length of the body all the way to the bottom of the thigh, it remains for the knee also to be placed in its own spot. When that is done, three unequal measurements of the total stature of the body will exist. Namely, from the top of the middle collarbone to the bottom of the thigh is the first and longest; from the bottom of the thigh to the middle of the knee is the second and indeed less long; from the middle of the knee to the bottom of the shin is the third and shortest. For the upper members—as is very easy to observe in a man—are usually longer and stronger. The **Body** <note>original: *Corpus*</note>, a name you often hear, you should understand as a fit length composed of parts and capable of motion. Now I return to those three measurements: know that they ought to agree with one another in a certain proportion, so that the proportion by which the length of the body matches the length of the hip—calculated from the top of the thigh to the middle of the knee—is the same proportion by which this is compared to the shin.

<summary>Dürer explains his fractional system for measuring a "rustic" male figure, providing a detailed list of anatomical landmarks and their proportional distances from the head to the feet.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, anatomy, measurement, bregma, Dürer's grid, artistic canon, Renaissance geometry</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on measuring the human figure. Dürer is moving from simple vertical measurements to a more complex geometric method for determining the proportional relationships between the collarbone, thigh, knee, and ankle.</meta>

B ij

Furthermore, you shall establish this rule—though I do not follow it in every instance—in the following way. Draw a triangle **a-b-c**. Let side **a-b** be the horizontal <note>original: *transuersum*</note> side. Let **b-c** be a vertical side, positioned so that **b** forms a right angle. Next, divide the vertical side **b-c** into three equal spaces using two points, **d** and **e**. From angle **a**, draw two straight lines to points **d** and **e**. By this method, you will derive the proportion I mentioned.

On a measuring rod <note>original: *regula*</note>, mark with two points the length of the entire body between the middle of the collarbone <note>original: *jugulum*</note> and the bottom of the thigh <note>original: *femur*</note>. Similarly, further down, mark the bottom of the ankle <note>original: *talum*</note> below the shin <note>original: *tibia*</note>. Label the point for the collarbone **f**, the bottom of the thigh **g**, and the bottom of the ankle **h**. Afterward, apply the rod so that point **g** touches line **a-d** of the aforementioned triangle. Do not move this point from that line; however, rotate the rod across those lines until point **f** reaches the triangle’s line **a-c**, and until the lowest point **h** reaches the horizontal line **a-b**. 

Once this is finished, line **a-e** will intersect the rod between **g** and **h**. At that intersection, mark the point **j**. You will then find three <term>proportional measurements<gloss>analogicas; measurements that relate to one another through a consistent ratio</gloss></term> designated alongside the length of the body. For the ratio of the space **f-g** to the space **g-j** is the same as the ratio of space **g-j** to space **j-h**. By point **f**, you should understand the top of the middle of the collarbone; by point **g**, the bottom of the thigh; by point **j**, the middle of the knee <note>original: *genu*</note>; and by point **h**, the bottom of the ankle below the shin. While doing this, notice that the rod applied to the triangle must be tilted with point **f** turned upward toward the vertical side **c-b**. You may call such a triangle an **equator** <term>equalizer<gloss>æquatorem; a geometric instrument for finding means or balanced proportions</gloss></term>. You can also use this calculation in reverse when you wish to invert a figure. Truly, it will provide great utility for the achievement of many things to those who use it correctly.

<summary>Dürer describes a geometric tool involving a right-angled triangle and a measuring rod to determine the harmonic proportions of the lower body. This method allows an artist to find the exact placement of the knee and ankle relative to the torso.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, human proportion, measuring rod, anatomy, collarbone, thigh, knee, ankle, equator, artistic theory</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of human proportions, moving from the torso and legs to the specific measurements of the arm using a geometric projection system.</meta>

Following this, we arrive at the measurement of the arm <note>original: *brachij mensuram*</note>, which we have set down as follows:

From the shoulder <note>original: *humero*</note>—specifically where the head of the humerus connects to the collarbone <note>original: *jugulum*</note>—down to the elbow <note>original: *cubitum*</note> measures two units of 11.

Furthermore, from the shoulder to the lowest point of the muscles is one unit of 10.

From the elbow to the very tips of the fingers <note>original: *digitos*</note> is one unit of 4.

From the fingertips back to the end of the hand <note>original: *extremam manum*; the wrist</note> is one unit of 10. However, it is not ungraceful <note>original: *inconcinnum*</note> if one prefers to extend this to one unit of 9, should that by chance please the artist.

->**[Geometric Diagram of Proportions]**<-

<note>The diagram illustrates a right-angled triangle labeled **a-b-c**. A series of lines radiate from point **a** to the vertical axis, intersecting a diagonal line representing the "Equator" or the central proportional divider. This geometric method allows the artist to maintain consistent ratios even when the scale of the drawing changes.</note>

<summary>This page details the proportional measurements of the human arm, from the shoulder to the fingertips, using a specific unit-based system. It includes a geometric diagram (Equator) used to calculate these ratios visually.</summary>

And such is the measurement of the height of all the limbs. Now we shall indicate the width from the transverse lines drawn across the limbs of the figure in an oblique view.

| | |
|:---|:---|
| You shall lay out the **palm** itself at one 25th and one 15th. | You shall mark the **anus** <note>original: *podicem*</note> itself within the cleft of the buttocks at a height of one 8th. |
| Finally, at the third line, you shall make the figure from the **back** broad across the **shoulder blades** <note>original: *scapulas*</note>, that is between the armpits: one 4th. | You shall make the **heel** broad to one 24th. |

And in this way and manner, the height and width of all the limbs of this body having been described in three ways, you shall consequently sketch the appearance and shape of the drawing as appropriately as possible according to your own talent. Nor is it of little use—something I am personally fond of doing—to bring those outlines and figures into line with the proposed image of a living person of similar stature; in this way they will fall more fitly and gracefully than if they were drawn haphazardly.

Indeed, I have expressed everything in the examples as exactly as I could. And it is remarkable in this part above all others with what skill nature has fashioned the human being, as if joined from two parts, and how she has joined the trunk onto the <term>hip-joints<note>original: *corendicis*; referring here to the pelvic structure or the hip sockets where the torso meets the legs.</note></term> as if onto a base. At which place in the examples we have carefully curved the lines around the belly and the <term>sacrum<note>original: *sacrum os*; the large triangular bone at the base of the spine.</note></term>, marking out such a form. These things, serving as examples of the first figure, you may mark with some sign so everything is clearer, such as the letter **.A.** for instance.

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the study of the "Type A" male figure to a more robust female figure, which Dürer categorizes as the "rustic" (agrestis) type. It maintains the same mathematical approach to human proportion but introduces a more simplified visual method for the accompanying diagrams.</meta>

After this, we will describe the figure of a woman who is somewhat stout, sturdy, and rustic, <note>Dürer uses the term "agrestis" (rustic or peasant-like) to describe a body type that is shorter and broader—seven heads high—than his more "idealized" figures.</note> whose form is very similar to the masculine type shown previously. Let her height also be set at seven of her own heads. Therefore, follow the method previously established, according to which you should also measure the parts of this body. Furthermore, in the examples, I have decided to omit the horizontal lines of the widths from now on, and instead only write the numbers that indicate those widths, though still marking all the locations with small dots. I thought this should be done to avoid the confusion of too many lines <note>original: "odium crebritatis in lineationibus"; literally "the hatred of the crowd of lines," referring to the visual clutter that occurs when too many measurement lines overlap.</note> in the drawings. You shall therefore form the limbs of such a female body according to the following measurements.

---

## Vertical Measurements of the Body

Between the top of the head <note>original: "sinciput"</note> and the top of the shoulders: one 10th and two 23rds.
And this same amount between the collarbones <note>original: "iugula"</note>: two 11ths.
And also between this and the bottom of the chin: one 7th.
Between the chin and the crown of the head: one 8th.
Between the chin upward to the top of the forehead: one 10th.
<gloss>This 10th part you shall divide into three equal parts: the top contains the forehead, the middle the nose and eyes/ears, and the bottom the jaw and chin.</gloss>
Furthermore, under the chin, you shall draw a fleshy part like a dewlap. <note>original: "palaear"; a term usually referring to the fold of skin under a cow's neck, used here to describe the neck flesh of a sturdy woman.</note>
Between the collarbones and the loins: one 5th.
Between the collarbones and just below the breasts: one 7th.
Between the collarbones and the nipples: one 9th.
Between these and the armpits: one 15th.
And between these and the chest: one 28th.
<note>In this place, you will observe that the back parts of the armpits—that is, those at the rear—are lower than those in the front.</note>
Between the loins and the navel: one 22nd.
Between the loins and the hip sockets <note>original: "coxendices"</note> or the top of the femur: one 9th.
Between these and the bottom of the belly: one 8th.
Between these and the bottom of the pubic area: two 11ths.
Also between these and the furthest point of the buttocks: one 5th.
Between the sole of the foot and the arch <note>original: "convexitatem pedis"</note>: one 20th.
Between that and the outer ankle: one 28th.
Between the bottom of the ankle and the middle of the knee: two 9ths.
<note>Or you may do this using the "equalizer" method we showed previously. In that case, the knee will stand a little lower than before, but you may follow whichever method you prefer.</note>
Between the middle of the knee and the bottom of the calf: one 8th.
Furthermore, you shall make the heels long: one 6th.

---

## Measurements of the Arms and Widths

Next, you shall extend the length of the arm from the top of the shoulder to the elbow: two 11ths.
From the elbow to the tips of the fingers: one 4th.
From there backward to the joint of the hand <note>the wrist</note>: one 10th.
And so, having designated the heights of the limbs, you will consequently mark the widths. First, for the body in an oblique view <note>a three-quarter or side-angled view</note> in this manner:

**Widths of the Head and Torso**
The oblique head through the crown shall measure: one 9th.
<note>When I name the crown or any other part in the examples, look to their marked points and the matter will be clear to you.</note>
Through the forehead: one 14th and one 15th.
Through the eyebrows similarly: one 14th and one 15th.
Through the nose: one 8th.
Through the chin and the neck: one 12th.
Through the collarbones, let the width be: one 10th.
Through the chest, that is, above the breasts: one 7th.
Through the armpits: two 13ths.
Through the nipples: one 12th and one 13th.
Through the bottom of the breasts: one 7th.
Through the loins, that is, below the ribs where they are girded: similarly one 7th.
Through the navel: one 10th and one 11th.
Through the hip sockets: one 8th and one 10th.
The thigh, immediately below the buttocks: let it be one 7th wide.

**Widths of the Legs and Arms**
Above the knee, let the width be: two 19ths.
Through the middle of the knee: one 12th.
Below the knee similarly: one 12th.
Through the middle of the calf: one 20th and one 22nd.
Through the bottom of the calf: one 12th.
Through the bottom of the shin: one 18th.
And next, you shall mark the width of the arm thus:
Through the shoulders: one 11th.
Through the muscles <note>the biceps/triceps area</note>: one 12th.
Through the joint of the elbow: one 20th.

<meta>This page transitions from the female proportions discussed on the previous page to the measurements of a male body ("Type E" or similar). It maintains the same fraction-based system where the total height of the body is divided into units (thirds, eighths, tenths, etc.) to ensure mathematical harmony in art.</meta>

E

## Next follows the measurement of the limbs of the male body, whose head is an eighth part of its total height. You shall proceed in a similar way, so that you establish the heights of the limbs in this manner.

Between the top of the head <note>original: "sinciput"</note> and the collarbones <note>original: "iugula"</note> there will be a space of one 6th.
Between the top of the head and the bottom of the chin is one 8th.
Between the bottom of the chin and the top of the forehead is one 10th.
By this tenth, you shall divide the face into three parts as was shown above.
Between the top of the head and the waist where we are girded is one 3rd.
Between the collarbones and the armpits <note>original: "alas"</note> is one 14th; however, from the back, the armpits are lower.
Between the collarbones and the breasts is one 10th.
Between the top of the head and where the body divides into the legs is one 2nd <note>This marks the exact midpoint of the body.</note>.
Between the waist and the navel is one 29th.
Between the waist and the hollows of the hips <note>original: "sinus coxarum"</note> is one 18th.
Between the same and the hip bones is one 20th and one 18th.
Likewise, between those and the pubic region is two 13ths.
And between the same and the bottom of the buttocks is one 10th and one 11th.
Between the bottom of the buttocks and the bottom of the scrotum is one 40th.
From there also to the middle of the thigh is one 15th.
Between the sole of the foot and the outer ankle is one 27th.
Between the ankle and where the foot begins to curve <note>The instep.</note> is one 21st.
And between the same and the middle of the knee is one 4th.
In establishing the knee, you may also follow the ratio shown above by the center line <note>original: "æquatorem"</note>.
Between the middle of the knee and above the knee is one 30th.
Between the middle of the knee and below the knee is one 30th, and the knee shall be enclosed within these parts.
Between the middle of the knee and the bottom of the outer calf is one 9th.
Between the middle of the knee and the bottom of the inner calf is one 15th and one 16th.
And so you shall pass to the arm.
From the top of the shoulder to the elbow is one 5th.
From the elbow to the tips of the fingers is one 4th.
From the tips of the fingers to the wrist joint is one 10th.

---

### Next, you shall approach the width of the profile form <note>original: "obliquæ formæ"; Dürer uses "oblique" to refer to the side-view proportions.</note> in this manner.

Across the crown of the head, you will make the head one 10th wide.
Across the eyebrows, the face is one 8th.
Across the nose is one 9th.
Across the chin and neck is one 10th.
The neck itself below the chin is one 16th.
and a little lower is one 14th.
The body across the collarbones is one 12th.
Across the chest is one 7th.
Across the breasts is one 7th.
Below the breasts is one 14th and one 15th.
Across the waist is one 16th and one 17th.
Across the navel is one 17th and one 18th.
Across the hollows of the hips is one 8th.
Across the hip bones is one 7th.
Across the pubic region or the buttocks and thigh is two 15ths.
You shall thicken the thigh itself below the buttocks to one 9th.
Across the middle of the thigh is one 19th and one 20th.
Above the knee is one 14th.
Across the middle of the knee is one 15th.
Below the knee is one 16th.
Across the middle of the calf is one 13th.
Across the bottom of the calf is one 17th.
Above the instep <note>original: "montem pedis"</note> of the foot is one 24th.
You shall extend the heel itself to one 6th.

### Next, you shall widen the arm in profile thus:

Across the shoulder is one 13th.
Below the armpits is one 17th.
Across the elbow joint is one 24th.
Across the muscles of the forearm <note>original: "musculos cubiti"</note> is 22.
Across the wrist joint is one 10th.
Across the hand itself thus hanging down is one 34th.

### After this, you will mark the width of the front-facing form <note>original: "aduersæ formæ"</note> in this manner.

You will widen the head across the forehead to one 9th.
Across the eyebrows is one 10th.
Across the ears is two 17ths.
