<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of the male figure's proportions (Type E), specifically focusing on the widths and thicknesses of various body parts as fractions of the total height. It concludes the description of this specific figure type and refers to an accompanying illustration labeled "B."</meta>

Through the nose, one 12th.
The width of the neck itself below the chin will be one 16th.
You shall make the torso across the collarbones <note>original: "iugula"</note> one sixth wide; and there, between the bones of the shoulders, the part will be one 11th and one 12th.
You shall make the chest and shoulders one 4th wide.
The space between the armpits <note>original: "alas"</note> will be one 6th.
Between the breasts, one 9th.
Across the loins, two 13ths.
Through the hollows of the hips, one 6th.
Through the top of the thigh, one 10th and one 11th. And at that same point they shall be distant [from one another]. The hip joints <note>original: "coxendices"</note> from each other, one 14th and one 15th.
You shall make the thigh itself immediately below the buttocks one 11th in width; the middle, that is the furrows, one 13th.
Above the knee, it will be one 16th.
The middle of the knee, one 18th.
Below the knee, one 20th.
The middle of the calf <note>original: "sura"</note>, one 15th.

The bottom of the calf, one 20th.
The bottom of the shin, one 34th.
The width across the ankles will be one 27th.
But of the foot across the outer toes, one 16th.
And thus proceeding to the arm, you shall thicken it through the muscles below the armpits to one 24th.
Above the elbow <note>original: "cubitum"</note>, one 26th.
Through the muscles of the elbow, one 19th.
Through the joint of the hand [the wrist], one 16th.
Let the width of the palm be one 16th.
You shall make the rear view across the shoulder blades <note>original: "scapulas"</note> one 5th wide.
The cleft of the buttocks <note>original: "rima podicis"</note> will be one 10th.
The heel shall extend one 28th.

Having observed these things through measurements, you shall describe the figure exquisitely, as indeed we have striven to do in the example. This you may mark with the letter B, just as A was marked above.

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical measurements from Albrecht Dürer’s study of human proportions. While the previous pages defined the "Type E" male figure, this section introduces the female counterpart, which follows a similar mathematical logic based on the "one-eighth" head-to-body ratio.</meta>

<margin>Signature C iij</margin>

To this male form, we shall add a similar female form, just as we did previously, whose head shall likewise be an eighth part of her total height. You shall establish her proportions in this manner:

- From the top of the head to the collarbones <note>original: "iugula"</note>, the height shall be 1/6.
- And likewise from there to the bottom of the chin, 1/8.
- From the chin upwards to the top of the forehead, 1/10. This tenth part, as shown above, you shall divide into three equal spaces to be assigned to the forehead, the nose, and the chin.
- From the collarbones to the waist <note>original: "lumbos"</note>, 1/5.
- And from there to the armpits <note>original: "alas"</note>, 1/16.
- And likewise from there to the breasts, 1/11.
- And from there even to just below the breasts, 1/9.
- From the waist to the end of the buttocks, 1/11 and 1/12.
- From there to the bottom of the pubes, 1/13 and 1/14.
- And from there to the top of the thigh or the hip, 1/10.
- And from there even to the navel, 1/40.
- From the sole of the foot upwards to the ankle, 1/30.
- And from there likewise to the arch <note>original: "conuexitatem"</note> of the foot, 1/21.
- And likewise from there to the middle of the knee, 1/8 and 1/9.
- Or indeed, you may place the knee upon the center line <note>original: "æquatorem"</note>, as has been taught.
- From the middle of the knee to just above the knee, 1/26; and there the knee begins to emerge.
- From the middle of the knee to the bottom of the calf, 1/8.
- After this, you shall proceed to the arm, such that the length between the shoulder and the elbow is 1/5.
- From the elbow to the tips of the fingers, 1/4.
- From the tips of the fingers to the joint of the hand <note>the wrist</note>, 1/10.

---

And thus you shall measure the members of the female form in the width of the **oblique** <note>profile or three-quarter view</note> position:
- Across the forehead, it shall be 1/9.
- Across the eyebrows, 1/16 and 1/17.
- Across the nose, 2/19.
- Across the chin and the neck, 1/11.
- The neck itself, just below the chin, you shall thicken to 1/17.
- Across the collarbones, 1/14.
- Across the chest, or above the breasts, 1/9.
- Across the nipples <note>original: "papillas"</note>, 1/8.
- Below the breasts, 1/9.
- Across the waist, or below the ribs where the body is girdled, 1/9.
- Across the navel, 1/8.
- Across the top of the thigh or the hip bone <note>original: "coream," likely a variation of *coxam*</note>, 1/6.
- Across the thigh immediately below the buttocks, 2/17.
- Above the knee, 1/14.
- Through the middle of the knee, 1/16.
- Through the middle of the calf, 1/14.
- Through the lower calf, 1/18.
- Through the bottom of the shin, 1/26.
- Across the ankles, 1/17.
- The length of the heel itself shall be 1/7.
- You shall make the arm at the shoulders 1/14 wide.
- Below the armpits, 1/18. Above the elbow, 1/26.
- Across the muscles of the forearm <note>original: "musculos cubiti"</note>, 1/24.
- Across the joint of the hand, 1/40.
- The hand itself, hanging down in this way, 1/36.

---

Afterward, you shall seek out the width of the **frontal** <note>original: "aduersæ," facing forward</note> form in this manner:
- Across the forehead, the width shall be 2/19.
- Across the eyebrows, 1/10. Across the nose, 1/12.
- The neck below the chin shall thicken to 1/17.
- Across the collarbones, 1/7. Where, a little lower, you will mark the shoulder sockets <note>original: "acetabula humeri"</note>, distant from each other by 1/7.
- Across the chest, 1/9 and 1/10.
- Between the armpits, 1/7. Between the nipples, 1/10.
- Across the waist, that is, where they are girdled, 1/7.
- Across the navel, 2/11.
- Across the top of the thigh, 1/9 and 1/10. Where indeed you shall place the hip joints <note>original: "coxendices"</note> at a distance from each other of 1/7.
- Across the thighs below the pubes, 1/10.
- Above the knee, 1/15. Through the middle of the knee, 1/18.
- Through the middle of the calf, 1/16.
- Through the lower calf, 1/19.
- Through the bottom of the shin, 1/34.
- Across the ankles, 1/29.
- Below the ankles, that is, across the arch of the foot, 1/31.
- Across the tips of the toes, the width of the heel <note>referring here to the foot's spread</note> will be 1/20.

---

Then, complete the arm thus:
- Across the shoulder muscles, the width shall be 1/24. Above the elbow, 1/28.
- Across the muscles of the forearm, 1/22.
- Across the joint of the hand, 1/34.
- The palm itself shall be spread to 1/20.
- Finally, for the **rear** <note>original: "auersam," turned away</note> form, across the shoulder blades—that is, between the armpits from the back—you shall make it 2/13 wide.
- In the buttocks, the cleft shall be 1/9.
- The heel bone shall be 1/30 wide.

It covers vertical heights and widths for three different perspectives: profile, frontal, and rear views.</summary>

->B I<-

And in this way, you shall appropriately represent <note>original: "effinges," meaning to model, fashion, or portray in a lifelike manner</note> the figure by following the outlines <note>original: "ductus," referring to the anatomical contours or the strokes of the pen defining the limbs</note> of all 
the limbs; and now you can see how we have endeavored to do this in the 
example provided. Let this figure indeed be 
[marked] B. I.

C iiii

# Vertical heights of the female limbs.

Top of the head <note>Latin: sinciput sursum</note>
Forehead
Eyelids
Nose
Chin

Collarbones <note>Latin: juguli, the hollow of the throat where the neck meets the torso</note>

You may also modify these figures described just before this in certain places, according to the method that follows.

And first, for the height of the man down to where the body divides into the legs, make it 1/2 <note>Latin: "unius 2" likely refers to the fractional division of the body's total height, where "one" is the whole and "2" is the divisor</note>.

<note>Dürer’s vocabulary reflects a mix of classical Latin anatomical terms and descriptive phrases to ensure the artist knows exactly where to place their compass or ruler.</note>

**Technical Terms Used:**
<term>Sinciput: The front or top of the head.</term>
<term>Vertex: The highest point or crown of the head.</term>
<term>Frons: The forehead.</term>
<term>Supercilia: The eyebrows.</term>
<term>Nasus: The nose.</term>
<term>Mentum: The chin.</term>
<term>Summæ scapulæ: The uppermost part of the shoulder blades.</term>
<term>Juguli: The collarbones or the hollow of the throat.</term>
<term>Alæ: Literally "wings," used here for the armpits.</term>
<term>Mamæ: The breasts.</term>
<term>Lumbi: The loins or lower back.</term>
<term>Umbilicus: The navel.</term>
<term>Femur: The thigh bone or upper leg.</term>
<term>Coxa: The hip.</term>
<term>Aluus: The belly or abdomen.</term>
<term>Pubes: The pubic region.</term>
<term>Nates: The buttocks.</term>
<term>Genu: The knee.</term>
<term>Sura: The calf of the leg.</term>
<term>Tibia: The shinbone or lower leg.</term>
<term>Planta: The sole of the foot.</term>

->D ij<- <note>Signature mark indicating this is the second leaf of the fourth gathering (D).</note>

<summary>This page details the vertical proportional measurements for a posterior (back) view of the human body, providing specific fractional ratios for features ranging from the crown of the head to the sole of the foot.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, posterior view, anatomy, Renaissance art, mathematical symmetry, anthropometry</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of the female figure's posterior to a new section focusing on the male body. Dürer introduces a specific canon of proportions here: the "nine-headed man," where the head serves as the modular unit representing one-ninth of the total body height. This continues his systematic approach to defining "ideal" beauty through geometry and arithmetic.</meta>

### Next follows the proportioning of a male body whose head is a ninth part of its total length. The same ratio will be applied here as we followed in the previous sections. For you shall measure an image of this kind by its parts, and first by its length.

From the top of the forehead <term>Sinciput: The front or upper part of the skull; here used as the starting point for vertical measurement.</term> to the bottom of the chin, let the height be one-ninth [1/9].
From here, directed upwards to the hairline, one-tenth [1/10]. And this same height shall be for the crown of the head also. You shall also distribute the face into this same tenth, according to the method shown above.
From the top of the head to the tops of the shoulder blades, the height should be one-fifteenth and one-sixteenth [1/15 and 1/16].
And likewise from there to the collarbones, one-sixth [1/6].
From the collarbones to the top of the chest, one-twenty-eighth [1/28].
Below the armpits, one-fourteenth [1/14].
To the breasts, one-twelfth [1/12].
Below the breasts, two-nineteenths [2/19].
To the loins <note>original: "lumbos"; the narrow part of the waist or small of the back.</note> where we are girdled, one-sixth [1/6].
From here indeed to the navel, one-twenty-sixth [1/26].
To the hollow of the hips, one-twenty-second [1/22].
To the top of the thigh or the hips, one-ninth [1/9].
To the end of the belly, one-eighth [1/8].
To the pubic area, one-seventh [1/7].
To the furthest point of the buttocks, one-sixth [1/6].
From the furthest point of the buttocks to the creases of the thighs <note>original: "femimum sulcos"; referring to the gluteal fold or the transition between the buttock and the upper leg.</note>, one-eleventh [1/11].
From the sole of the foot to the highest curve of the foot, one-twenty-third [1/23].
And likewise from there to the ankle, one-thirty-fifth [1/35].
From the bottom of the ankle to the middle of the knee, one-fourth [1/4].
From the middle of the knee to just above it on the outside, one-twentieth [1/20].
From the middle of the knee to just above it on the inside, one-thirtieth [1/30].
From the middle of the knee to just below it on the outside, one-eightieth [1/80].
From the middle of the knee to just below it on the inside, one-fortieth [1/40].
Likewise from the middle of the knee to the bottom of the outer ankle, one-tenth [1/10].
Likewise from there to the bottom of the inner ankle, one-ninth [1/9].
The length of the heel should be two-thirteenths [2/13].
After these, you shall note the length of the arm in this way: let it be drawn from the shoulder to the joint of the elbow, two-elevenths [2/11].
From the joint of the elbow to the tips of the fingers, one-fourth [1/4].
From the tips of the fingers back to the joint of the hand <note>The wrist.</note>, one-tenth [1/10].

### Having designated the height of the parts, you shall also mark the width. First, for the oblique <note>original: "obliquae imaginis"; referring to a three-quarter or turned view rather than a flat frontal view.</note> image in this manner.

Let the head be one-twelfth [1/12] wide across the forehead.
Across the eyebrows, one-ninth [1/9].
Across the nose, one-tenth [1/10].
Across the throat and chin, mark two-twenty-thirds [2/23].
Across the neck below the chin, one-eighteenth [1/18].
Across the tops of the shoulder blades, one-seventeenth [1/17].
Across the collarbones, one-twelfth [1/12].
Across the top of the chest, one-eighth [1/8].
Near the armpits, one-fifteenth and one-sixteenth [1/15 and 1/16].
Across the breasts, one-eighth [1/8].
Below the breasts, one-sixteenth and one-seventeenth [1/16 and 1/17].
Across the loins where we are girdled, one-eighteenth and one-nineteenth [1/18 and 1/19].
Across the navel, one-eighteenth and one-twentieth [1/18 and 1/20].
Across the hollow of the hips, one-eighteenth and one-nineteenth [1/18 and 1/19].
Across the hips or top of the thigh, one-fifteenth and one-sixteenth [1/15 and 1/16].
Across the end of the belly and the buttocks, one-eighth [1/8].
Across the pubic area, one-sixteenth and one-seventeenth [1/16 and 1/17].
Let the thigh itself below the buttocks have a width of one-tenth [1/10].
And further down through the crease, one-eleventh [1/11].
Above the knee on the outside, let the width be one-fifteenth [1/15].
Above the knee on the inside, two-thirty-firsts [2/31].
Through the middle of the knee, one-eighteenth [1/18].
Below the knee on the outside, one-nineteenth [1/19].
Below the knee on the inside, one-eighteenth [1/18].
Through the middle of the calf, one-fifteenth [1/15].
Through the bottom of the calf on the outside, one-sixteenth [1/16].
On the inside, one-eighteenth [1/18].
At the bottom of the shin, one-twenty-eighth [1/28].
Across the instep of the foot, one-twenty-fourth [1/24].

### Following is the description of the arm for the oblique image

Make the arm one-fifteenth [1/15] wide across the shoulders.
Below the armpits, one-twentieth [1/20].
Across the joint of the elbow, one-twenty-sixth [1/26].
Above that, one-twenty-fifth [1/25].
Across the joint of the hand <note>The wrist.</note>, one-forty-eighth [1/48].

Finally, you shall widen the rear-facing figure between the armpits by one 6th [1/6]. The heel itself by one 35th [1/35]. But for the cleft of the buttocks <term>podex: an anatomical term for the anus or the cleft of the rear</term> it will be one 11th [1/11].
And with these spaces thus designated by points and numbers as shown, you shall express the form of the proposed image with a fitting drawing. We have placed an example of this matter below, marked with the letter D.

But if by chance it should please you to make the head of this figure a little larger and a little taller, you may take an eighth part of the total length from the crown—or as I should more correctly speak in this argument, from the forehead <term>sinciput: the upper/front part of the skull</term>—to the sole of the foot. And according to that, you may make the head from the chin upwards a little more prominent, with all other things remaining unchanged. In this way, it will happen that
<margin>Z</margin>
the place of the crown <term>vertex: the highest point of the head</term> is found at that height where the forehead of the previous head was. And it will be suitable in the profile [oblique] figure to describe the forehead above the eyebrows as slightly prominent. And in this way, the total length of this body will be almost eight times the head. Which is itself declared in the examples placed below.

To this male body, I shall add a suitable female one, whose height is also nine parts of the head. You shall measure it by parts in this way:

From the top of the <term>sinciput</term> <note>the crown or top of the head</note> down to the bottom of the <term>mentum</term> <note>the chin</note>, let the length be one ninth.
From the bottom of the <term>mentum</term> upward to the top of the <term>frons</term> <note>the forehead</note>, one tenth.
From this tenth, you shall seek those three equal spaces for the mouth or face, as has often been shown.
From the <term>sinciput</term> to the top of the <term>scapulae</term> <note>shoulder blades</note>, let the height be one fifteenth and one sixteenth.
From the same to the <term>iugula</term> <note>the hollow of the throat</note>, one sixth.
And from these to the <term>pectus</term> <note>the chest</note>, one thirtieth.
But as far as below the <term>alae</term> <note>the armpits, literally "wings"</note>, one sixteenth.
To the <term>mammae</term> <note>the breasts</note>, one eleventh.
Below the <term>mammae</term>, one ninth.
To the <term>lumbi</term> <note>the loins or waist</note> where they are girded, two elevenths.
From there indeed to the <term>umbilicus</term> <note>the navel</note>, one twenty-sixth.
To the top of the <term>femur</term> <note>thigh</note> or <term>coxa</term> <note>hip</note>, one tenth.
To the lower <term>alvus</term> <note>the belly or abdomen</note>, two seventeenths.
To the beginning of the <term>pubis</term>, one seventh.
To the bottom of the <term>pubis</term>, one twelfth and one thirteenth.
To the ends of the <term>nates</term> <note>the buttocks</note>, one eleventh and one twelfth.
From there indeed to the middle of the <term>femur</term>, one twelfth.
From the <term>planta</term> <note>the sole of the foot</note> to the <term>mons pedis</term> <note>the instep, literally "mountain of the foot"</note>, one forty-second.
And from there to the middle of the <term>genu</term> <note>the knee</note>, one fourth.
From the middle of the <term>genu</term> to above it, one twenty-sixth.
From the middle of the <term>genu</term> to below it, one thirtieth.
To the bottom of the outer <term>sura</term> <note>the calf</note>, one eleventh.
To the bottom of the inner <term>sura</term>, one ninth.
The length of the <term>calx</term> <note>the heel</note> itself shall be one seventh.
The length of the <term>brachium</term> <note>the arm</note> from the top of the <term>humerus</term> <note>the shoulder</note> to the joint of the <term>cubitus</term> <note>the elbow</note> shall be two elevenths.
From the tendon of the <term>cubitus</term> to the tips of the <term>digiti</term> <note>the fingers</note>, one fourth.
From here indeed back to the joint of the <term>manus</term> <note>the hand</note>, one eleventh.

Indeed, you shall broaden the image of the side profile <note>original: "obliquæ formæ"</note> through its members in this way:
The <term>caput</term> <note>head</note> across the <term>frons</term> shall have a width of one twelfth.

Across the <term>supercilia</term> <note>eyebrows</note>, one tenth.
Across the <term>nasus</term> <note>nose</note>, one eleventh.
The <term>gula</term> <note>throat</note> and <term>mentum</term>, one thirteenth.
The <term>collum</term> <note>neck</note> below the <term>mentum</term>, one twenty-first.
The top of the <term>scapulae</term>, one nineteenth.
The <term>iuguli</term> <note>the hollow of the throat</note>, one fourteenth.
The <term>pectus</term>, one tenth.
Near the <term>alae</term> <note>armpits</note>, let the space be two nineteenths.
Across the <term>papillae</term> <note>nipples</note>, one eighteenth and one nineteenth.
Below the <term>mammae</term>, two nineteenths.
Through the <term>lumbi</term> <note>loins</note> where they are girded, two twenty-firsts.
Across the <term>umbilicus</term>, one ninth.
Across the top of the <term>femur</term> or <term>coxa</term>, one seventh.
Through the lower <term>alvus</term> and the <term>nates</term>, one fourteenth and one sixteenth.
Across the <term>femur</term> below the <term>nates</term>, one tenth.
Across the middle of the <term>femur</term>, two twenty-firsts.
Above the <term>genu</term>, one fifteenth.
Across the middle of the <term>genu</term>, one eighteenth.
Below the <term>genu</term>, one nineteenth.
Across the middle of the <term>sura</term> <note>calf</note>, one sixteenth.
Across the lower inner <term>sura</term>, one nineteenth.
Across the lower <term>tibia</term> <note>shin</note>, one twenty-eighth.
Across the <term>mons pedis</term> and <term>talus</term> <note>the ankle</note>, one twenty-sixth.
To these you shall also add the widths of the arm in profile, and you shall make it across the <term>humerus</term> broad by one eighteenth.
Below the <term>alae</term>, one twentieth.
Through the joint of the <term>cubitus</term>, one thirty-ninth.
Below the same through the <term>cubitus</term>, one twenty-sixth.
Through the joint of the <term>manus</term>, one forty-ninth.
The <term>palma</term> <note>palm</note>, however, will occupy one fortieth.

But you shall make the front-facing image <note>original: "aduersam effigiem"</note> in this way:
The top of the <term>frons</term> broad by one eleventh.
The middle, one tenth.
Across the <term>supercilia</term>, one eleventh.
Across the <term>aures</term> <note>ears</note>, one tenth.
Across the <term>nasus</term>, one thirteenth.
Across the <term>collum</term> below the <term>mentum</term>, one twenty-first.
Across the top of the <term>scapulae</term>, one nineteenth.
Across the <term>iugula</term>, two thirteenths.

Below the armpits, let it be one twenty-sixth wide.

Through the wrist joint, one fortieth.
The palm <term>palma</term>, moreover, shall be one twenty-second wide.
For the rest, you shall make the back of the body <note>the "aversed" or rear view</note> one seventh wide between the armpits.
The cleft of the buttocks <term>podex</term> <note>original: "rimam podicis"</note>, one tenth.
The heel bone <term>calcaneus</term>, moreover, shall extend one thirty-seventh.
And so, with all the members thus noted, you shall describe a fitting form, just as we have often advised and as can be seen practiced by us in the subject example, which is indeed marked by the letters **D.f.** <note>likely "Die Figur" or a reference to a specific diagram in Dürer's work</note> Now, if it pleases you to place a somewhat larger head upon a body of this kind, follow the method demonstrated in the figure immediately preceding this one.

The loins <term>lumbi</term> where they are girded <note>the narrowest part of the waist where a belt or girdle would be fastened</note>
The navel <term>umbilicus</term>

The top of the thigh <term>sumum femur</term> or hip <term>coxa</term>
The lowest part of the abdomen <term>ima alvus</term>

The lowest part of the pubic area <term>ima pubes</term>

The middle of the thigh <term>medium femur</term>

Above the knee <term>supra genu</term>

Below the knee <term>infra genu</term>

The lowest part of the inner calf <term>imum sure interius</term>

The bottom of the ankle <term>imum tali</term>
The sole <term>planta</term>

<summary>This page lists the essential anatomical reference points used by Dürer to calculate the proportions of the human figure, organized vertically from the head to the feet.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, human proportion, Albrecht Dürer, facial features, leg anatomy, torso measurements</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s geometric investigation into human proportions, shifting from general principles to the specific measurements for a "slender and taller" male figure. It follows the previous page’s anatomical glossary by applying those terms to a rigorous mathematical scale where the head serves as one-tenth of the total body height.</meta>

I will now propose one more slender figure of taller stature, for which the head shall be <term>one-tenth</term> <note>original Latin: "vna 10"</note> of its total length. 
I will first teach how to create the male body; its height shall be as follows:

<margin>10</margin> From the <term>crown</term> <note>Latin: "sinciput"</note> of the head to the bottom of the chin, let there be one-tenth.
<margin>11</margin> From there, moving upwards to the very top of the forehead, one-eleventh. 
In this space, the form of the mouth or face shall be inscribed.
<margin>17</margin> From the crown to the top of the <term>shoulder blades</term> <note>Latin: "scapulas"</note>, two-seventeenths.
<margin>13 14</margin> To the <term>collarbones</term> <note>Latin: "iugula"</note>, one-thirteenth and one-fourteenth.
<margin>6</margin> To the shoulders, one-sixth.
<margin>25</margin> From the shoulders to the top of the chest, one-twenty-fifth.
<margin>17</margin> To the armpits, one-seventeenth.
<margin>13</margin> To the nipples, one-thirteenth.
<margin>21</margin> Below the nipples, two-twenty-firsts.
<margin>13</margin> To the loins where we are girded <note>the waist</note>, two-thirteenths.
<margin>30</margin> From here to the navel, one-thirtieth.
<margin>21</margin> To the hollow of the hips, one-twenty-first.
<margin>8</margin> To the top of the thigh or hip, one-eighth.
<margin>14 15</margin> To the <term>private parts</term> <note>Latin: "pudenda"</note>, one-fourteenth and one-fifteenth.
<margin>13</margin> To the end of the scrotum, two-thirteenths.
<margin>11</margin> To the bottom of the buttocks, two-elevenths.
<margin>11</margin> From here to the groove of the thigh, one-eleventh.
Between the bottom of the buttocks and the sole of the foot, you shall place the middle of the knee.
<margin>35</margin> From the sole upward to the bottom of the ankle, let there be one-thirty-fifth.
<margin>26</margin> From there to the <term>instep</term> <note>Latin: "montem pedis," literally "mountain of the foot"</note>, one-twenty-sixth.
<margin>30</margin> From the middle of the knee to above the knee, one-thirtieth.
<margin>40</margin> And below the knee, one-fortieth.
Likewise, from the middle of the knee to the bottom of the calf:
<margin>10</margin> on the outside, one-tenth.
<margin>9</margin> on the inside, one-ninth.
<margin>7</margin> The length of the heel itself shall be one-seventh.

### Next, you shall measure the lengths of the arm in this way

<margin>11</margin> From the joint of the shoulder to the joint of the elbow, you shall make two-elevenths.
You will mark the length from the joint in two ways. Either you shall make the whole length from the joint to the tips of the fingers one-fourth and one [part] of the whole, and from the tips of the fingers back to the joint of the hand <note>the wrist</note>, you shall draw one-eleventh. Or, from the joint to the fingertips, you shall draw three-elevenths, and from the fingertips back to the joint of the hand, two-twenty-firsts.

---

After this, you shall also mark the widths of the <term>profile figure</term> <note>Latin: "effigiei obliquæ"</note> according to the points of height in this manner:
The width through the forehead shall be one-thirteenth.
Through the eyebrows, one-eleventh.
Through the nose and temples, one-eleventh.
Through the cheek and the back of the neck, one-thirteenth.
Through the chin and the throat, one-fourteenth.
Through the neck below the chin, one-twenty-second.
Through the tops of the shoulder blades, one-twentieth.
Through the collarbones, one-thirteenth.
Through the tops of the shoulders, one-eleventh.
Through the top of the chest, two-seventeenths.
Next to the armpits, one-eighth.
Through the nipples, likewise one-eighth.
Below the nipples, two-seventeenths.
Through the loins where we are girded, one-eleventh.
Through the navel, likewise one-eleventh.
Through the hollow of the hips, one-tenth.
Through the top of the thigh, one-seventeenth and one-eighteenth.
Through the private parts, one-ninth.
The thigh itself below the buttocks thickens to one-eleventh.
The middle of the thigh—that is, the groove—to one-twelfth.
Above the knee, let the width be one-seventeenth.
Through the middle of the knee, one-nineteenth.
Below the knee, one-twentieth.
Through the middle of the calf, one-thirty-second and one-thirty-fourth.
Through the bottom of the outer calf, one-seventeenth.
Through the bottom of the inner calf, one-nineteenth.
Above the instep, one-thirty-second.
Through the top of the instep, one-twenty-ninth.
Through the middle of the same—that is, below the ankle—one-twenty-third.

### The arm in profile shall have this measurement

Through the shoulder, one-seventeenth.
Below the armpits, one-twenty-first.
Through the joint of the elbow, one-thirtieth.
Above that, one-twenty-eighth.
Through the joint of the hand <note>the wrist</note>, one-fiftieth.

<summary>This page details the specific mathematical proportions for a tall, slender male figure, providing a vertical and horizontal (profile) inventory of measurements from the crown of the head to the feet. These ratios are defined as fractions of the total body height, establishing a canon for Renaissance artistic anatomy.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, slender male figure, anatomical measurements, Renaissance art theory, anthropometry, profile view proportions</keywords>

Furthermore, if by chance it pleases you to make a slightly larger head for this body, you shall take one-ninth part <note>rather than the standard one-tenth used for this specific figure</note> and make its lower point be the chin and the upper point be the <term>crown</term> <note>Latin: "sinciput"</note>. In this way, the height of the head will increase somewhat. It will also follow, as is right, that a slightly larger width will result. The rest of the features, however, will remain in the demonstrated form. We have expressed this very thing in our examples. But then the head will no longer be one-tenth of the stature of this body, but nearly one-ninth.

->Let the mark of this image be **E**.<-

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the proportions of the slender male figure "E" to the corresponding measurements for the female body. It maintains the same fractional system where the total body height is the unit, divided into parts (1/10th, 1/12th, etc.) to establish an ideal canon of beauty.</meta>

->Consequently, I will explain the measurement of the female body corresponding to the previously described<-
->male figure. Its height, therefore, shall be as follows.<-

From the crown of the head <note>original Latin: "sinciput"</note> where the hair parts to the bottom of the chin: one 10th.
From here to the top of the forehead: one 12th.
And within this 12th, the face or the arrangement of the whole mouth is distributed, as has often been shown.
From the crown to that fold <note>original Latin: "palear," literally a dewlap, referring to the fleshy part under the chin</note> beneath the chin: one 18th and one 19th.
To the top of the shoulder blades: one 8th.
To the collarbones <term>jugula</term>: two 13ths.
To the shoulders: one 6th.
At the shoulders to the top of the chest: one 22nd.
Below the armpits: one 16th.
To the nipples: one 11th.
Below the breasts: one 9th.
To the loins where they are girded <note>the natural waistline</note>: two 11ths.
From here to the navel: one 40th.
To the top of the thigh: one 10th.
To the bottom of the belly: let two 17ths be drawn.
To the beginning of the pubes: one 13th and one 14th.
To the bottom of the pubes: one 12th and one 13th.
To the furthest part of the buttocks: one 11th and one 12th.
From here to the middle of the thigh: one 12th.
From the sole of the foot upwards to the middle of the knee: let there be two 7ths.
To the bottom of the ankle: one 38th.
From the middle of the knee to the instep of the foot <note>the "mountain" of the foot</note>: one 4th.
To the bottom of the calf on the inner side: one 9th.
On the outer side: one 10th.
Below the knee: one 30th.
Likewise, from the middle of the knee to above it: one 30th.
The length of the heel: one 14th and one 15th.
The length of the arm shall be this:
From the shoulder to the bend of the elbow: let two 11ths be placed.
From the bend of the elbow to the tips of the fingers: one 4th.
From here back to the wrist joint: one 11th.

And thus, having arranged the height of the limbs and marked them each in their places, you shall also mark the spaces for the widths. And first, the widths of the profile image <note>original Latin: "obliquæ," referring to the side-view or three-quarter view diagram</note>.

Through the forehead, therefore, let the width be: one 13th.
Through the eyebrows: one 11th.
Through the nose and eyebrows: one 12th.
Through the throat and chin: one 14th.
You shall make the neck under the chin one 24th wide.
Through the top of the shoulder blades: it shall be one 22nd.
Through the collarbones: one 17th.
Through the top of the shoulders: one 13th.
Through the chest: one 10th.
Through the nipples: one 19th and one 21st.
Below the breasts: one 20th and one 21st.
Through the loins where they are girded: one 11th.
Through the navel: one 10th.
Through the top of the thigh or hip: one 14th and one 15th.
Through the bottom of the belly: one 15th and one 16th.
Through the top of the pubes and the buttocks: one 16th and one 17th.
The thigh below the buttocks: one 10th.
And in the same place lower down, through the groove: two 23rds.
Above the knee: one 17th.
Through the middle of the knee: one 19th.
Below the knee: one 20th.
Through the middle of the calf: one 17th.
Through the bottom of the calf on the outside: one 18th.
Through the bottom of the calf on the inside: one 19th.
Through the bottom of the shin: one 32nd.
Through the curve of the foot and the ankles: one 27th.
But the arm shall be as follows: Through the shoulder, one 19th in width.
Under the armpits: one 23rd.
Through the bend of the elbow: one 34th.
Above it: one 30th.
Through the wrist joint: one 60th.
The palm indeed shall be widened to one 45th.

The widths of the front-facing image follow.
Through the forehead, let it be: one 13th.
Through the top of the temples: one 13th.
Through the eyebrows: one 13th.
Through the ears: one 12th.
Through the nose: one 16th.
Let the neck under the chin be one 25th wide.

<meta>This page continues the specific anatomical measurements for the slender female figure (identified as figure 'E'). The previous page detailed the vertical heights; this page focuses on the widths (latitudes) across various parts of the body, maintaining the fractional system where each number represents a part of the total height (e.g., "one 22nd" is 1/22 of the total height).</meta>

<sig>E iii</sig>

Across the tops of the shoulder blades <term>scapulas</term>, let the width be one 22nd.
Across the collarbones <term>iugula</term>, two 17ths.
Across the tops of the shoulders <term>humeros</term>, one 7th. And in that same place, the bones of the upper arm <note>the humerus</note> are positioned at a distance of two 15ths from each other.
But across the chest <term>pectus</term> and shoulders, let two 11ths be drawn.
Between the armpits <term>alas</term>, one 9th.
Between the nipples <term>papillas</term>, one 12th.
Below the breasts <term>mamas</term>, one 8th.
Through the loins <term>lumbos</term> where they are girded <note>the narrowest part of the waist</note>, likewise one 8th.
Through the navel <term>vmbilicum</term>, two 13ths.
Through the top of the thigh <term>femur</term>, two 11ths. And in that same place, the hip bones <term>Coxendices</term> shall be at a distance of one 8th from each other.
Across the lowest part of the belly, there shall be two 11ths.
Through the thigh below the buttocks <term>natibus</term>, one 12th.
Through the groove of the thigh, two 27ths.
Above the knee <term>genu</term>, one 19th.
Through the middle of the knee, one 22nd.
Below the knee, the same amount.
Through the middle of the calf <term>suram</term>, one 19th.
Through the lowest part of the calf on the outside, one 21st.
On the inside, one 23rd.
Through the bottom of the shin <term>tibiam</term>, one 48th.

---

Through the bridge <note>literally "the mountain"</note> of the foot and the ankles <term>talos</term>, one 43rd.
Further down, however, one 46th.
The width of the heel <term>calcis</term> itself through the outer toes <note>referring to the outermost edges of the foot's width</note> shall be one 22nd.
The arm <term>brachium</term> belonging to this figure shall be one 28th wide below the armpits.
Above the joint of the elbow <term>cubiti</term>, one 34th.
Below this, one 26th.
Through the joint of the hand <note>the wrist</note>, one 46th.
The palm <term>palma</term>, however, shall be drawn at one 24th.
The width of the figure from the back <note>the "averse" view</note> between the armpits shall be two 15ths.
The cleft of the buttocks <term>podicis</term>, one 10th.
The heel bone <term>calcaneus</term>, one 40th.

---

Then indeed, as has often been said, you shall draw a form according to these notations that is suitable for each view: the profile <note>oblique</note>, the front <note>adverse</note>, and the back <note>averse</note>. But if you wish to make the head of this body somewhat larger, you shall do so as we taught for the male body, and as our example which we have placed below indicates. And let the mark of this figure be E.j.

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drawing the human figure, shifting from the specific anatomical landmarks of the female form to the general principles of movement, sexual dimorphism, and the precise geometric construction of the male head.</meta>

Having depicted the images in the manner prescribed above—concerning the spine <note>original: "spina"</note> and the joints of the other members—it is necessary to explain how they should be bent. Although it would have been ideal for readers to be familiar with this already, we have marked the diagonal images <note>Dürer is referring to "oblique" or three-quarter views</note> with small triangular signs along the length and curve of the spine to make our instructions easier to understand. These marks are always placed in the rear fourth of the broad body. Similarly, in the front-facing images, you will see certain points marked with circles on the joints.

Furthermore, although we have measured all the images above as being of equal length according to a single rule to simplify the task, you must keep in mind that in any actual composition, the stature of slender figures should be drawn taller <note>Dürer suggests that "graceful" or thin figures should be proportionately longer to maintain their aesthetic "slenderness" compared to stockier figures.</note>.

Now, if by chance images of a man and a woman are to be established of a single or appropriately matched scale, the line measuring the woman's height should be shorter than the man's by one part in eighteen. If this is neglected, the woman would appear larger than the man, because female bodies must be drawn a bit more fleshy and full-bodied <note>original: "carnosiuscula et habitiora"</note> than those of men. Men, however, are to be drawn more compact, solid, and less fleshy.

We have prescribed this extensive system for the depicted images—from the largest to the smallest—so that every artist has the faculty to treat any intended work with abundance and variety as they please. We shall discuss this matter more wordily in the pages that follow.

As has been understood, we have designated four distinct types of human bodies. Now, however, we shall investigate the composition and features <note>original: "lineamenta"</note> of a single part—the head—and first, specifically, the male head, with greater diligence and exactness. We will follow the previous system, though I will apply this description to a longer scale <note>or "measuring rod"</note> than I used in the earlier images, because the smaller parts must be indicated; the brevity of the previous scale would have caused those details to be obscure.

To begin the diagonal view <note>original: "obliqua figura," referring to a profile or three-quarter view</note> of the male head, you must describe a square <note>original: "quadratum"</note> that is equal on all sides, based on one-eighth of the total body length. Once this is established, the side facing the nose shall be **a**, the back shall be **b**, the top **c**, and the bottom **d**. Having done this, you will measure all the individual parts of the head by their length and describe them with parallel transverse lines. You will assign each line its own letter, so that you may easily collect the measurements of every part of the head, just as we have placed before your eyes in the accompanying examples. The method is as follows:

Draw a transverse line **e** at a distance of one-tenth from side **d**, as was shown previously in the tall male figure of eight head-lengths. That line will divide the head and the forehead. However, you should make the crown <note>original: "verticem"</note> higher by one-third between **c** and **e**. Next, divide the space between **c** and **d** with two lines, **f** and **g**, into three equal spaces. The upper line, **f**, will touch the eyebrows and the tops of the ears. The lower line, **g**, will touch the bottom of the nose and the bottom of the ears. At that same point, the back of the head <note>original: "occiput"</note> sinks into the neck.

Next, divide the space **e**-**f** into three equal spaces using two points, and at the lowest point, draw a transverse line **h**. This line will touch the beginning of the forehead where it hangs over the eyebrows, from which the curve of the forehead will round out toward the root of the hair. Then, divide **f**-**g** into three equal spaces with two points, the highest of which you will mark with a transverse line **i**; in this way, the eye is enclosed within **f**-**i**, and both corners of the eye—the inner and the outer—will extend to the middle of **f**-**i**.

Likewise, divide **f**-**g** into four equal spaces with three points, the lowest of which you will mark with a transverse line **k**. This will touch the upper prominence of the nostrils. The bottom of the ear is also contained within **f**-**g**. Next, divide **g**-**d** with a transverse line **l** into two equal spaces; this line will touch the top of the chin, just as **d** touches the bottom. Then, divide **g**-**l** with a line **m** through the middle, which will pass through the middle of the cheek. When you have divided **g**-**m** into three...

<meta>catchword: three</meta>

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric principles for bending limbs and the proportional differences between male and female figures, before beginning a technical, step-by-step guide to constructing the male head using a lettered grid system.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, male head, female proportions, geometry, artistic anatomy, facial measurements, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical instructions on human proportion. While the previous page established the differences between male and female statures, this section moves into the specific geometric "lattice" or grid system used to construct the human head in profile (oblique view).</meta>

equal spaces; the lowest will be occupied by the upper lip, while the remaining two will be assigned to that part seen under the nose, resembling small channels <note>original: "quasi cannulæ," referring to the philtrum</note>. When, however, you have divided the section **l.m.** into two equal spaces, the one above will be occupied by the lower lip, and the other by that valley-like depression between the lip and the chin, which they call the <term>nympham</term> <note>Dürer uses this term to refer to the philtrum or the groove of the chin; in modern anatomy, this is the mentalis depression</note>. And so you will have the principal parts of the head marked by transverse lines according to their height, as they are seen above and below. It follows that I should show the width or thickness—or, as our people say, the depth of the head—through its various parts. This we shall indeed do by means of perpendicular lines. By "depth," you should understand whatever is drawn back from the front part where the nose is located.

You shall divide the two sides **a.b.** with perpendicular lines **n.o.p.q.r.s.** into seven equal spaces. Line **n** will touch the pupil of the eyes and the corners of the mouth <note>original: "buccæ angulos"</note>. But the interior corner of the eyes—as we are now speaking, that is, the one closest to the temples—you will terminate in the middle of **n.o.** These measurements shall be taken on both sides, depending on whether the face looks this way or that.

After this, you shall draw a <term>diagonion</term> <note>a diagonal line</note>, that is, a slanting line, from where the transverse line **c** and the perpendicular **n** form an angle, to the angle made by **a.f.**, and you will mark it with the letter **t**. Afterward, you shall draw another oblique line from the same angle **c.n.** to the angle made by **a.g.**, and you will add the mark **v**. The forehead shall be curved along the line **n.t.**, while you will shape the nose along the line **n.v.** Now, where the transverse line **e** intersects the oblique line **n.t.**, you will describe the root of the hair above the forehead. Next, draw one perpendicular line **x** between the perpendiculars **a.n.** and the transverse lines **d.g.**; this line will touch the extremities of the upper lip and the chin. Then, divide **n.y.** between the transverse lines **m.d.** with two perpendicular lines into three equal spaces. The first of these lines will touch the edge of the lower lip; the second will touch that "valley" I mentioned above under this lip. You shall draw the <term>Supercilia</term> <note>eyebrows</note> almost to **o**, so that the middle parts swell out a little beyond the transverse line **f**. Within the perpendiculars **q.r.** and the transverse lines **f.g.**, you will encompass the entire ear. However, you will place the lowest part of the ear in the first half of the space between **q.r** and **f.g.** You shall place the crown of the head within **s.b.** and **c.e.**, specifically near the third part of this space closest to **s**, and likewise the third part near the transverse line **e**. On the transverse line **g**, within **s.b.** in the fourth part of this space near **s**, you will set the head upon the neck. Once these are thus arranged, you will describe the outlines of the oblique head through these "lattices" of lines, so that the top of the head touches **c** between **p.q.** and the back of it touches **b** between **h.f.**; thus it is drawn around within this quadrilateral all the way to the neck, which then reaches the lower side.

Afterward, you shall draw the <term>sinciput</term> <note>the front/top part of the skull</note> from the summit to the forehead, and consequently, you will shape the forehead, the nose, and the cheek. The tip of the nose will touch **a** between **f.g.** In this way, the whole head, enclosed in that square, will touch each of its sides. Furthermore, you shall draw the <term>maxillam</term> <note>the jawbone</note> upward and backward behind the ear. And you will express the ears and eyelids in their proper places. You shall draw the whole circuit of the forehead beneath the roots of the hair and the sinciput through **e** within **p.q.**, moving inward to the middle of the ear at **o**. Then describe the throat downward from line **d**, within **o.p.**, beginning at the fourth part of the space nearest to **o**. As for the neck, if you like, you can make it either one-fourteenth or two-twenty-sevenths wide. You who handle these matters, observe the drawing of the outlines with great diligence; for he who neglects this will quickly spoil the work with some deformity, even if the junctions and lattices of the lines demonstrated above do not allow one to wander far astray.

This path for finding the form of the oblique head having been completed, it follows that we should demonstrate the front-facing <note>original: "aduersæ," referring to the en face view</note> head in the same way, establishing it within a rectangular quadrilateral. This rectangle will be longer on one side—equal in height, indeed, but less wide.

You shall produce the transverse lines in order through the proposed quadrilateral from **c** to **d**, following the same system you used for the oblique face, according to the arranged letter marks.

<summary>This page provides a highly technical, geometric guide for drawing a human head in profile using a coordinate grid ("lattices"). It details the precise placement of facial features like the philtrum, eyebrows, and jawline based on intersecting horizontal, vertical, and diagonal lines.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, head construction, geometry in art, philtrum, sinciput, maxilla, anthropometry, Renaissance drawing techniques</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical instructions on drawing the human head. While the previous section focused on the side profile and its vertical divisions, this page provides the geometric grid for the front view (face) and the rear view (back of the head), followed by an introduction to a specialized drafting tool called the "transfer triangle."</meta>

Regarding these lines, you will observe the highest and lowest parts of the face. Afterward, you shall cut across them with perpendicular lines to show the width.

You will add two perpendicular lines **a.b.** on either side of the proposed transversals, spaced 10 parts apart from each other. Then, divide this space equally with four other perpendicular lines **c.d.e.f.** In the middle, at **d.e.**, you will establish the <term>sinciput</term> <note>the top of the head or the forehead area</note> between the transversals **c** and **e**. Next, you will draw the nose so that the prominence of the nostrils touches **d.e.** on both sides, between transversals **f** and **g**. You will bring the corners of the mouth <note>original: "buccae," which can mean cheeks, but here refers to the lateral limits of the mouth or jaw</note> through the transversal **m** to **d.e.**, and between these and the chin, you will form the face. You shall place the eyes so that their four corners reach **c.d.e.f.** within the transversals **f** and **g**.

After this, divide the perpendiculars **a.c.** and **f.b.** with two other perpendiculars **g.h.** through the middle. These two lines will touch the top of the cheeks on both sides through transversal **f**. Moreover, the eyebrows will end on both sides between **g.c.** and **f.h.** below transversal **f**. Furthermore, divide **a.g.** and **h.b.** through the middle with two more perpendicular lines **i.k.** With these, you will separate the open expanse of the forehead from the hairline on both sides between transversals **e** and **f**. Furthermore, the temples will transcend these lines on both sides so that half of the ears are covered and hidden from view within transversals **f** and **g**. You will shape both ears between **a.i.** and **k.b.** and transversals **f** and **g**, so that they exceed the perpendiculars **a.b.** above and extend toward the perpendiculars **i.k.** below.

When all these are finished, you shall add the outlines so that where the top of the head touches transversal **c** within the perpendiculars **d.e.**, it is rounded from there on both sides toward **a.b.** and transversal **h**, and from there to the ears near transversal **f** between the middle lines **a.i.** and **k.b.** Next, you shall draw the open forehead and the jaws through the perpendiculars **g.h.** and transversal **m**, and conclude at the chin. Likewise, you shall draw the neck from transversal **m** to the lowest transversal **d**, and make it 15 parts wide, or a little more. Soon, you will mark the face with the appropriate expressions of the eyes, nose, cheeks, chin, and other parts.

Finally, you will come to the third rectangle, and in it, you will describe the back of the head. You will express this according to the description and the circuit of lines of the front-facing head; you shall establish the crown of the head in its place, and wherever the head is rounded below, you shall join it fitly and neatly to the neck.

Now, if you are to project a head fashioned by the method we have prescribed so that the circumference, boundaries, and surfaces of all its parts appear through the transversal lines—as if the head I have shown in the drawing were perhaps made of wax and cut through any of the indicated transversal lines—the surface of this cut would then be known. Indeed, painters today call this by their usual term **"pressing into the foundation"** <note>original: "in fundamentum deprimere," a term for creating a ground plan or orthographic projection</note>. To attempt this, you will provide yourself with a triangle as I shall teach you. By means of this triangle—since that which is proposed can be transferred or changed into another while maintaining the accuracy of the proportion—I have chosen to call it the **transferrer**. You shall prepare it thus: you will mark a triangle in which one angle is a right angle, with these numbers **1 2 3**. One angle will be entirely right, as I said, so that it can be used in any direction, whether it contains equal or unequal, long or short sides. Any side of this triangle—for it does not matter which—you will mark with points, as for example, for the present, **4 5 6**. From these, you will draw parallel lines to the other side of the triangle, by which that side is similarly divided. From the new points of this division, you will draw other parallel lines to the remaining undivided side, whereby its division will also be accomplished.

<summary>This page details the grid-based construction of the human head from the front and back views and introduces the "transfer triangle," a geometric tool used to project and scale proportions.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, face geometry, orthographic projection, transfer triangle, sinciput, drawing techniques, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical drafting instructions for human proportions, likely by Albrecht Dürer. The previous page detailed the construction of the front-facing head (the "adverse" view) using a grid. This page introduces a geometric tool—the "transfer diagram"—to transform these proportions into a foreshortened or "depressed" view, allowing the artist to depict the head as if looking downward.</meta>

From this diagram, you will be able to derive the distinctions of many things; we have set it out in a drawing below before we proceed to teach how it can be adapted to the <term>depression of the head</term> <note>original: "capitis depressionem"; this refers to foreshortening or the flattening effect when a head is tilted downward</note> of which we spoke.

---

-> 1 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 4 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 5 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 6 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 2 <-
-> 3 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; <-
-> **The Transfer Diagram.** <note>original: "Schema transferentis"</note> <-

---

For that "depression," as we call it, you shall adapt this "transferrer" in this manner. You will set forth the forms of the head—both the profile <note>original: "obliqui"</note> and the front view <note>original: "aduersi"</note>—constructed in the manner described above within their quadrilaterals. Then, from the square of the profile head, you will draw all the perpendicular lines **a.n.o.p.q.r.s.** as far down as necessity requires. Similarly, from the quadrilateral of the front-facing head, you will draw all the perpendiculars **a.i.g.c.d.e.f.h.k.b.** Afterward, you shall draw a straight transverse line evenly across all the lines extended in this manner below the forms of the mentioned heads.

Having done this, you will mark where this transverse line intersects the perpendiculars **a.b.** of the front-facing head; next to line **a** you will write **1**, and next to **b**, **2**. Then, from mark **1** downwards, you will measure a space equal to the width of the front-facing head, and at the end of that space you will mark **3**. You will then join **2** and **3** with a slanted line—that is, the <term>hypotenuse</term> <note>the longest side of a right-angled triangle</note>—and you will have completed the triangle which I have named the "transferrer."

After this, you will notice where the slanted line **2.3** intersects the perpendiculars drawn from the front-facing head, and from those points of intersection, you will draw parallel transverse lines through the perpendiculars that were drawn from the profile head. And where the transverse line **2.1** and line **3** cross the perpendiculars of the two sides of the profile head **a.b.**, you will mark them **4.5** in the first place and **6.7** in the second. This will be the oblong quadrilateral in which the "depression" of the head, as I mentioned, will be contained.

<summary>This page explains how to use a geometric "transfer triangle" to project a standard profile and front view of a head into a foreshortened perspective. By drawing perpendicular lines through a diagonal hypotenuse, the artist creates a new grid for a tilted or "depressed" view of the head.</summary>
<keywords>transfer diagram, foreshortening, head proportions, geometry in art, Albrecht Dürer, perspective, drafting techniques</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Albrecht Dürer’s geometric method of head construction. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how to draw perpendicular lines from a profile view to a transfer diagram; this page explains how that grid determines the placement of facial features and discusses alternative proportions, such as the seven-head-height canon.</meta>

Once this is completed and marked with the numbers **4, 5, 6, and 7**, with the help of the prepared triangle and by drawing lines for both the front-facing and profile views of the head, the quadrangles <note>original: "quadrangulis"; the individual cells within the grid system</note> contained therein will indicate where the intersections of all parts of the head fall. For each of those smaller quadrangles will hold the specific locations of their parts, such as the eyes, nose, cheeks, chin, ears, neck—and to put it simply—the features or <term>lineaments</term> <note>original: "lineamentorum"; the distinctive shapes or outlines of the face</note> of the whole head. We have also included examples of all these: the head in profile, front-facing, rear-facing, and "depressed" <note>original: "depressi"; seen from above or tilted downward toward the viewer, creating foreshortening</note>, as we said we would. It is very useful to have these examined and to understand the flat surfaces of the parts in the manner I have described.

However, if anyone should prefer to draw the head so that it is one-seventh of the entire body's length—whereas in this illustration we have made it one-eighth—he should maintain all other proportions while first extending and widening the profile head to that measurement. He shall make the neck two parts of 27 wide <note>Dürer uses a complex fractional scale; "27" refers to a specific unit of measurement in his "Meßstab" or ruler system</note>. Similarly, the front-facing head shall be appropriately expanded so that the space across the forehead is about two parts of 17, but it will contract again near the ears, which themselves will now project more. Likewise, the roots of the hair and the broad field of the forehead will open up further; all of these things may be observed by those studious in these matters in the examples provided below. Truly, it must not be ignored that a refined <term>commensuration</term> <note>original: "commensurationem"; the proportional relationship between parts of a whole</note> where the head is one-seventh of the total length is by no means to be looked down upon.

---

By the same method that I have taught how the head ought to be "depressed" <note>tilted or foreshortened</note> toward the base, you will be able to depress the entire image, whether it is standing straight or in some other gesture.

<summary>This page explains how a grid system (quadrangles) defines facial features and provides instructions for adjusting head proportions from a 1:8 ratio to a 1:7 ratio. It concludes by noting that these foreshortening techniques for the head can be applied to the entire human body.</summary>
<keywords>Dürer, proportion, head construction, foreshortening, grid system, lineaments, commensuration, seven-head canon</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the geometric construction of the head to the precise measurement and proportions of the hand, specifically the "strong man" canon used in Dürer's anatomical studies.</meta>

<sig>F</sig>

Now I shall also describe the palm of a strong man more precisely through all its small parts, and I shall do so just as I set out above. Draw a line **10** units long. Then draw two transverse lines, **c** and **d**, and separate them by a perpendicular line whose height is **10** units, marked at **a** and **b**. Likewise, draw another perpendicular line, **e** and **f**, at a moderate distance from the first. Then, you will divide **a–b** with small points or marks into **18** equal spaces (totaling **19** marks). However, **e–f** shall be placed so that it passes through the middle of the palm, extending from the tip of the middle finger where it touches **c**, down to line **d**, which splits the joint of the hand with the forearm—that is, the <term>carpus</term> <note>original: "carpon"; the wrist bones</note>. Next, from point **1**, draw a transverse line **g**; this will touch the tip of the finger closest to the smallest one, which the Greeks call the <term>paramesos</term> <note>original: "parameson"; the ring finger</note>. Next, mark a point midway between **1** and **2**, from which you will draw a transverse line **h**; this will touch the tip of the index finger. Next, from point **4**, draw a transverse line **i**; this will touch the tip of the little finger. Finally, draw a transverse line **k** from point **7**; this will touch the tip of the thumb.

After you have marked the tips of the fingers, you shall also establish their lowest parts—the "roots" from which they grow out of the hand—in this manner: From point **11**, draw a transverse line **l**; this will pass through the lowest joints of the index and middle fingers, whose position in the hand is seen to be level. In this way, the middle finger becomes longer than the entire remaining lower part of the hand. Where line **l** intersects the perpendicular **e–f**, mark that spot **m**. Place one foot of a compass on point **f** and the other on **m**. From there, move the compass in a straight line across the hand from the middle finger toward the little finger as far as needed. At the curve of this line, mark the lowest joints of the little finger and the one next to it (the ring finger). For the position of these is lower than the previous ones. After this, draw a transverse line **n** from mark **14**, which will run through the lower joint of the thumb.

Now that the heights of the hand and fingers have been measured, the next step is the arrangement of the positions of all the joints in this manner:
From mark **10**, draw a transverse line **o**, which will run through the upper joint of the thumb. Next, divide the index finger in the middle with a transverse line between **h** and **l**; this will pass through its middle joint. From this point to the top of the finger, divide the space into **7** equal parts using **6** points. Then, from point **3**, draw a transverse line; this will pass through the highest joint <note>original: "summum articulum"; the joint nearest the fingernail</note> of the index finger. A transverse line **p** drawn from point **6** will pass through the middle joint of the middle finger. Likewise, divide the space between the transverse lines **c** and **p** into **7** equal parts with **6** points, and draw a transverse line from point **3**; it will pass through the highest joint of the middle finger. The transverse line **k** will be seen to pass through the middle joint of the ring finger. Then, divide the space above this finger into **9** equal spaces with **8** points, and from point **4**, draw a transverse line; it will pass through the highest joint of that same finger.

For the length of the little finger, you may either leave it as it fell according to the previous calculation, or you may make it equal to the distance from the middle joint of the middle finger to its base. Divide this length into **11** equal spaces with **10** points, and from point **6**, draw a transverse line; it will pass through the middle joint of the little finger. Then, divide the space of the top **6** points of this length again into **11** equal spaces with **10** points. From point **6**, draw a transverse line; it will pass through the highest joint of the little finger. Meanwhile, take care to notice which numbers and measurements are taken from the perpendicular **a–b**, and which are taken from the other smaller spaces.

You shall make the height of the nails of each finger equal to half of the space that lies between the tip of the finger and the first (highest) joint.

Furthermore, it should be noted that on both sides of the middle finger, the "cleft" <note>the webbing or separation between fingers</note> of the fingers is found at the middle length of the hand; but the cleft between the little finger and the one next to it is somewhat lower, and the cleft of the thumb is lower still.

<summary>This page provides a technical, geometric guide for drawing the human hand based on a grid system, specifying the exact placement of fingertips, joints, and the wrist using a series of coordinate lines and points.</summary>
<keywords>hand proportions, anatomy, geometry, finger joints, carpus, ring finger, thumb, measurement, Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drawing the human hand, moving from the vertical measurements (length) established on the previous page to the horizontal measurements (width) and the depth (thickness) of the hand in profile.</meta>

When you have truly established the height of the hand and its parts, you shall next seek out its width. However, you will not be able to maintain the method I proposed earlier for the painting of images—namely, that the appearance of the oblique <note>The side-view or profile view.</note> figure should be investigated first—while preserving the specific properties of the subject in this instance. Therefore, you will first arrange the widths of the flat, open palm (which is not in an oblique position) according to its parts in this manner. At the joint where the palm meets the arm, you shall make it seven-nineteenths [7/19] wide along the transverse line **d**, relative to the length within the space **a–b**. Indeed, along the transverse line **m**, you shall make the palm eleven-nineteenths [11/19] of such parts; for it must be widest at the joint of the thumb. Furthermore, that width of the hand which extends from the edge of the index finger along the transverse line **l** and through the lowest joints of the fingers—towards the fragment of the circle <note>Refers to the curved line or arc where the fingers meet the palm.</note> mentioned above near the root of the little finger—you shall measure as eleven-nineteenths [11/19] of the length of the index finger. You shall make the <term>middle finger</term> <note>original: "Medium digitum"</note>, which is the longest, one-fifth [1/5] as wide at its lowest part as its own length. At its highest part, however, you shall make that width narrower by subtracting one-fourth [1/4] from it. You shall make the base of the <term>index finger</term> <note>original: "Indicem"</note> as wide as you made the middle finger, but the tip slightly more contracted. You shall make the <term>little finger</term> <note>original: "Digitum minimum"</note> and the one next to it each one-fifth [1/5] as wide at their bases as their own length, but from this width you shall subtract one-fourth [1/4] at the tips; you shall widen the middle of the <term>thumb</term> <note>original: "pollicem"</note> by one-third [1/3] of its length.

The lowest parts of the fingers are so set into the hand that the roots of each penetrate into the middle of their <term>width</term> <note>original: "latitudinem"</note>, which we have expressed in the examples by marking them with these small circles.

It must be considered here that no finger in the hand is found to be exactly similar and conforming to another, which will be easily apparent to anyone who investigates. It should also be noted that there is no straight extension of the whole palm, but rather it seems to wander outward, as it were, near the little finger.

Once these things are finished, you shall also seek out the width or <term>thickness</term> <note>original: "densitatem"</note> of the oblique hand. Let it be five-nineteenths [5/19] of the length **a–b** wide at the joint where it is joined to the arm. At the lowest joint of the thumb and the transverse line **n**, you shall draw half of that width which we demonstrated above the open palm ought to have. You shall draw just as much near the palm at the fleshy part. But because that part of the hand is hollowed into the palm, as it were, with the lowest parts of the thumb and little finger converging inward, it happens that the thickness of the oblique hand is not as great as it appears and as the outer lines express. At the first joint, you shall make the thumb as thick as the space from the top joint of the little finger upward; but at the summit, you shall contract it by subtracting one-fourth [1/4] from this thickness. Again, where it tends toward the lower joint, you shall make it quite thick; that will be the oblique width—that is, the thickness—of all the fingers in the same manner as we showed in the open palm; only in the joints does this occasionally fail.

And in this way, having noted the length, width, and thickness of the hand, you shall subsequently fashion its form appropriately with lines in your drawings, an example of which you see placed below by us. If anyone wishes to imitate this, I judge that he must labor with great diligence so as not to distort anything or go astray in the drawing of the lines. For indeed, to paint these harmoniously is no common task nor the work of a lazy hand.

Now next, I shall describe the foot or heel of a strong man more precisely and thoroughly, as I have done with the hand, following a similar calculation based on the length of the body, where the head is found to be one-eighth [1/8] of the total. 8.

Therefore, one-sixth [1/6] of this <note>One-sixth of the total body height.</note> shall be given to the foot, which will be widened by one-seventeenth [1/17]. You must enclose this space in a <term>quadrilateral</term> <note>original: "quadrilaterum"; a four-sided shape, in this case, a rectangle.</note>, which by the nature of the thing should be oblong. On the side where the upper part is longer, you shall assign the letter **a**; on the corresponding lower, longer part, **b**. Of the shorter sides, mark the first one—that is, the right side where the toes look—with the letter **c**, and the rear side near the heel with **d**. In this quadrilateral, the foot shall be drawn so that, when placed flat and pressed down, it touches all the sides of the figure. You shall first divide the length of this quadrilateral between **c** and **d** with two lines, **e** and **f**, into three equal spaces, so that **e** is placed first. At this point, it should be observed that although the letter marks may seem placed indiscriminately, the ones whose names are spoken first are always to be considered primary. And so, with these spaces arranged, you shall establish the toes of the foot in the first space between **c** and **e**. In the middle space between **e** and **f**, place whatever part of the open foot lies between the toes and the ankles, where the bones and nerves are. In the final space, place the heel and the joint of the leg with the foot. 

Afterward, you shall divide line **d** with six points—**g**, **h**, **i**, **k**, **l**, **m**—into seven equal spaces, and from point **g**, you shall draw a straight line to line **c**. Wherever that line touches the other, write the mark **n**. Where it truly cuts the perpendicular line **f**, write **o**. Afterward, divide the space between **l** and **m** through the middle with point **p**, from which point a line shall be drawn parallel to the perpendicular **f**; wherever it touches it, place **q** there. And from there, you shall soon draw an oblique line to **f**. Afterward, you shall place point **r** in the middle of side **e**, halfway between **a** and **b**, and soon divide the space between **r** and **b** with two points, **s** and **t**, into three equal spaces. Afterward, you shall divide the space **b–e** with two <gloss>two</gloss> points, **v** and **x**, into three equal spaces. And also divide **e–f** with two lines, **y** and **z**, into three equal spaces. Furthermore, divide **f–d** with two points, **s** and **t**, into three equal spaces. Soon, where you see line **g** cutting line **e**, assign the mark **s** there. Afterward, from point **h**, draw a transverse line to line **f**. Wherever it touches, assign **a j**. 

Afterward, divide **r–s** with two points, **1** and **2**, into three equal spaces. Also divide **y–e** with two points, **1** and **2**, into three equal spaces. And then divide **s–d** with four points—**1**, **2**, **3**, **4**—into five equal spaces. From point **3**, draw a perpendicular line to the transverse line **h a j**, and there place the mark **b j**. At this place it should be known that, according to this calculation of ours, all the marks of the shorter lines are found inscribed on the transverse line **a**. From there, several oblique lines are also derived: one **e–n** <term>diagonal</term> <note>original: "diagonios"; a line connecting opposite corners.</note>. Likewise, one **r–k**. Likewise, one from point **2** (between **r** and **s**) to point **y**. Likewise, one **t–z**. Likewise, one **b–o**, which is itself a diagonal. Likewise, one **v a j**. 

Afterward, you shall draw a straight line from point **st** to the oblique line **f–q**; wherever it touches, assign **e i**. Afterward, you shall fix one foot of a compass on point **k**, with the other reaching point **n**. Therefore, with that one fixed in **k** as said, you shall swing it downward to the oblique line **r–k**. And so, even now keeping that foot of the compass fixed, you shall swing it further from the oblique line **t–z** to the oblique line **b–o**. Furthermore, with one foot of the compass fixed in the same place, you shall move the other slightly so that it touches point **2** on line **b** within **y–e**, and from there swing it right across to the oblique line **b–o**. Next, you shall still leave that one foot of the compass fixed at point **k**, but you shall open the compass slightly so that the other foot touches point **r**, and from there swing it downward to the oblique line **t–z**. And by these curved lines, the toes of the foot and their extremities shall be marked. Of these, the lowest joints and the connections with the foot shall be arranged así: you shall set a point on line **e** midway between the oblique lines **b–o** and **v a j**. And in that same place, you shall place the bottom joint of the toe which is nearest to the smallest toe. Afterward, you shall fix one foot of the compass at point **z** and the other at that point just made on line **e**. And with that foot fixed at **z**, you shall swing this one upward to the transverse line **g–n** wherever it...
