Where the swinging leg of the compass touches <note>The author is describing the use of a compass to mark points on a drawing grid.</note>, that will be the location of the lower joint of the **big toe**. Next, on the curved line drawn upward in this manner, you shall place two middle points within the oblique lines **G. η. l. 3. b. o.** In that same place, you shall position the lowest joints of the **middle toe** and the toe next to the big toe. You will see their locations indicated in the illustration by small oblong circles. You shall mark the lowest joint of the **little toe** with a point placed above line **e.**, halfway between the transverse line **b.** and the oblique line **v. a. i.** 

Regarding the **big toe**, in which there are two joints, you will notice that the first part is somewhat long. You shall divide the **three middle toes** by the spaces between their joints, so that the lowest segment of each equals the other two in length. Furthermore, the total length of these spaces will exceed the middle spaces by one-quarter [1/4], which occurs because of that fleshy protrusion under the nails. Near the lowest joint of the little toe, you shall place a somewhat thick **callus** <note>original: "callum"; a pad of flesh or hardened skin.</note>, and you shall define its extremity at line **ρ.**. You shall also make the lower part of this toe somewhat longer than the others. You shall make the individual **nails** of the toes slightly less than half as long as the space of the part they sit upon. After this, you shall place one leg of the compass on point **η.** and the other slightly above the point of the lowest joint of the little toe on line **e.** From there, by swinging the compass upward, you shall mark with small lines where the foot begins to split into toes.

Furthermore, you shall draw the shapes of the clefts <note>The gaps between the toes.</note> and their hollows a bit further back. After this, you will notice that line **f.** touches the highest part of the foot where it is joined to the leg, which they now call the **mountain** <note>original: "montem"; the instep or arch of the foot.</note>. However, the shape of the section or cut—that is, the plane in this part—shall extend below line **f.** by two **z.** units toward line **s.** Finally, you shall divide **β. d.** at four points into three equal spaces. From point **2.**, you shall draw a straight line perpendicularly; this will touch the back of the ankle near the heel, which the Greeks call the <term>tenonta</term> <note>original: "tenonta"; referring to the Achilles tendon.</note>.

And so, with the transverse, parallel, perpendicular, oblique, diagonal, and curved lines thus arranged—by which the posture projected onto the plane of the foot is described—you shall consequently express the figure in the following manner.

You shall extend the **big toe** from line **e.** slightly above the oblique line **e. n.**, just as its lower joint protrudes. Next, you shall bend it slightly below the oblique line **e. n.** And again, where its upper joint protrudes on both sides, you shall bend it back up to line **e. n.** From there, you shall draw it straight until it is enclosed in the previously marked curvature between **n.** and **r.** Then, draw the whole part back over line **r. κ.** to the point where the foot splits into toes.

Afterward, in a similar way, you shall enclose the individual toes within their prescribed curvatures and the extended oblique lines. At this point, you should observe that the big toe does not extend perfectly straight in a **well-composed foot**, as is often seen in certain unattractive bodies. So that this might be better understood, I have described the positions of the toes using those oblique lines in the illustration, by which the toes may be depressed more or less, according to what beauty and the logic of the joints require. Thus, you will notice a space left between the big toe and the one next to it, while all the others lean toward the little toe, and the little toe presses against them on the oblique line **v. a. i.** You shall make the little toe as long as one-third of line **b.**, measured from point **e.** where its lowest joint stands.

I would wish for you to diligently learn and contemplate the forms of the individual toes, which are different from one another, as well as the bumps of the joints and the shapes of other parts that no one could name individually. Therefore, I refer you to the **illustration**, where these things both should and could be expressed. After this, from the bottom of the little toe toward the back of the foot, you shall round off the outer edge toward the angle of **b. η.**

However, you shall draw the **interior outlines** of the foot specifically, curving toward line **y.** Between the transverse—

When these things are finished, I have not neglected to provide the cross-sections through **e.** and **f.**, drawn separately as they would appear on a flat plane, so that this entire method of ours may be better understood. For in the same way I have described these two sections, you may trace the **cross-sections** <note>original: "caesuras"; these are transverse slices showing the thickness and shape of the limb at a specific height</note> of all parts of the entire body, whatever form they may take, whether straight or oblique.

You shall enclose these perpendicularly drawn lines with two transverse lines: thus you will have created a quadrilateral space longer on one side, with the length being as described in the previous form of the foot, and the height being one 24th. Within this, you shall draw this construction of the foot. Then, you shall divide side **a. b.** with two transverse lines **e. f.** into three equal spaces. Thus those perpendicular lines will indicate to you the positions of all parts of the foot lengthwise: namely, the extremities of the toes, then the joints and the gaps between them, and finally the positions of the nails. Lastly, you will see the face of the whole foot, and how, as I said, it is constructed: and its cross-section where the shin or leg is fixed into the heel. Indeed, the transverse lines will show you the height (which in this context is the thickness) in this manner. Place the sole or footprint plainly on line **b.** Next, divide side **c.** between the transverse lines **f. b.** with two points, **1.** and **2.**, into three equal spaces. Having also explained the method of the foot, I shall describe in the following sections the **female head** <note>original: "caput muliebre"</note> more precisely than I did above. I will follow the same path that I previously used in the refined investigation of the male head. Just as we instructed you elsewhere to make the female head one-eighth <note>Dürer’s measurement system uses the total height of the body as the unit "1." Thus, "one-eighth" means 1/8th of the total body height.</note> of the height in your images, you shall also do so now; and in this precise measurement, you shall adapt the quantity to the accurate manner of the male head. The whole matter shall be carried out as follows.

You shall arrange three quadrilaterals <note>four-sided shapes</note> with the help of a transfer tool. Each of these will have a height of one-eighth. The first will also have a similar width, and thus it will be a square. You shall make the width of each of the remaining two one-tenth. The square shall be assigned to the **profile view** <note>original: "obliquo capiti"; literally the "slanting" or "oblique" head</note> of the head. Of the remaining two, one shall be assigned to the **front view** <note>original: "adverso"</note> and the other to the **rear view** <note>original: "averso"</note>. You shall mark the two sides with letters: the right side, indeed, where the nose and mouth face, with the letter **a.**, and the left with the letter **b.** Next, with other perpendicular lines between **a.** and **b.**, you will distinguish the whole width of the head—or, as we said, its depth—marking all the parts as they begin from the front and recede toward the back. You shall mark each of these with their assigned notes; the numbers of the measurement, however, will be fractions of the total image length.

Therefore, you shall place the first line **c.** after **a.** at a space of one 80th. This is the part that the forehead touches above, where the roots of the hair are; the outer edges of the eyebrows will also touch it, which should not be joined above the nose. The upper lip will also touch the same. Next, you shall place another line **d.** at one 64th from **a.**; let the outer edges of the eyelids touch this line. Likewise the lower lip, and likewise the tip of the chin. Next, you shall place another line **e.** at one 56th from **a.** The hollow above the eyelids will touch it, as will the edge of the pupil, the innermost prominence of the nostrils, the corner of the cheek, and that which we called the **philtrum** <note>original: "nympham"; Dürer uses this term to refer to the vertical groove between the nose and the upper lip</note>. 

Next, you shall place another line **f.** at one 40th from **a.**; the corners of the eyes near the temples will touch it. Next, you shall place another line **g.** at one 32nd from **a.**; the inner eyebrows near the temples will touch it, as will the hollows under the jaws. Next, you shall place another line **h.** at one 22nd from **a.**; the edge of the neck will touch it. Next, you shall place another line **i.** at one 14th from **a.**; the beginning of the ear will touch it. Next, you shall place another line **k.** at one 11th from **a.**, which the outer edge of the ear will reach, but the earlobe will be suspended between this and **i.** Next, you shall place another line **l.** at one 9th from **a.**; the **crown of the head** <note>original: "vertex"</note> and the joining of the back of the head with the neck will touch it. You shall also lead this line downward from here. Finally, you shall place line **m.** at two 17ths from **a.**; this will define the extremity of the middle of the neck.

Once all the perpendicular lines have been drawn and the parts that should touch them have been noted, you will consequently distinguish the height—that is, the head above and below—which shall be done with parallel transverse lines.

The first of these, which will be the upper side, you shall make **n.**; the top of the head will touch it. The last, on the bottom side, you shall make **y.** Next, you shall draw the transverse line **o.** one 80th below **n.**; the crown will touch it. Next, you shall draw the transverse line **p.** one 30th from **n.**; the origin of the hair above the forehead will touch it. Next, you shall draw another line **q.** one 16th from **n.**; the tops of the eyelids will touch it. Likewise, the tops of the ears; and the eyebrows will lie on the same, running slightly upward in their middle. Next, you shall draw another line **t.** in the middle between **q.** and **y.**; the bottom of the nose will touch it, and the joining of the neck with the back of the head will fall upon the same. Next, you shall draw another line **r.**, by which the uppermost third part of the equally divided space between **q.** and **t.** shall be cut; the lower eyelids or cheeks shall touch it. But you shall place both corners of the eye, as well as the pupil, in the middle between **q.** and **r.** Next, you shall draw another line **s.**, by which the bottom fourth part shall be cut...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drafting the female head using a grid system. Within the equally divided space between lines **.q.** and **.t.**, the highest points of the nostrils and the bottom of the ear will touch those lines. Next, divide the space between **.t.** and **.r.** in the middle with line **.y.**; the top of the chin will reach this line. Then, divide **.t.** and **.r.** again with line **.v.** through the middle, which will pass through the center of the cheek. Next, divide the space **.t.v.** into three equal parts: in the lowest part, you shall place the upper lip. In the other two parts, you will draw that channel under the nose which we mentioned, which you will find the Greeks call the **philtrum** <note>original: "philtrum"; the vertical groove between the base of the nose and the upper lip.</note>. Likewise, divide **.v.r.** into two parts; in the upper one, include the lower lip, and in the lower one, the small valley between the lip and the chin called the **nymph** <note>original: "nympham"; an archaic anatomical term here referring to the labiomental groove.</note>.

After this, you shall draw a diagonal line from the angle made by lines **.c.p.** to the angle **.a.y.**; within the space **.p.q.**, the forehead will cross this line, while the nose will descend below it within the space **.q.t.**

With these tasks completed, you shall approach the next established quadrilateral <note>a four-sided shape</note> and mark it likewise with transverse lines using the transfer tool. Mark one of the two longer sides with the letter **a.** and the other with the letter **b.** Next, draw nine perpendicular lines, by which you will divide the entire space between **a.** and **b.** into ten equal spaces. These shall be distinguished by the same number of letters: namely, **d.e.f.g.h.i.k.l.m.** Next, divide the spaces **.a.d.** and **.m.b.** by drawing two lines, **.c.** and **.n.**, through the middle of each.

And so, with all the parts of the face arranged and designated, you shall express the form within the prepared quadrilaterals. However, you should use finer, softer, and less forceful lines than those used for the male images <note>Dürer suggests that feminine features should be rendered with more delicate linework to distinguish them from the more rugged "virile" proportions.</note>. First, draw the **profile head** <note>original: "obliquum caput"</note> so that the top touches the transverse line **.n.** near the perpendicular **.J.**, and the back of the head rests on side **.b.** within the space **.p.q.** Then, draw in the crown, the forehead, the nose, the cheek, the chin, the jaw, the ears, the eyes, and the open forehead—each in its previously noted place. You shall add the neck as one-fourteenth <note>A measurement of the total body height.</note> of the figure.

Next, you shall also shape the **front-facing head** <note>original: "aduersum caput"</note> so that the top touches the middle line **.n.** The temples should descend to sides **.a.** and **.b.**, to which they are applied on both sides within the space **.p.q.** Afterward, the rounding of the jaws shall be drawn from the perpendiculars **.c.** and **.n.** down to the transverse line **.r.**, where they meet at the chin. Both ears are to be expressed between lines **.a.e.** and **.n.b.** and the transverse lines **.q.s.** But the lowest parts of the ears should extend slightly downward, here beyond **.e.** and there beyond **.n.** Then, the neck should be drawn down from the ears to the lower side **.y.**, where it should have a width of one-fifteenth. After this, let the forehead open up on both sides between the perpendiculars **.a.e.** and **.n.b.** and the transverse lines **.p.q.** The roots of the hair are to be drawn from the angle **.h.p.** upward on both sides to the middle of the transverse lines **.p.o.** The eyes are to be placed so that their corners are defined by lines **.e.g.** and **.i.l.** The eyebrows in the space **.f.k.** should cross the middle transverse line **.q.** and end on both sides near the temples within the perpendiculars **.d.e.** and **.l.m.** Next, the nose shall be drawn, but the protruding "tubes" of the nostrils on both sides should be contained by the perpendiculars **.g.i.** on either side, which the corners of the cheeks will also closely reach.

After this, in the remaining quadrilateral, which is similar to the one in which the front-facing head was drawn, you shall place the figure and formation of the **rear-facing head** <note>original: "capitis auersi"</note>. And so, finally, you shall proceed to that projection onto the flat plane to find the surfaces of each individual height, following exactly the same path we showed you above in the treatment of the male head.

I have strove to present all these things in the examples with such diligence and attention that I trust they can be easily understood even without written instructions, provided one is not entirely foolish.

Furthermore, in foreshortening <note>original: "exaltando"; referring to the perspective view when the head is tilted or raised.</note> the female head, follow the same method shown above for the male, but moderate it, as all features of this sex ought to appear less grand.

<summary>This page provides specific geometric instructions for drafting the features of the female head (nostrils, chin, philtrum) and outlines how to render the head from profile, front, and rear views using a coordinate system.</summary>
<keywords>female proportions, facial geometry, Albrecht Dürer, philtrum, nymph, profile view, front view, rear view, drafting techniques, anatomical measurements</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the proportional measurements of adult female figures to the specific geometric construction of an infant's body, continuing Dürer’s systematic approach to human anatomy through grid-based drawing.</meta>

To these images of adult bodies of both sexes, I shall now add a description of a tender little body—that is, one of an infant age. The method for this will be similar to the ones described above. For, just as I showed before, three figures must be presented here with three lines of length: one for the profile <note>original: "formæ obliquæ"; a side view</note> form, another for the front-facing <note>original: "aduersæ"</note> view, and a third for the rear <note>original: "auersæ"</note> view. 

Two transverse <note>horizontal</note> lines will be placed above and below these, of which the upper one shall be marked **.a.** and the lower **.z.** And thus, in turn, the heights of the little body will be distinguished by transverse lines in this manner:

Since the proposed lines of length occupy the space of the little body from the top of the head to the bottom of the sole, so that the top of the head is at **.a.** and the sole is at **.z.**, you must first measure from the top of the head—which we called the **sinciput** <note>original: "sinciput"; the front and upper part of the skull</note>—one [part out of] 4 <note>Dürer’s system often uses "parts" where the total height is divided into specific units</note>. There, you shall draw one transverse line **.b.**; this will separate the bottom of the neck from the head through the throat. 

You shall include this space in a square in which the head is contained, which I have decided to describe more precisely through all its parts before I proceed to other measurements. Let the vertical side of this square—the one where the nose faces, or the front—likewise be **.a.**, and the other or posterior side be **.b.** 

You will first seek out the heights of the parts by marking the transverse lines, as we also instructed for the previous images. From **.a.**, you shall draw another transverse line at [a distance of] 24 [units]; this will touch the very top and edge of the forehead. Soon after, from **.a.**, a transverse line **.e.** shall be drawn at [a distance of] 16 [units]; it will pass through the crown of the head. From this, draw another transverse line **.d.** at 6 [units], which will touch the bottom of the chin and the junction of the **occiput** <note>original: "occipitij"; the back of the head</note> with the neck. And at this line, the tops of the shoulder blades <note>original: "scapulæ"</note> will also bulge. For this is how it happens in the rounding of the neck, when you represent it with curved lines...

<summary>This page introduces the proportional system for drawing an infant's body, specifically detailing the grid and fractional measurements required to map the head from profile, front, and rear perspectives.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, infant proportions, anthropometry, Renaissance drawing techniques, sinciput, occiput, geometric grid, anatomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the detailed geometric instructions for drawing an infant's head and body within a coordinate grid, transitioning from the vertical divisions of the head to the horizontal widths and the proportions of the torso.</meta>

...so there is no error. After this, you shall divide the side **.a.b.** into three equal spaces using two points. You will cut off the lowest of these with line **.e.**, upon which the little fold of flesh under the chin <note>original: "palearculum"; literally a "wattle," referring here to an infant's double chin</note> will rest; thus you see that the very short necks of these small bodies are described. Furthermore, you shall divide line **.a.b.** through the middle with line **.f.** The ends of the eyebrows will rest on this, and in the middle, they will rise slightly above it. This same line will touch the tops of the ears. At this line, the back of the head <note>original: "occipitium"</note> will curve toward side **.b.**

Furthermore, divide the space **.f. d.** <note>likely a coordinate reference in the diagram</note> through the middle with line **.g.**; the bottom of the nostrils and the bottom of the earlobe will touch it. Additionally, divide **.f.g.** into three equal spaces with two lines, **.h.** and **.i.** The upper of these, **.h.**, will pass through the pupil of the eye and both corners. The entire eye is contained within the horizontal lines **.f.** and **.i.**, and line **.i.** itself will touch the protruding parts above the nostrils <note>the flares of the nose</note>. The bottom of the ear will be contained between **.i.** and **.g.**

Afterward, divide **.g. d.** into five equal spaces with four horizontal lines: **.k. l. m. n.** Of these lines, **.k.** will touch the upper lip, and **.l.** will pass through the cheek. Then, divide the space between **.l.** and **.m.** through the middle; above this point, you will place the lower lip, and below it, extending down to **.m.**, that little valley—that is, the <term>nymphula</term> <note>a term used here for the vertical groove or hollow, likely the philtrum or the dip above the chin</note>—shall be inscribed. Furthermore, at line **.l.**, the back of the head will curve in a singular way, which you will recognize more clearly in the illustration. Line **.n.** will pass through the middle of the chin where that little hollow <note>a dimple</note> is seen, as well as the bottom of the jaws and the rising throat. 

Next, through these horizontal lines, vertical lines shall be drawn as indicators of the width—or, as I have said, the depth—of all parts, in this manner: In the middle of the sides **.a. b.**, you shall inscribe the vertical line **.c.** This will pass through the base of the ear and stand in the middle of the torso of the body. Then, draw another line, **.d.**, between **.a.** and **.c.** This will descend through the end of the eyebrow and the temple near the forehead, until it marks the beginning of the neck.

After this, divide **.a. d.** into five equal spaces with four lines: **.e. f. g. h.** Line **.e.** will touch the upper lip and the part of the nose where the bridge is seen to be slightly depressed. Line **.f.** will touch the outer edges of the eyebrows, the tip of the nose, the "little moon" of the cheeks <note>the curve of the cheekbone</note> near the mouth, and that groove above the chin which we called the <term>nymphula</term>. Line **.g.** will fall upon the hair above the forehead, the hollow of the eyelids, the pupil of the eye, and the lowest, innermost part of the chin. Line **.h.** will touch the corner of the eye near the temples. But the other center will be between **.g.** and **.f.** 

After this, divide **.a. e.** through the middle with line **.i.**; it will touch the edge of the forehead, which the nose will exceed slightly. After this, divide **.e. f.** with another vertical line, **.l.** <note>likely a secondary coordinate</note>, through the middle; it will touch the edge of the lower lip and the chin. After this, establish line **.k.** at an interval of three 20ths from **.a.**; this is where the ear ends. But the bottom of the earlobe will be described in the other part of the space between **.c.** and **.k.**, specifically that which is closest to **.c.** Furthermore, place line **.l.** at an interval of one 5th from **.a.** This will reach the bottom of the back of the head where it joins with the receding neck. The neck itself will end in the middle of the space between **.k.** and **.l.** Finally, divide **.l. b.** with three points into four equal spaces, the third of which will designate the crown of the head. 

Thus, having noted all parts of the head within the established square of heights and widths, before I express them in drawing, I shall set down the measurements of the remaining parts of this small figure of stature, and I shall follow through with the front and rear views.

Between the top of the thigh (or the hip) and the sole of the foot, you shall mark the middle of the knee. From the middle of the knee to the lowest inner ankles, let there be one [part] 36 <note>These numbers refer to specific units or coordinates on the drafting grid used by the artist to ensure perfect symmetry.</note>. Likewise, from there to the lowest part of the calf, one [part] 9. From the sole upward, let one [part] 20 be the location of the "mountain" <note>original: "montis"; referring to the instep or the arch of the foot</note> on the foot. The arm, however, is described thus:

From the shoulder to the joint of the elbow, let two [parts] 11 be drawn. From the joint of the elbow to the tips of the fingers, one [part] 4. From here, however, back to the wrist joint, one [part] 9.

Following are the notations for the parts across the width of the **oblique image** <note>the figure seen from the side or in profile</note>, which represent thickness.

Across the shoulders, let there be two [parts] 15.
Across the top of the chest, let there be one [part] 6.
Across the nipples, two [parts] 11.
Below the same, one [part] 11 and one [part] 12.
Across the loins where we are girded <note>the waist</note>, one [part] 6.
Across the navel, two [parts] 11.
Across the curve of the hips, one [part] 10 and one [part] 11.
Across the top of the thigh, one [part] 9 and one [part] 10.
Across the lower belly and the buttocks, one [part] 5.
Across the outer testicles, one [part] 6.

The thickness of the thigh itself, immediately below the buttocks, shall be one [part] 7, and a little lower, one [part] 14 and one [part] 15.
Above the knee, it shall be one [part] 8.
Across the middle of the knee, one [part] 10.
Below the knee, one [part] 11.
Across the middle of the calf, one [part] 10.
Across the lower calf, one [part] 12.
Above the mountain of the foot, one [part] 16.
The length of the heel, moreover, shall be one [part] 13 and 14.

But the parts of the oblique arm shall be widened thus:
Across the shoulders, let it be one [part] 10.
Under the armpit, one [part] 12.
Across the elbow joint, one [part] 16.
Across the forearm nearest the joint, one [part] 15.
A little further, one [part] 18.
A little above the hand, one [part] 23.
But the **Palm** shall be thickened into one [part] 21.

And so, once all the parts of the oblique body have been noted, you shall pass to the position of the **front-facing image**. You shall bring over to it all the lines of length from the oblique body by means of a transfer tool. And so, in turn, you shall also examine the widths. And first, indeed, you shall track the head more precisely, having established here also a quadrilateral <note>a four-sided grid</note>, which now, however, will cease to be a square; for the width will be two [parts] 9, and thus somewhat shorter than the length. Let the sides of this width of the quadrilateral—that is, the vertical lines—be noted with the letters **p.** and **b.** Then you shall divide it through the middle with the perpendicular line **c.**, which will indeed pass through the crown, the nose, the cheek, and the chin. 

Afterward, you shall divide the spaces **a. c.** and **c. b.** through the middle with two lines, **d.** and **e.** These two lines will define the outer edges of the eyebrows near the temples, and likewise the neck. Afterward, you shall divide the space between **d.** and **e.** into three equal spaces with two lines, **f.** and **g.** To these will reach the corners of the eyes near the nose, the outer protrusions of the nostrils, and the corners of the cheeks; and by these, the chin shall also be defined. 

After this, you shall divide the space **a. d.** into three equal spaces with two lines, **h.** and **i.** Likewise, divide **e. b.** into the same number of equal spaces with the same number of lines, **k.** and **l.** The lines **h.** and **l.** will define the width of the face crosswise. The ears indeed shall be inscribed such that their tops tend toward **a.** and **b.** on both sides. The bottoms of the ears, however, shall fall sloping within **h.** and **l.** The two perpendiculars **i.** and **k.** will touch the "little moons" of the cheeks <note>original: "lunulas malarum"; the rounded, puffed-out part of an infant's cheeks</note>, that is, the seemingly swollen cheeks. After this, you shall divide **a. h.** and likewise **l. b.**, both into five equal spaces using four points. And from the two points nearest **a.** and **b.** on both sides, you shall draw two perpendiculars, **m.** and **n.** These will define the width of the head between the cross-lines **c.** and **f.** The forehead, however, will open up on both sides beyond **h.** and **l.**, between the cross-lines **c.** and **f.** The eyebrows will end on both sides between **i.** and **d.** and **e.** and **k.**, below the cross-line **f.**

And thus arranged, and with the notation of all parts within this quadrilateral, you shall proceed to track the remaining widths of this little body <note>original: "corpusculi"; this refers to the small figure or child being used as the model for these proportions</note>. And you shall make it this wide:

Across the shoulders: one [part] 4.
And in that same place you shall place the shoulder bones, at a distance from each other of two [parts] 9.
Across the highest part of the chest, the width will be two [parts] 7.
Between the armpits: one [part] 5.
Between the nipples: one [part] 7.

Under the armpit, wide: one [part] 17.
Through the tendon of the elbow: one [part] 16.
Below that: one [part] 14.
Through the joint of the hand <note>the wrist</note>: one [part] 20.

> Now, let the total length of this kind of small body be a third part of the length of the master body <note>original: "corporis materni"; literally "maternal body," referring here to the primary or adult figure from which this smaller scale is derived</note>. Once this has been measured as prescribed, the appropriate form must consequently be expressed in the oblique <note>side/profile</note>, the front-facing, and also the back-facing views. Just as we show not only by the writing, but even somewhat more clearly by the examples placed below, where both the curve of the spine and the positions of the other limbs are indicated. Otherwise, the back-facing small body will have the same outer lines as the front-facing one. But between the armpits, the width will be slightly more spread out than one [part] 5. But the crack of the buttocks <note>original: "rima podicis"</note> shall occupy one [part] 9. The heel <note>original: "calcaneus"</note> will extend one [part] 22.

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book Two of Albrecht Dürer's treatise. While the previous section concluded the study of a child's proportions using a fractional grid, this page introduces a more sophisticated and precise decimal-like measurement system involving specialized symbols for the "measuring rod," "number," "portion," and "fragment."</note>

<sig>G iiij</sig> <note>This is a printer's signature mark, used to help binders assemble the book in the correct order.</note>

# Albrecht Dürer on Symmetry
## book two.

This second book on the measurements of the human body's limbs will cover the same subject we treated in the previous book, but it will show another way to arrive at the proper sizes of parts in the human body. This method will be somewhat more refined, and we will proceed by using a <term>measuring rod</term> <note>original: "perticula"</note> and its various divisions in this manner.

You shall prepare a <term>measuring rod</term> whose length is one-third of the total height of the figure you wish to create. In our instructions, we have made its sign or mark like this: — ☧

You will divide this into ten equal spaces, each of which shall be called a <term>number</term> <note>original: "numerus"</note> and marked with this sign: — Ƹ

Next, divide the "number" I mentioned into ten equal spaces likewise. Each of these shall be called a <term>portion</term> <note>original: "portio"</note> and have this sign: — ⊥

Finally, divide one of these "portions" into three equal spaces. Each of these shall be called a <term>fragment</term> <note>original: "frustulum," literally "a small crumb"</note> and is to be understood by this sign: — %

You will proceed in this way: when you wish to begin the work, you should have a suitable tablet at hand, in which you will draw five straight lines like little columns. Thus, there will be four spaces between the columns. You will assign each its own sign: to the first, the <term>measuring rod</term> ☧; to the second, the <term>number</term> Ƹ; to the third, the <term>portion</term> ⊥; and to the fourth, the <term>fragment</term> %. And when you begin to seek out the heights, widths, and thicknesses of the body through all its parts and limbs, you will add the appropriate measurement to each recorded note, written in its own space—whether it falls under the measuring rod, the number, the portion, or the fragment. If, by chance, nothing falls into a space, you will mark it like this: o. You will proceed with the measurements in the order demonstrated above. You will set out three lines for the total length of the figure to be made: one for the profile view <note>original: "obliquo corpori"</note>, another for the front view <note>original: "aduerso"</note>, and the third for the rear view <note>original: "auerso"</note>. At the top and bottom of these, you will place two horizontal lines. Then, you will first define all parts of the height in the profile view with horizontal parallel lines, from which you will extend them to the front view as the work requires. For the proportion of height follows a simple logic.

As we have done before and will do everywhere, we have explained these things with examples placed below. In these, you will see the measurements of lengths or heights added in the margin for each profile figure, drawn from the individual horizontal indicator lines, as was said. Each measurement will have its own signs from those we mentioned: the measuring rod, the number, the portion, and the fragment. Likewise, you will see the spaces noted for widths and thicknesses. Therefore, with these things introduced and explained, I will proceed to present the system of measurements, teaching in the customary manner, and beginning first with height.

<summary>In this introduction to Book Two, Dürer introduces a new, highly precise measurement system based on a "measuring rod" (one-third of total height) divided into numbers, portions, and fragments. This system allows for standardized recording of body proportions across profile, frontal, and rear views.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Book Two, measurement system, measuring rod, perticula, decimal proportions, artistic anatomy, Renaissance geometry</keywords>

By defining these exact locations, he provided artists with a mathematical framework to reconstruct the human form with perfect Renaissance symmetry.</note>

<note>In these woodcuts, Dürer moves from theory to practice. By labeling the female form with these specific numbers and "Parts," he attempted to create a universal mathematical canon for beauty and anatomy. This allowed other Renaissance artists to reproduce "perfect" proportions by following his grid rather than relying solely on visual estimation.</note>

Another **proportional measurement** <note>original: "commensuratio"</note> of the male body follows, using and maintaining the method shown above. And first, you shall measure the lengths.

From the front of the head <note>original: "sincipite"</note> to the crown, the face shall be:

| | <term>rod</term> <note>original: "perticula," Dürer’s largest unit of measurement</note> | number | portion | bit |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| To the forehead | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| To the bottom of the chin | 0 | 8 | 6 | 0 |
| To the top of the shoulder blades | 0 | 9 | 2 | 0 |
| To the collarbones | 1 | 1 | 4 | 0 |

From the top of the forehead to the very bottom of the chin, three equal spaces shall be made by two lines; in one of these the forehead is placed, in the second the nose, eyes, and ears. In the third, the cheek and chin; afterward you will proceed to measure:

| | <term>rod</term> | number | portion | bit |

| Let it be through the tops of the shoulders | 1 | 0 | 2 | 0 |
| Through the <term>collarbones</term> (iugula: the hollow where the neck meets the chest) | 1 | 2 | 2 | 0 |

Once these things are completed, you will adapt the configuration of this body also with notations, just as is appropriate for the profile and front-facing posture. But you shall draw the front view within the outer lines of the front-facing line. Furthermore, the space of height between the collarbones and the back armpits has been indicated above.

# Proportional Study of the Female Figure (Rear View)

<note>This page consists of a detailed woodcut from Albrecht Dürer’s 1528 treatise. It depicts a female figure from behind, overlaid with a coordinate-like system of horizontal lines. These lines correspond to the anatomical points described in the previous tables (such as the shoulders, waist, and calves). The fractions and letters indicate the specific widths required to draw this figure in perfect proportion.</note>

->[Woodcut illustration of a female figure from behind]<-

3/4

1/8 <term>Trum</term> <note>original: "T"; a major unit of measurement in Dürer's system, equal to 1/10th of a "foot" or 1/60th of the total body height.</note> <term>Teil</term> <note>original: "L"; a "part" or subdivision, representing 1/10th of a Trum (or 1/600th of the total body height).</note>

5/8 <term>Trum</term> <term>Teil</term>

1/8 3 <term>Trums</term>

<note>Dürer’s use of these fractions—3/4, 1/8, and 5/8—applied to his "Trum" and "Teil" units allowed artists to replicate his proportions regardless of the total size of the drawing. This system, known as the Exempeda, was a significant attempt to bring the precision of architecture to the study of human anatomy.</note>

<summary>This page presents a woodcut illustration of a female figure viewed from the rear, featuring Albrecht Dürer’s specific proportional measurements. It serves as a visual application of the mathematical rules for human anatomy established in his 1528 treatise.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, woodcut, anatomy, Renaissance art, Trum, Teil, female figure, measurement systems</keywords>

In the following sections, we will describe another male body that differs from the previous ones, following the same method as before. First, I will prescribe the length of the parts, which constitutes the height; these should be recorded according to the primary line of the figure in profile <note>original: *obliqui corporis*; literally "oblique body," referring here to a side-view or three-quarter view</note>, though they may be applied to other views as far as necessary.

I iii

<note>This page features a seminal woodcut illustration from Albrecht Dürer's *Four Books on Human Proportion* (original: *Vier Bücher von menschlicher Proportion*), published posthumously in 1528. The diagram establishes a system of measurement for the human body, using **Parts** <note>original: "Teile," abbreviated as T</note> and **Lines** <note>original: "Linien," abbreviated as L</note> as the primary units of proportion.</note>

# Proportions of the Male Body: Posterior View

<note>This page features a central diagram of a male figure seen from behind. The figure is marked with a series of coordinates and fractional measurements used to define the "ideal" symmetry of the back, shoulders, and legs. Extensive ghosting from the reverse side of the paper is visible, but the primary markings remain clear.</note>

->**The Rear View of the Human Figure**<-

### Proportional Markers and Measurements
<note>In his treatise, Dürer used fractions to indicate how many parts of the total body height each specific anatomical width or segment should occupy.</note>

<gloss>4/3</gloss>
<gloss>1</gloss>
<gloss>39/75</gloss>
<gloss>H H</gloss> <note>The letters 'H' serve as horizontal markers, likely indicating the outermost points of the shoulder blades or the hips to ensure lateral symmetry.</note>
<gloss>17/73</gloss>

***

### Anatomical Points of Reference
<note>While the text on this specific page is dominated by numerical data, the markers correspond to the following anatomical features visible in the diagram:</note>

*   **The Upper Torso:** Measurements for the width of the shoulders and the alignment of the shoulder blades.
*   **The Spine:** The vertical axis used to balance the left and right sides of the body.
*   **The Pelvic Region:** Specific markers for the width of the waist and the curve of the buttocks.
*   **The Lower Limbs:** Proportions for the thighs, the tapering of the calves, and the placement of the ankles.
*   **The Feet:** Measurements for the heels and the outer edge of the feet.

<note>Dürer’s system was intended to allow artists to recreate the human form with mathematical precision, treating the body as a geometric problem to be solved through "symmetry," which in this context means "proportionality."</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the posterior proportions of the male figure, using fractional measurements and coordinate markers to define the symmetry of the back and limbs. It represents a key component of Albrecht Dürer's scientific approach to artistic anatomy.</summary>

<keywords>human proportion, anatomy, symmetry, Renaissance, Albrecht Dürer, measurement, posterior view, male figure, mathematical art</keywords>

<note>Dürer’s system of measurement on this page uses four units: the 'perticula' (part), 'numerus' (number), 'portio' (portion), and 'scustulum' (minute part). These represent ever-smaller fractions of the total body height, allowing an artist to scale the figure to any size while maintaining "perfect" Renaissance proportions.</note>

Now, following this, I shall present another measurement of the **male body**, observing the order established above, and first setting forth the **height**. It will be as follows:

| Description | <term>rod<span>original: *perticula*; Dürer's largest unit, representing 1/6th of the total body height</span></term> | <term>number<span>original: *numerus*; a secondary division of the body height</span></term> | <term>portion<span>original: *portio*; a further fractional division</span></term> | <term>grain<span>original: *frustula*; the smallest unit of measurement in this system</span></term> |

| To the top of the shoulder blades | 0 | 8 | 3 | 0 |
| To the collarbones | 1 | 0 | 6 | 0 |

Then, between the top of the **forehead** and the bottom of the **chin**, you shall mark three equal spaces with two lines. In the highest of these, you will place the forehead; in the middle, the nose, eyes, and ears; and in the lowest, the cheek and chin. And thus you shall continue to measure.

For this body, if you fix the point of a compass on the navel as the center, a circular line touching the toes of the extended foot will also graze the tips of the fingers when the arms are spread and raised upwards. <note>This is Dürer's description of the "Vitruvian Man," a concept of perfect geometry where the human body fits within both a square and a circle.</note> All of these things we have presented for contemplation in the following examples.

### Scientific Proportions of the Human Body
**Anatomy** | **Proportion** | **Albrecht Dürer**

<note>This section represents the application of Renaissance humanism to the study of the body. Albrecht Dürer (1471–1528) was obsessed with finding a mathematical formula for beauty. He believed that by using a compass and ruler, an artist could recreate the "perfect" human form as designed by nature.</note>

The measurements are divided into:
*   **Part** <note>original: *Teil*</note>: The primary division (1/10th of the body).
*   **Segment** <note>original: *Trum*; also referred to as a "piece"</note>: A smaller subdivision used for fine details like the thickness of a finger or the curve of an ankle.
*   **Woodcut** <note>original: *Holzschnitt*</note>: The method of printing used to create the diagram, where the image is carved into a block of wood.

<note>The geometric arc shown in the diagram—which is not explicitly transcribed but is essential to the page—demonstrates the "sweep" of the limbs. It shows how the body fits within a circle or square, a concept famously explored by Vitruvius and later Leonardo da Vinci.</note>

<summary>This page lists the precise mathematical measurements for the rear view of a male figure, utilizing Dürer’s decimal-like system of "Parts" and "Segments." It serves as a technical manual for artists to achieve perfect anatomical proportions through geometry.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, anatomy, Renaissance art theory, anthropometry, mathematical measurement, woodcut illustration, 16th-century science</keywords>

| Through the muscles, the shoulder width | 0 | 2 | 5 | 0 |
| Above the elbow joint | 0 | 2 | 0 | 0 |
| Below the same | 0 | 2 | 6 | 0 |
| Through the wrist joint | 0 | 1 | 6 | 0 |
| The palm shall be opened <note>i.e., measured across its width.</note> | 0 | 2 | 9 | 0 |

With these descriptions of the parts of the three-fold female stature, you will accommodate the figures, each in its proper place. You will also circumscribe the rear view—as we have often advised—within the same outer lines as the front view has. But the natal cleft <note>the vertical groove of the buttocks</note> shall measure:

| | † | ε | — | % |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| | 0 | 5 | 7 | 0 |
| And the heel | 0 | 1 | 5 | 0 |

If you fix the point of a compass on the navel of this body with the feet extended and the hands spread upwards, you will be able to draw a circle so that the extremities of the fingers and the heel touch the arc of the circle. <note>This is a female variation of the famous 'Vitruvian Man' geometry, proving the body's proportions fit perfectly within a circle centered on the navel.</note> Just as we have shown in the examples.

<summary>This page provides comprehensive width and thickness measurements for the female body, ranging from the head to the feet, and concludes with the geometric proof that a well-proportioned body fits within a circle centered on the navel.</summary>
<keywords>female proportions, anatomy, geometry, Vitruvian circle, Albrecht Dürer, anthropometry, artistic theory, Renaissance measurements</keywords>

# Study of Human Proportion: Posterior View

<note>This page contains the numerical data for an anatomical diagram. Albrecht Dürer used a modular system where the body is divided into "Parts" (marked as 'T' for the German <term>Teile</term>). These measurements define the width and length of muscles and bones in relation to the figure's total height.</note>

### Key Terminology
*   **Anatomy**: The study of the structure of the body.
*   **Proportion**: The comparative relation between things or magnitudes.
*   **Dürer**: Referring to Albrecht Dürer (1471–1528), the author and artist.
*   **Geometry**: The branch of mathematics concerned with shapes and lines.
*   **Anthropometry**: The scientific study of the measurements and proportions of the human body.
*   **Parts**: <note>original: "Teile"</note> The primary unit of measurement in Dürer's proportional system.
*   **Numerals**: The digits used to express the specific subdivisions of the "Parts."

<note>The diagram associated with these numbers shows a man viewed from behind. His right arm is extended to the side, touching a vertical boundary. A curved line (a circular arc) originates from a point on his spine, demonstrating how the rotation of the arm fits within a perfect geometric circle. This illustrates the Renaissance belief that the human body is governed by mathematical laws.</note>

<summary>This page provides the specific numerical measurements (in "Parts") and geometric labels for a posterior diagram of a male figure. It demonstrates Dürer's method of using math to map the human body and its range of motion within a circular arc.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, Albrecht Dürer, posterior view, anatomical measurement, Renaissance geometry, anthropometry, Teile</keywords>

<note>Dürer’s system of measurement on this page breaks the body down into four units: **Parts** (the largest division), **Numbers**, **Portions**, and **Fragments**. These are not standard inches or centimeters, but fractions of the figure's total height, allowing an artist to scale the drawing to any size while maintaining perfect proportions.</note>

| **Then you will arrange the arm in this way.** | | | | |
| Wide below the armpits | 0 | 2 | 4 | 0 |
| Above the elbow joint | 0 | 2 | 1 | 0 |
| Below the same | 0 | 2 | 9 | 0 |
| Through the joint of the hand <note>The wrist.</note> | 0 | 1 | 5 | 0 |
| But you shall make the palm wide | 0 | 2 | 8 | 0 |

With these notations, you shall outline the figure of the male and female body with suitable and appropriate curves, as you will be able to see we have striven to do in the examples. But if you wish to place a slightly larger head upon these bodies, you shall extend it on the line of height. — <margin>0 0 8 0</margin>

And thus, having been lengthened, you shall also make it slightly thicker and slightly wider in the oblique and frontal body, just as it was shown ought to be done before and as has been demonstrated by us in the examples.

Dürer’s system relies on dividing the total body height into fixed units: the "particle" (1/6th of the total height), the "number" (1/10th of a particle), the "portion" (1/10th of a number), and the "scruple" (1/10th of a portion).</meta>

In this body, with the arms extended so that the fingertips equal the height of the top of the head, and with the foot of a compass placed on the navel, the line of the circle will touch the extremities of the head, hands, and feet; this itself you may observe in the examples. <note>This describes the "Homo Quadratus" or Vitruvian Man principle, where a human figure with outstretched limbs fits within a square and a circle.</note>

<term>Albrecht Dürer</term> — The leading artist of the Northern Renaissance who sought to find a mathematical basis for beauty.
<term>Proportion</term> — The relationship of the size of one body part to another.
<term>Anatomy</term> — The study of the internal and external structure of the body.
<term>Renaissance</term> — A period of "rebirth" in Europe (14th-17th century) where art and science became deeply intertwined.
<term>Theory of Proportion</term> — A system of rules used by artists to create "perfect" or realistic human figures.
<term>Geometry</term> — The mathematical study of shapes, used here to define the swing of the arm.
<term>Woodcut</term> — A printing technique where an image is carved into wood; this was the primary way Dürer’s books were mass-produced.

<note>Dürer’s use of the circular arc demonstrates his belief that the human body functions like a machine governed by geometric laws. By recording these measurements, he provided a toolkit for other artists to recreate the human form without needing a live model present.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the posterior view of Dürer's male figure, detailing specific measurements for the back and the circular trajectory of the arm. It highlights Dürer’s application of geometry and "Parts" to map anatomical structure and movement.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, Albrecht Dürer, anatomy, rear view, geometric arc, Renaissance art theory, mathematical drawing, musculoskeletal measurements</keywords>

This reflects the Renaissance belief that man was the "measure of all things."</note>

### Key Technical Terms

*   **Anatomy**: The study of the internal and external structures of the body.
*   **Proportion**: The relationship of one part of the body to another and to the whole.
*   **Symmetry**: The balanced arrangement of body parts on opposite sides of a center axis.
*   **Anthropometry**: The scientific study of the measurements and proportions of the human body.
*   **Albrecht Dürer**: The German Renaissance master (1471–1528) who authored this treatise to provide artists with a mathematical basis for drawing the human form.
*   **Woodcut**: A relief printing technique where an image is carved into a block of wood; this was the primary method for illustrating books in the 1500s.

<summary>This page features a woodcut of a male figure from the back, used to demonstrate Dürer’s geometric system of human proportion. It includes precise fractional measurements for the figure's width and posture, illustrating the intersection of art and mathematics during the Renaissance.</summary>

<keywords>posterior anatomy, human proportion, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance art theory, geometric drawing, mathematical symmetry, anthropometry</keywords>

I shall again describe the male body according to a different method, as I have shown should be done above. Its measurement <note>original: "commensuratio"</note> is as follows.

| | module | part | portion | bit |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| From the forehead <note>original: "sinciput"</note> to the crown | 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 |

| To the top of the shoulder blades | 0 | 8 | 6 | 0 |
| To the top of the shoulders | 1 | 0 | 6 | 0 |
| To the collarbones <note>original: "iugula"</note> | 1 | 1 | 5 | 0 |

Then, whatever space lies between the top of the forehead and the bottom of the chin, you shall divide into three equal spaces with two lines. The highest part shall measure the forehead; the next, the eyes, nose, and ears; the lowest, the mouth <note>original: "bucca"</note> and chin. And although it is correct to fashion the face this way in all images, we do not always remember to do so. You, however, will be able to transfer these proportions from one instance to all others.

<note>These measurements correspond to the physical reach and the skeletal structure of the back and arms shown in the diagram. Dürer used these precise fractions to allow other artists to recreate the human form perfectly to scale, regardless of the size of their canvas.</note>

Let us also describe the female body of suitable stature. Let the height, therefore, be measured from the crown of the head <note>original: "sincipite"; referring here to the very top or front-top of the head.</note>.

| | <term>small pole</term> ⎿ <note>original: "perticula"; Dürer’s largest unit of measure, usually 1/6th of the total body height.</note> | <term>number</term> ○ <note>original: "numerus"; a decimal subdivision of the small pole.</note> | <term>portion</term> / <note>original: "portio"; a smaller subdivision, usually 1/10th of a "number."</note> | <term>fragment</term> ╳ <note>original: "frustulum"; the smallest unit in this system, 1/10th of a "portion."</note> |

<meta>This page transitions from the study of male proportions to the specific measurements of the female figure, as detailed in Albrecht Dürer’s foundational work on anthropometry. # Female Proportional Measurements

->Anatomical Proportions of the Female Body<-

The following values represent the standardized measurements used to construct a geometrically "perfect" female form. These units are part of a complex scaling system where the total height of the body is divided into specific fractions.

## Head and Neck
3, <term>Thirds</term> <note>original: "T3" for "Trittel"; in Dürer’s system, this represents a third of a smaller unit of measure</note>, 5, 9, 3, 9, 5, <term>Thirds</term>, 3, 3
<term>Thirds</term>, 2, 1, 1, 2

## Torso and Arms
9, 5, 1, 2, 1, 1, <term>Thirds</term> 1/3, 8, 9, <term>Thirds</term>, 1, 3, 6, 7, <term>Thirds</term>

**Arm and Hand:**
4, 3, 2, 3, 4, 2, 8, 1, 3, 2

## Waist and Hips
8, 8, 1 1, 2, 2, <term>Thirds</term> 1/3, 9, 5, 1, 3, 1, 3, 2, 1, 3, 1, <term>Thirds</term> 1/3, 1, 3, 1, 3, 1, <term>Thirds</term> 1/3, 1, 3, 4, 1, 9, 9, <term>Thirds</term>, <term>Thirds</term>, 1, 9, 2

## Legs and Feet
5, 6, <term>Thirds</term>, 4, 2, <term>Thirds</term>, 1, 3, 1, 3, <term>Thirds</term>, 1, 2, 4, 3, 3, 9, <term>Thirds</term> 3 + 2, <term>Thirds</term>, 1, 1, <term>Thirds</term> 1, 1, 3, 5, <term>Thirds</term>

<note>Dürer’s "Trittel" (Thirds) allow for minute adjustments in the thickness and curvature of the limbs, ensuring that the drawing remains mathematically consistent even in the smallest details of the ankles or wrists.</note>

<summary>This page lists the precise numerical proportions for a female figure according to Albrecht Dürer's geometric system, using units known as "Trittel" (thirds) to define body volume and width.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, female anatomy, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance mathematics, Trittel, anthropometry, artistic measurement</keywords>

<meta>This page marks a transition from the female proportions discussed on the previous page back to the male figure. # The Proportions of the Male Body: Posterior View

->Geometric Measurements of the Figure from Behind<-

This page features a detailed woodcut illustration of human proportions, showing a male figure from the back. This perspective is vital for artists to understand the symmetry of the shoulders and the alignment of the spine. The figure's right arm is extended horizontally, and an arc—a quarter-circle—is drawn connecting the fingertip to the base of the feet. This geometric frame helps define the reach of the limbs in relation to the body's total height.

### The System of Measurement

Various numerical fractions are placed at key anatomical points to denote proportions. Like the female figure described previously, Dürer uses a modular system to ensure every part of the body is mathematically linked to the whole.

**The Torso and Shoulders:**
Measurements here define the breadth of the shoulders and the narrowing of the waist. 
<margin>Width of the back</margin>
The numbers indicate the distance from the spine to the outer edge of the <term>scapulae</term> <note>The shoulder blades.</note>.

**The Limbs:**
The extended arm allows for a measurement of the limb's full length, while the legs are measured at the widest part of the calf and the narrowest part of the ankle.
<note>The quadrant arc illustrates the "sweep" of the arm, a technique used to ensure that the limbs are not drawn too long or too short for the torso.</note>

### Proportional Units
As seen in the earlier studies, this diagram utilizes <term>Thirds</term> <note>original: "Trittel"; in Dürer’s system, this represents a third of a smaller unit of measure, allowing for extreme precision.</note>. These small units are essential for capturing the subtle curves of the back and the thickness of the thighs.

*   **Upper Body:** Fractions defining the nape of the neck and the depth of the chest.
*   **Lower Body:** Precise values for the buttocks, the back of the knees, and the heels.

<note>Dürer’s "Thirds" allow for minute adjustments in the thickness and curvature of the limbs, ensuring that the drawing remains mathematically consistent even in the smallest details of the ankles or wrists.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the posterior proportions of a male figure, using a quadrant arc to demonstrate the geometric relationship between the body's height and the reach of the arms.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, male anatomy, Albrecht Dürer, posterior view, geometry in art, Renaissance measurement, Trittel</keywords>

# The Proportions of the Female Body

Next follows the description of another female body, matching the male body described above. Therefore, its heights will be as follows:

| | Rod | Number | Portion | Fragment |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| From the <term>top of the head</term> <note>original: "sincipite"</note> to the <term>crown</term> <note>original: "verticem"</note> let it be | 0 | 0 | 7 | 0 |
| To the <term>forehead</term> | 0 | 1 | 6 | 0 |
| To the bottom of the <term>chin</term> | 0 | 6 | 4 | 0 |
| To the top of the <term>shoulder blades</term> | 0 | 8 | 7 | 0 |
| To the <term>collarbones</term> <note>original: "iugula"</note> | 1 | 0 | 2 | 0 |
| From the <term>forehead</term> to the <term>eyebrows</term> | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |

<note>Dürer uses a hierarchical measurement system: 1 Rod (perticula) is divided into 10 Numbers (numerus), each Number into 10 Portions (portio), and each Portion into 10 Fragments (frustulum). This allowed for extreme precision in Renaissance art.</note>

Afterward, you shall divide the space between the <term>eyebrows</term> and the <term>chin</term> through the middle with one transverse line; with the two spaces thus described being equal, you will attribute the <term>nose</term>, <term>eyes</term>, and <term>ears</term> to the upper space. To the other, the <term>cheek</term> and <term>chin</term>. And so you shall proceed to measure:

| To the top of the <term>thigh</term> <note>original: "femur"</note> | 0 | 6 | 5 | 0 |
| To the bottom of the <term>belly</term> | 0 | 7 | 5 | 0 |

This body likewise can be enclosed within the circular line of a compass that touches the extremities of the hands and feet, as has been said several times above regarding others, and expressed by us in the examples. <note>Dürer refers here to the "Vitruvian" principle where a well-proportioned human body fits within a circle and a square.</note>

# Posterior View of the Male Figure

<note>This page features a diagram of a male figure seen from the rear, illustrating a system of proportions. Albrecht Dürer used this woodcut to show how the human form can be measured and mapped using a consistent scale. The figure is marked with alignment lines and a circular arc extending from the arm to define the body's reach and geometric symmetry.</note>

<note>The numerical values correspond to Dürer’s <term>Parts <note>original: "Teile" (abbreviated as T in the original woodcut), a fractional unit representing 1/600th of the figure's total height.</note></term> system. This allowed artists to recreate perfectly proportioned figures regardless of the physical size of the drawing or sculpture.</note>

In the following, I shall describe the figure of a man of quite tall and slender stature, which may serve as an example for anyone wishing to depict a somewhat elongated body. Therefore, his measurement <note>original: "commensuratio"</note> shall be as follows.

<note>Dürer’s measurement system uses four units: the **Rod** (T), which is 1/6th of the total body height; the **Number** (ε), which is 1/10th of a Rod; the **Portion** (λ), which is 1/10th of a Number; and the **Fragment** (x), which is 1/3rd of a Portion. This allowed for extremely precise anatomical ratios that could be scaled to any size.</note>

It exemplifies Dürer's Renaissance attempt to use geometry to standardize various human body types for artists.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, slender man, anatomy, Renaissance art, anthropometry, mathematical drawing, De Symmetria partium</keywords>

And thus accordingly you shall represent the figure with appropriate outlines of the threefold body <note>This refers to showing the figure from three perspectives: front, back, and side.</note>, as we have often advised and as we have done in the examples placed below. You shall also provide the front of the body, encompassing it with the outermost lines of the front view.

| | † <br> Rod | ∿ <br> Number | ◯ <br> Portion | ✕ <br> Fragment |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Cleft of the buttocks | 0 | 5 | 8 | 0 |
| And for the heel | 0 | 1 | 6 | 0 |

In measurements of this kind, students of these matters will notice that the oblique widths, which are the thicknesses <note>original: "desitates," likely a variant of *densitates*, referring here to the depth of the body from front to back.</note>, are smaller than the frontal widths through the whole trunk of the body, but are larger through the whole leg—that is, the thigh and the shins. Furthermore, it should be known that it will not be a great task if anyone wishes to investigate the figures described in the previous book by the measurements of the numerical parts in this manner as well. Remember also the variation of the head which I have explained several times; for when the head is positioned differently, the entire appearance of the body appears different.

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the measurements of the male figure discussed previously to the female figure. It follows the same rigorous mathematical system used throughout Albrecht Dürer's work, establishing the "ideal" heights, widths, and depths of the female body through fractional units.</meta>

To match the previously described male body, I will now add the female body in the following sections, the heights of which are as follows.

<note>Dürer uses a specific set of symbols for his measurements: the "rod" ($\oplus$) is the largest unit, followed by the "number" ($n$), the "portion" ($L$), and the "fragment" ($\dot{\overline{\dot{}}}$). This system allowed Renaissance artists to scale a figure to any size while maintaining perfect proportions.</note>

| | $\oplus$ Rod | $n$ Number | $L$ Portion | $\dot{\overline{\dot{}}}$ Fragment |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| From the forehead to the crown of the head | 0 | 0 | 5 | 0 |
| To the forehead | 0 | 1 | 2 | 0 |
| To the bottom of the chin | 0 | 6 | 7 | 0 |
| To the tops of the shoulder blades | 0 | 8 | 2 | 0 |
| To the tops of the shoulders | 1 | 0 | 6 | 1 |
| To the collarbones <note>original: "iugula"</note> | 1 | 1 | 2 | 0 |
| From here to the top of the chest | 0 | 1 | 6 | 0 |
| Below the armpit | 0 | 3 | 2 | 0 |
| Below the armpit from the back | 0 | 3 | 9 | 0 |
| To the nipples | 0 | 4 | 8 | 0 |
| Below the breasts | 0 | 6 | 1 | 0 |
| At the waist <note>original: "in cinctum," referring to the belt-line or narrowest part of the torso.</note> | 1 | 1 | 2 | 0 |
| From here to the navel | 0 | 1 | 3 | 2 |
| To the top of the thigh or hip | 0 | 6 | 0 | 0 |

<note>These numerical fractions are part of Dürer’s "fractional" system. Instead of using a fixed ruler, he divided the total height of the body into fractions (e.g., the head being a certain fraction of the total height) to ensure the proportions remained consistent regardless of how large the final drawing was made.</note>

***

<margin>The drawing depicts a female figure from the back, intersected by a central vertical axis to ensure symmetry. Horizontal lines cross the body at the points identified on the previous page—such as the shoulders, the waist, and the buttocks—to provide exact coordinates for the artist.</margin>

<note>Dürer’s work on proportion was a lifelong project, eventually published in his "Four Books on Human Proportion." He sought to find a mathematical basis for beauty, moving away from medieval "rules of thumb" toward a more scientific, Renaissance approach to the human figure.</note>

<summary>This page presents a posterior diagram of a female figure, utilizing a system of mathematical fractions to define the body's proportions. It represents Albrecht Dürer's rigorous attempt to standardize the human form through geometry and observation.</summary>
