--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general proportional study of the female body to the specific, technical construction of the human head. It introduces the geometric "grid" system Dürer used to map anatomical features in profile.</meta>

<sig>D ij</sig>

At the end of this book, having presented two male heads, I will teach their formation more exquisitely and certainly than I have done before, in the following manner.

First, you shall establish a square, just as we instructed in the previous book; this will serve for an oblique <note>original: "obliquo," here meaning a profile or side-view</note> head. However, the spaces of its sides will be one-seventh part of the total height of the image. You may also make them one-eighth part, provided all other things are preserved similarly to what is prescribed for the seventh part. And so, you will mark the vertical side of the established square with the sign **a** at the front, and **b** at the back. The upper transverse <note>horizontal</note> side will be **c**, and the lower side **d**. Then, you shall divide that seventh part we mentioned into 8 equal parts—these are the numbers which, as you should remember, we indicate with this symbol **ξ**. Then, divide that number into ten portions, indicated by this symbol **⊥**. <note>These symbols represent Dürer’s specific units: ξ (the "measure-part") and ⊥ (the "fractional part" or "tenth").</note> With these, we shall carry out the measurements of the parts of the head, first designating the perpendicular lines in this way:

From line **a**, introduce a perpendicular line at **ξ 0, ⊥ 9**. Let this be line **e**; it should touch the outer edge of the pupil and the innermost part of the nostrils. Afterward, place line **f** from **a** at **ξ 1, ⊥ 8**. This will touch the outer edge of the eyebrows near the temples. The corner of the eye nearest the nose is established in the middle of **e** and **f**. Then place a third perpendicular, and let it be **g**, at **ξ 3, ⊥ 5** back from **a**. This will define the ear near the temples. A fourth line, **h**, placed **ξ 1, ⊥ 0** back from **g**, will describe the outer edge of the ear. After this, place line **i** forward from **b** at **ξ 1, ⊥ 3**. Near this line, the <term>occiput</term> <note>the back of the skull</note> sits upon the neck. Then, extend line **k** from **b** at **ξ 0, ⊥ 7**. This will touch the <term>vertex</term> <note>the highest point of the skull</note> above and the neck below, near the transverse line **d**.

And you shall then place the transverse parallel lines in this order:

Below **c**, let a transverse line **l** be placed at **ξ 2, ⊥ 6**; this will designate the hairline above the forehead. Then divide the space between **c** and **l** with another transverse line **m** through the middle; this will pass near perpendicular **k** through the vertex. Afterward, divide the space between **l** and **d** with two lines, **n** and **o**, into three equal spaces. Line **n** will run through the eyebrows and touch the top of the ear. Line **o** will touch the bottom of the nose and the bottom of the ear. Afterward, divide the space between **o** and **d** with line **p** through the middle; this will touch the top of the chin. Then divide the space between **o** and **p** through the middle with line **q**. Thus, the upper lip will stand between **o** and **q**, and the lower lip will pass between **q** and **p**. Line **q** will also pass through the middle of the cheek, that is, the <term>synarmogen</term> <note>from Greek: the meeting point or "seam" where the lips close</note> of the mouth. Afterward, divide the space between **n** and **o** with line **r** through the middle. In the entire upper space, you shall place the eye. Then, divide the space between **r** and **o** into seven equal spaces, and cut off the bottom three with line **s**. This will touch the highest points of the nostrils. The lowest part of the earlobe will be contained between **f** and **o**.

Then, two oblique lines shall be drawn: one from the angle enclosed by perpendicular line **e** and transverse line **c**, to the angle of perpendicular **a** and transverse **o**. Above this line, the forehead is drawn between transverse lines **l** and **n**. But the nose is drawn below it, between transverse lines **n** and **o**. The other oblique line will descend from the angle of the vertical side **a** and transverse **n**, back to the angle of perpendicular **e** and transverse **d**. By this line, both lips and the edge of the chin will be defined.

Once these are completed, the form of the head shall be expressed. Thus, the top of the head should be rounded toward perpendicular **h** and side **c**. The occiput, however, should be rounded between transverse lines **l** and **n** toward side **b**. The thickness of the sloping neck—that is, from the junction of the occiput and the neck to the throat—shall be **ξ 4, ⊥ 5**. We have taken great care to present all these things to your eyes in the following examples.

But it may happen that such a head appears somewhat larger in some places than the proportions of the proposed image would allow. You may therefore make it smaller in this way: let the <term>sinciput</term> <note>the front/top part of the skull</note> be rounded in the middle of transverse lines **c** and **m** near perpendiculars **g** and **h**. And let the occiput be at **f** in the middle of transverse lines **l** and **n**. Then, near the junction of the occiput, you shall curve the neck at line **o**, at a distance from side **b** of **ξ 1, ⊥ 8**. Then draw it back toward side **d** and perpendicular **i**. I would wish that either...

<summary>This page provides highly detailed, geometric instructions for constructing the human head in profile using a grid and specific numerical units. It demonstrates Dürer's transition from general body proportions to a "coordinate-system" approach for facial features.</summary>
<keywords>head proportions, facial construction, geometry in art, Albrecht Dürer, occiput, vertex, synarmogen, Renaissance anatomy, profile drawing</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for mapping the human head onto a geometric grid. It concludes the second book of the treatise, moving from specific facial measurements to a general rule for measuring other limbs, while offering a disclaimer about the quality of the printed illustrations.</meta>

...so that you may carefully understand the reasoning, for we have not set forth this kind of variation without purpose.
And let this be the description of one head. But for the second, it is as follows.

You shall establish a square in the same way as I advised above, making the vertical sides first **a** (at the front) and then **b** (at the back). But of the transverse <note>horizontal</note> sides, let the upper be **i** and the lower be **k**. You shall first mark this square with perpendicular <note>vertical</note> lines, and place one drawn back from **a** at **ξ 1, ⊥ 0**, which shall be **c**. This will touch the outer edge of the pupil and the innermost part of the nostrils. Again, you shall draw back from **a** at **ξ 2, ⊥ 2** the line **d**; this will define the eyebrows, and above line **k**, the innermost part of the chin. The corners of the eyes near the temples will be terminated in the middle of **c** and **d**. Again, you shall place back from **a** at **ξ 4, ⊥ 0** the line **e**. This will touch the ear where it grows out from the temples, and near that, the side of the sinciput <note>the front or top part of the skull</note> grazes the side **i**. Again, you shall draw line **f** back from **a** at **ξ 5, ⊥ 0**. This will mark the end of the ear. You shall place line **g** forward from side **b** at **ξ 1, ⊥ 0**; this will touch the back of the neck and describe the joint where the occiput <note>the back of the head</note> meets the neck. 

Now, you shall make the transverse parallels as we have said, in this way. From the transverse line **i**, downward at **ξ 0, ⊥ 8**, let another transverse line **l** be placed; this will touch the crown of the head near the perpendicular line **g**. Again, place below **i** the transverse line **m** at **ξ 1, ⊥ 4**; this will touch the root of the hair above the forehead. Afterward, you shall divide the space **m** to **k** with two lines, **n** and **o**, into three equal spaces. Line **n** shall indeed pass through the eyebrows and the top of the ear. But line **o** will touch the bottom of the nose and the ear. And near this, the occiput rests upon the neck. From here, the neck will be thick down to the throat at **ξ 4, ⊥ 6**. Again, you shall draw line **p** below **n** at **ξ 1, ⊥ 0**. Between **p** and **n**, the whole eye will be contained, and the corners will stand in the middle. Again, place line **r** above **o** at **ξ 0, ⊥ 5**; this will touch the highest points of the nostrils, while the bottom of the earlobe will be specifically expressed between **r** and **o**. Again, place line **s** above **k** at **ξ 1, ⊥ 0**; this will touch the top of the chin. Then, divide **o** to **f** by line **t** through the middle; this will pass through the middle of the cheek. 

And so you shall also draw two oblique lines. One from the intersection of perpendicular **c** and transverse **i** to the intersection of perpendicular **a** and transverse **o**. The other, indeed, from the intersection of perpendicular **a** and transverse **n** to the intersection of perpendicular **c** and transverse **f**. Above the first line, the forehead will be drawn between **m** and **n**. Below it, the nose, between **n** and **o**. Along the other line, the lip, cheek, and chin will be expressed. And so, once all parts have been marked with straight lines, you shall then subsequently inscribe the form of the head, just as we have shown in the example placed below. 

Nor should it be ignored that, in a similar way, the measurements of all limbs can be sought out individually from their heights. As an example: let the leg be placed from the middle of the knee to the bottom of the ankle; this will be the height. From this, you will seek out the widths—for instance, you will make the calf one-fourth part of the height in width, and you shall draw half of this fourth through the ankles. Thus, as I have said, it will be permitted at your discretion to do this for individual limbs and to seek out everything exactly.

---

### Conclusion of the Second Book

And let this be the end of the second book of the proportional measurements of straight forms. Here, it has pleased us to ask the readers for pardon for any errors that may by chance occur in the woodcut illustrations. For it was neither fair nor within our power to guarantee against flaws in the engraving <note>original: "celaturae"</note> or the corruption of the wooden types <note>original: "typorum ligneorum," referring to the woodblocks used for printing images</note>. However, those things can still be corrected from the text, which we have taken the utmost care and diligence to publish as correctly as possible, fixing the mistakes of the printers during re-reading. A good and fair reader will, I hope, find our work pleasing and acceptable; as for the malicious and the detractors, as the Greeks say, Hippoclides does not care. <note>original: "Hippoclides," referring to the Greek proverb "οὐ φροντὶς Ἱπποκλείδῃ" (Hippoclides doesn't care), used to express total indifference to the opinions or criticisms of others.</note>

<summary>This page concludes Book II of the treatise, providing final instructions for the geometric construction of a profile head and a general method for measuring limbs like the leg. The author closes by acknowledging that while the text is carefully edited, the woodcut illustrations may contain technical printing flaws.</summary>
<keywords>head proportions, geometry, facial anatomy, sinciput, occiput, grid system, leg measurements, printing errors, woodblocks, Hippoclides, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page serves as the concluding material for the volume, featuring a formal apology for printing errors (errata), a table of corrections, and the colophon indicating the publication details in Nuremberg, 1532, following the diagrams of proportions on the previous pages.</meta>

I ask you who read these words not to be offended if errors have been admitted anywhere during the printing, and especially that you might correct them using the list of corrections provided below. This pardon must be sought from you because this work was an unfamiliar task for the engraver <term>chalcographo</term> <note>original: "chalcographo", referring to the copperplate engraver or printer</note>, and also because the translator’s mind was distracted by such a wide variety of thoughts, especially since neither the former nor the latter had a partner to share in the labor. For, as Diomedes' sentiment is found in Homer:

> When two together join their works and hands,
> One might helpfully anticipate the other’s
> Many-sided counsel; but the heart and mind
> Of a single man is slower, and the sharp edge of the mind is weak and improvident.

From the middle of the knee to above it, let it be 1. 21. Below, however, one .40. And so the knee concludes. In the middle, both what is above and what is below. At its lowest point on the outside, let there be 2 19. On the inside, one 8. | E iij, side 2, column 1, line 5: read **below it** |

## Of the most Renowned Painter and <term>Geometer</term><gloss>a mathematician specializing in the study of shapes and proportions</gloss>
# Albrecht Dürer’s two books on the variety of
## figures, the bending of limbs, and the 
## gestures of images; which to those [books] 
## on <term>symmetry</term><gloss>the mathematical relationship and proportion of body parts</gloss> formerly 
## published, have now for the first time 
## been translated into Latin 
## and added.

### In the Year 1534.

<note>original Latin: "Clariſſ. Pictoris et Geometrae Alberti Dureri de varietate figurarum et flexuris partium ac geſtib. imaginum libri duo..." This title announces the first Latin translation of Dürer's "De Varietate Figurarum," which was originally written in German. The translation was completed by the scholar Joachim Camerarius to make Dürer's scientific approach to art accessible to the "Republic of Letters"—the international community of European intellectuals who used Latin as their shared language.</note>

<note>The mention of "formerly published" books on symmetry refers to the first two books of Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion," which had been published in Latin in 1532. This 1534 edition completes the set for Latin readers.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for the 1534 Latin translation of Albrecht Dürer's treatise on the movement, diverse types, and posing of the human figure in art. It marks a significant moment in the spread of Renaissance artistic theory from Germany to the rest of Europe.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometry, Latin translation, 1534, Renaissance art theory, Joachim Camerarius, anatomy for artists</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page serves as the translator's preface to the 1534 edition of Albrecht Dürer's work. It follows the title page and provides historical context regarding the translation process and Dürer’s death. The translator, the scholar Joachim Camerarius, explains his struggle to render Dürer's technical German into scholarly Latin.</meta>

### Preface to the readers for the following books.

When I first took into my hands the books composed by **Albrecht Dürer** on the <term>art of painting</term><gloss>original Latin: "artificio picturæ," referring to the scientific and technical principles of art</gloss> to be translated into the Latin language, I was led partly by my own goodwill toward him, and partly compelled by the requests of friends which seemed to carry the weight of a command. I soon felt that the success of the outcome did not quite match my enthusiasm, nor did it satisfy that singular desire I had to please both myself and those who had imposed this labor upon us. Nor did this seem so strange to me upon reflection, for I had undertaken a task without fully weighing the burden, a subject for which my strengths were scarcely sufficient. Whether it was in my eagerness to gratify those who asked for this work, or through my own initial negligence, this difficulty had escaped me at the start. Therefore, I felt a greater anxiety as the work progressed, both because the weight of it was unforeseen and because it was no longer possible to cast aside the burden once it had been taken up. Had I been permitted to do so, it would have been an easy path by which I could have freed myself from care and trouble. In this hesitation and low spirit, as is usually the case, we moved forward at a slower pace, and as if step by step. 

Therefore, we published the first two books separately as they were completed, partly to provide a sample of our industry to the readers, and partly to rest for a little while after covering that much of the distance. At the same time, we did this because the remaining books required greater attention; for we knew that the first book had been diligently revised by the author and printed while he was still alive, whereas he was snatched away by death before the edition and correction of the remaining parts could be finished. And would that it had seemed right to God that this task had been given to us while he was still living! We would have carried it out both eagerly and, as I trust, with praise. Now, because of the longing for him, there has been both a certain weariness, and the opportunity for praise was surely removed by his death. 

In wishing for this, I do not only look to myself and my own affairs, but rather also to him and the reputation of the deceased, by whom I felt myself greatly loved, and whom I held in the highest esteem for his own merit. I also make these wishes for the sake of those who are students of these matters. For these writings would surely exist as published by the author himself—not just a small part of them, but the whole—composed in such a way that they would be a source of great admiration to the learned and of profit to all students. Now, even though the former will not be able to think little of these works, nor the latter be helped only a little, yet that excellence of care and refinement might perhaps be found wanting, than which nothing is more beautiful or more excellent. 

The friends of our Dürer also saw this; after the printing of the work had already begun and the first book was finished, when the author passed away, they brought this labor to a conclusion with a careful warning that the author was not present for the final edition. I also thought it necessary to signal this to the readers in this place—where, as I said, I have rested a little as if the burden were set down—so they might know what was only drafted by Dürer and what they possess as fully polished. Regarding ourselves and our work, we shall refrain from speaking for the present. Since we affect no praise, I do not think it is to be feared that anyone will come forward to criticize us. For just as that boy in **Virgil** <note>This refers to Ascanius in Virgil’s Aeneid (Book 4, lines 158–159), who, while hunting, finds small prey boring and longs for a real challenge.</note>, born to the greatest hope, pays no heed to harmless beasts:

->And among the sluggish herds, he prays that a foaming boar<-
->might be given to his vows, or that a tawny lion might<-
->descend from the mountain.<-
<note>original Latin: "Spumantemque dari pecora inter inertia votis / Optat aprum, aut fulvum descendere monte leonem."</note>

So do men of great learning gladly pass over writings of this kind, and they easily allow the works of <term>Aristarchus</term><gloss>a reference to Aristarchus of Samothrace, a famous ancient critic, used here to represent harsh literary critics</gloss> to remain hidden, from whom they perceive that their name will not be celebrated but rather obscured. What is there in this genre of writing—as far as the style is concerned—that is not only meager, but I might almost say unfortunate? They seem worthy of pity if they have been forced into this by chance, and they certainly cannot be subject to envy. But I have mentioned these things at length...

<summary>In this preface, translator Joachim Camerarius discusses the difficulties of translating Dürer's technical work after the artist's death. He explains that while Dürer oversaw the first book's publication, the subsequent books were completed by friends and the translator, lacking the author's final polish.</summary>
<keywords>Joachim Camerarius, Albrecht Dürer, preface, Latin translation, Virgil, Aristarchus, posthumous publication, art theory, technical writing</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Joachim Camerarius to the Latin edition of Albrecht Dürer’s theoretical works. It follows the translator's initial remarks about the difficulty of the task and now addresses the reader directly regarding the value of Dürer's contributions and the risks of unauthorized, inferior reproductions.</meta>

...[more] than was necessary. As for what I said regarding the wretched state of these writings, which receive a translator who is plainly a slave to such a subject and such a fortune, if anyone thinks this is less than true, let them try the experiment themselves for a few lines; they will surely understand that I have spoken much more sparingly than the reality of the matter warrants. 

As for the rest, if you have perhaps read those earlier parts, see that you also come to know these. They are certainly prepared with the highest skill, for which reason, if anything is lacking in the composition, you should easily allow it to be compensated for <note>Camerarius is asking for the reader's indulgence if his Latin style is not perfectly elegant, given the complexity of the technical subject matter.</note>. And, if until this time you have seen nothing better published on these matters, it would be fair to embrace the best works with grateful hearts—and I am certain that you have seen nothing [better]. For just as I believe some exceptional precepts of the ancients in this art still remain and have not been utterly destroyed, so also I think they are preserved with the greatest care, more than any maiden whom the poet—that master of morals—commands to be guarded in many-walled chambers <note>This is a classical allusion, likely to the myth of Danae or a general reference to the Roman poet Horace's views on morality and protection.</note>. 

If ever something of that ancient art should come forth, God willing, it will—whatever it may be—leave even **Dürer’s** <term>works</term><gloss>original: "Dureriana"</gloss> in no small esteem; then, however, you may by right defer to venerable antiquity. In the meantime, since we have found that nothing exists that surpasses **Dürer’s** work, you will have done your duty if, embracing these as the best, you do not cease to both seek and hope for better things. These things he wished to be brought forward even at the cost of overshadowing his own works after his death. For although he easily excelled others in many various sciences, yet in that man, even this knowledge was surpassed by his integrity of mind, sincerity, generosity, moderation, and holiness. Although we have spoken of these things elsewhere—much less and much more meagerly than was fitting given the abundance and magnitude of the subject—we have decided not to speak further of them now. 

Furthermore, I think the learned will not be able to despise this teaching, since it is not only refined by art but also pleasant in practice—not merely for labor and hands, but rather for understanding and judgment. For who is so unrefined or so devoid of any sense of elegance that they do not habitually delight in contemplating a good and beautiful <term>painting</term><gloss>original: "pictura"</gloss>? 

But there is a huge difference between spectators. For those unskilled in the art know nothing either precisely or with any certainty, but are captivated only by the colors and brilliance of the panels—the more refined among them also by the subject matter. But the learned examine even the tiny details and everything individually, and through their judgment, they discern and perceive. They are like those who greet individuals by name, while the others are like those who salute a great crowd of people all at once. 

But since I think that as soon as this work is released, there will be some who will rush upon it, as it were, and drag it under their own presses <note>Camerarius is warning against "pirate" editions or unauthorized copies that were common in the early days of printing.</note>, how much it concerns students to receive it from here rather than elsewhere—where it might be expressed according to our authorized copies—is not so easy to say as it is declared by the reality itself. For since almost greater clarity and certainty consist in the examples [illustrations] than in the precepts [text], and it is impossible that these should not be violated by frequent reproductions—whether through the <term>types</term><gloss>original: "typis", referring here to the woodblocks or plates used for printing diagrams</gloss> themselves being corrupted or carelessly explained—even if I were to grant the greatest skill and knowledge to those who wish to transfer them, who does not see how easily errors hang over us? 

We have found that even from the very same <term>types</term><gloss>woodblocks</gloss>, different figures have come out when printed again. The writings of other authors are usually corrupted in the text alone, but in this genre, they are also corrupted in the drawings. In this category also belong those <term>cosmographical</term><gloss>original: "cosmographica", referring to maps or descriptions of the universe</gloss> phenomena and <term>Geographic</term> works. But let them see to this themselves, lest by lingering on this point we seem to care more for the sale of books than for the benefit of the readers. And so, from here on, we shall proceed to set forth the teaching of **Albrecht** <note>Albrecht Dürer.</note>.

<summary>Joachim Camerarius defends the quality of Dürer's work against ancient standards and warns readers against unauthorized, lower-quality printed editions that might distort Dürer's precise diagrams.</summary>
<keywords>Joachim Camerarius, Albrecht Dürer, printing quality, piracy, art theory, technical diagrams, antiquity, painter's integrity</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Joachim Camerarius’s introductory preface to the actual technical text of Albrecht Dürer’s third book on human proportion. The focus shifts from defending the publication to the systematic explanation of how an artist can vary the human form through geometric and proportional changes.</meta>

# By Albrecht Dürer, mathematician and painter.
## Two books on the variety of figures.

In this book, which is the third we have established on the art of painting, our purpose is to demonstrate certain methods by which the figures of the body described previously can be varied according to the individual judgment of each artist, following their own proportions and the relationship of the limbs to one another. Whether a larger or perhaps a more slender work is pleasing to someone, it can be done in such a way that everything appears different and varied from what came before, seemingly retaining neither the former shape nor the original measurement. We have decided to explain these principles similarly for the images of men and women, as well as children.

In the beginning, it must be known and understood from what things the variations of figures usually arise. One category of these is the contrast in measurements. For when opposites are joined, they become more distinct, which does not happen in similar things. To this category, the following are subject:

| Large. | Small. |
|---------|---------|
| Long or extended | Short or brief. |
| Stretched or dilated | Contracted. |
| Thick | Slender. |

These are indeed differences of quantity, and they consist in adding or subtracting, in the "much" and the "little."

The second is the category of quality, in which there are differences of age, such as youth and old age; of the constitution and manner of the body, such as lean, fat, hard, or smooth; of form, such as beautiful and lovely, or ugly and unpleasant; and likewise of habit <note>Here, "habit" (Latin: *habitus*) refers to the characteristic posture, bearing, or physical state of the body.</note>, such as curved, straight, equal, unequal, sublime or high, humble or low, round, angular, sharp, blunt; and similarly upright, transverse, oblique; also position, from which come right, left, front, back, above, and below.

Through these distinctions, the diversities of figures can be sought out. Of these, we shall now provide examples in which the principal and almost only teaching of these matters is contained. First, let the difference of large and small be proposed. This must be considered in two ways. First, insofar as it relates to the whole compared to the whole, where one is called large and the other small, yet in which a similarity and almost the same appearance can remain. Thus, in those things where the same ratio and harmony of parts is placed, they can be described as large or small, as we see happening in sketches. For they want even royal palaces to be built from a form sketched on paper; likewise, no one fails to say and feel that a sphere of a longer diameter has a similarity with another whose diameter is much shorter. But when this difference is noticed in the parts, then a dissimilarity of the whole to the whole follows. For example, if someone makes the trunk of the body rather large, but the lower parts quite small. This very thing can be applied to all the limbs, and these variations are infinite. We use these terms of "large" and "small" with respect to those things between which they are placed.

Next was the proposed difference of length and shortness. If, therefore, someone wishes to extend or shorten a certain figure—for example, that which was described by us as the male figure in the first book and first place—he will observe slenderness and contraction in length, but thickness and dilation in shortness, according to the proper com- <note>The text breaks off mid-word (com-), likely continuing as "commensurationem" or "proportion" on the following page.</note>

<summary>Dürer introduces the third book of his treatise, moving from fixed proportions to the principles of variation, categorizing differences into quantity (size, length, thickness) and quality (age, form, posture).</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, artistic variation, quantity vs quality, anatomy in art, Renaissance art theory, scaling</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions from Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion" (specifically Book III). Having introduced the concept of variation in the previous section, Dürer now moves into the geometric mechanics of how to alter a figure's height, width, and thickness using a specific triangular tool he calls the "Variator."</meta>

measurement. And when both of these are done in the ratio of width and thickness, the spaces marked in the primary figure must be retained. If, however, both that elongated figure and this shortened one are adjusted to the height of the original image, then those spaces of width and thickness will exist as very different things according to the constitution of each. But the height spaces, though now matching <note>meaning they have been scaled to fit the original total height</note>, will show the greatest dissimilarity, because there has been a departure from the original figure—toward tallness on one hand and toward shortness on the other. Thus, all parts of the former <note>the tall figure scaled down</note> will have become more slender and contracted, while those of the latter <note>the short figure scaled up</note> will have become thicker and more distended.

And in this way, the heights of individual parts in the body can be appropriately lengthened or shortened within the marked transverse lines that designate the heights of the parts. If you make larger intervals between these lines, the figure will come out longer; if smaller, it will come out shorter. By these, as has been said, the ratio of thickness and width is not varied. But that elongated one and this shortened one are distinguished from one another. Afterwards, in a new measurement, they will depart far from the original description. 

To search for this same thing in detail through every single part while lengthening and shortening would involve too much labor and excessive boredom. Therefore, we have condensed this whole certain teaching into this method, by which those things I mentioned may be performed correctly and easily through the service of a single triangle. You shall describe it thus:

Mark one line with two points, according to which the other two of length and brevity may be described. Let this be **a b**, of the same height as the first male figure was in the first book. You will divide this by height using the spaces of the transverse lines. For you know that the heights of the parts are shown by those lines. From points **a** and **b** of this line, draw two lines to another proposed point **c** on the right, which, meeting at that point, describe a triangle from base **a b**. And similarly, meeting at the same point **c**, the lines for the heights of all the parts will be drawn out. 

Afterwards, next to this middle line, you will place one <term>parallel (παράλληλος)</term> <note>original: "παράλληλος"</note> line for the elongation, **f g**, to the left, and another for the shortening, **d e**, to the right toward point **c**. Moreover, the further you place either this one or that one from the middle, the greater the difference in elongation or shortening will be. Therefore, through these lines themselves, those passing lines of the middle height will divide them appropriately according to the ratio of each, just as the matter requires. 

We have placed an illustration of this triangle below, drawn by our own hand. It will be useful for many and varied purposes. And let its name be the **Variator** <note>original: "varianti"</note>. For with the help of this, the parts of things are varied, and wonderful changes are produced, either by increasing or decreasing the statures. For example, if one wished to produce a proposed figure whose height encompasses spaces that would be seven heads high, to a height of eight, nine, or ten head spaces—by adding to the height between the top of the head and the bottom of the foot the spaces of one, two, or three heads. The same must be understood for diminishing the stature. 

But if you perhaps prolong the stature as we said, you will then also adjust the somewhat elongated head according to what the form of the proposed image requires, so that consistency and beauty <note>Latin: *decor*—referring to the aesthetic fitness or appropriateness of the proportions</note> are preserved; the same will be observed in shortening, according to the nature of the thing. But then if you wish to measure it, the height will not have the same number of head-spaces as before. At this point, I advise those wishing to use this method of change demonstrated by us, that they should be willing to engage with the hands and feet separately described by us above with great diligence and attention, as it is in no way an easy matter.

->The form of the Variator follows.<-

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric construction of the "Variator," a triangular tool used to scale human proportions up or down while maintaining or intentionally distorting ratios. He emphasizes that while the tool simplifies the math, the artist must still use judgment to ensure the final figure remains aesthetically pleasing.</summary>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how to manipulate the proportions of a figure using a geometric grid and a physical drafting tool involving a silk thread. It follows the previous page’s introduction to the basic lines of perspective.</meta>

So then, with all these lines distinguished as taught, the form of the image will next be inscribed in its own place. We were unable to do this in the previously described examples of lines due to the smallness of the paper <note>original: "ob cartæ paruitatem"; a common complaint in early printing where space was limited</note>. However, we have done so through equal and congruent spaces of height in the following sections, preserving the due proportions <note>original: "commensurationibus"</note> of each individual part, following the method and reasoning that will be explained. This method may be used by those wishing to make any variety of changes. 

A figure formed according to line **f.g.** will be more slender and contracted, while one formed toward line **d.e.** will be thicker and more spread out. This variation is no less useful for these modes of slenderness and thickness, or contraction and extension, than it is for modes of length and shortness. It is not out of place or inconsistent to describe all images in the examples as being of nearly equal height—that is, so that the height of one occupies as much of the paper as the other—even though their actual statures may be quite different. In this way, everything will be clearer and more evident for the sake of comparison. By this same reasoning, one can also use these methods on separate parts of the images, such that some parts vary while others do not. In that case, whatever is taken away from one must necessarily be added to another. The same is to be understood for each of the transverse lines in the height of the image, and according to these, the methods of thinning or thickening, as well as contraction or extension. For whenever you have established two other lines on either side of a single proposed principal line, the appropriate change is revealed in all places: on one side for length, and on the other for shortness. 

But now I shall show the manner in which anyone can furnish his workshop <note>original: "officinam"; the artist's studio or workspace</note> with this triangle and use it if the situation requires.

First, draw a transverse line <note>a horizontal baseline</note> from point **a.** to point **b.**, which should be fixed in a certain place. From point **a.**, another line **a.c.** is extended; this should be fixed at point **a.** but movable from the other point **c.**, so that while it is joined to the transverse line **a.b.** in the same place, it can be moved closer or further away at point **c.** Let no one suspect that I am speaking of lines and points in the way Geometers <note>original: "Geometræ"; referring to theoretical mathematicians</note> are accustomed to doing, for whom all these things are proposed to be understood only in the mind. We are now providing for the works of the hands; but nevertheless, although we want all these things to be expressed physically, they must be referred back to the exact and invariable certainty of those things which are understood by the mind alone as indivisible and unique. 

The use of what we have described will be as follows: On a small board prepared for this purpose, you will mark the transverse line **a.b.**, and at point **a.** you will firmly fix a needle or a small nail <term>clauiculum</term> <note>a small pin used as a pivot point for drafting</note>. To this, you will attach an even and somewhat strong thread, such as silk <term>sericum</term>, and stretch it to the other point **c.** to represent that movable line. After this, place a perpendicular line **d.e.** in the middle of the transverse line **a.b.** 

With these things prepared in this manner, know that the proposed part which you wish to extend or shorten is to be placed on the perpendicular line **d.e.**, so that it ends resting upon the transverse line **a.b.** Afterwards, stretch the thread (which serves as line **a.c.**) so that it touches the top of the established part on line **d.e.** Then, you may make that part as much longer as you wish. 

This is the method: In front of line **d.e.**, you will draw a line of length <note>original: "prolixitatis"</note>, which we call **f.g.**, similarly perpendicular, which is defined above by the extended thread of line **a.c.** and below by the transverse line **a.b.** This, for example's sake, is **f.g.** If, however, you wish to make the proposed part shorter, you will achieve this by the line of shortness <note>original: "breuitatis"</note>, which is also placed perpendicularly on the transverse line **a.b.**, but behind the perpendicular **d.e.** toward point **a.**, and is defined above by the thread of line **a.c.** Let this be, for example, **h.i.**

<summary>This page describes a practical drafting tool using a fixed pin and a silk thread to mathematically adjust the proportions, thickness, and length of figures in a drawing.</summary>
<keywords>drafting tools, silk thread, geometric proportion, artist's workshop, lengthening, shortening, perpendicular lines, Renaissance geometry</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of how to scale human proportions using a geometric device. The previous page discussed the theory of shifting proportions; this page introduces the specific physical tool used for these calculations.</meta>

In this way, with three perpendicular lines placed in order upon a horizontal base line, the space of the proposed and principal length will be established in the middle line **d.e.** In the closer line **f.g.**, that same length is extended <note>original: "productum"</note>; in the further line **h.i.**, it is shortened <note>original: "correptum"</note>. And the line of the thread **a.c.** designates both of these proportionately when it is stretched to the top of line **e.d.** The use of these can be applied to innumerable things, even where the specific names of the spaces cannot be given—for instance, if it is not possible to name exactly what fraction a part is of a known length, such as one half, one third, one fifth, or one sixth.

A very clear example of this rule is shown below, so that our teaching might be placed before the eyes of the readers. We have decided to name this instrument the **Chooser** <note>original: "Deligenti"; likely a Latinization of a term related to choosing or selecting proportions</note>, because it serves the selection of those parts which one might wish to make larger or smaller in any given subject. Through these rules and with the help of this **Chooser**, the male figures of slenderness and thickness shown shortly hereafter were drawn.

In these variations, it must be noted that all bodies, when becoming larger through extension, thickening, or widening, have parts that are further apart and, as it were, more expansive. Conversely, those that are shortened have parts that are more gathered and close together. Thus, slender statures appear softer in appearance than thicker ones. This instrument clearly displays the shape and function of a **quadrant** <term>quadrant: a mathematical instrument shaped like a quarter-circle, used for measuring angles or proportions</term>, which we have also indicated with curved lines.

---

### The "Chooser" Figure.

<vocab>Figure of the Chooser, perpendicular lines, of length, extended, shortened, by extension, by thickening, by widening, of a quadrant</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the 'Deligens' or 'Chooser,' a quadrant-like tool designed by Dürer to mathematically scale human figures into more slender or thicker proportions. It explains how a taut thread and three vertical scales allow an artist to systematically adjust lengths without needing complex fractional labels.</summary>
<keywords>Deligens, scaling instrument, quadrant, human proportion, mathematical art, Albrecht Dürer, geometry in art</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the "Chooser" instrument introduced previously, moving from the abstract theory of the tool to its practical application in drawing human figures of different scales—specifically how to maintain grace and anatomical realism when stretching or compressing proportions.</meta>

But regarding the taller statures <note>original: "productioribus staturis"</note> of the following images, it should be known that one part out of four <note>likely referring to a specific unit of the scale used in the diagram</note> has been added to their height. Even if one wishes to use this method, he must adjust the head a bit more precisely; if this is neglected, the whole figure will appear less graceful <note>original: "inuenustior"</note>. Therefore, by raising the chin upwards, or by taking a little bit off the top of the head, the parts will be measured out proportionally through the benefit of the **Chooser** <term>Chooser: the Latin "Deligens," a name Dürer gives to his proportion-calculating instrument</term>. In our examples, we have taken nothing off the top, but instead made the space from there to the very bottom of the chin two 15ths <note>original: "duas 15."</note>, and from the top of the chin to the top of the forehead, one 10th <note>original: "vnam. 10."</note>. Once all the parts of the height have been distributed by the benefit of the **Chooser**, the width <note>original: "latitudo"</note> and thickness <note>original: "densitas"</note> already introduced are retained. We have also placed examples of these heads next to the figures in the illustrations.

I have made the space of the foot occupy two 13ths <note>original: "duas. 13."</note>. For it had turned out slightly shorter than was right; likewise, we flattened the arch <note>original: "monticulū," literally "little mountain"</note> of the foot somewhat. The beauty of the figure also required that the legs, which the method of variation had suggested, should now be described as even more slender and slightly more contracted. Furthermore, the measured spaces for the thickness of this image have one 5th <note>original: "parte vna. 5."</note> less than the spaces of the principal image. I have delineated how I believe all these things can be done correctly, in every part and place, with a diligent and studious hand in the examples, so that I might instruct readers not only with words, but by the thing itself.

Regarding the shortened <note>original: "correptiore"</note> figure, it seemed necessary to give this warning: In the description of the **Variant** <note>original: "Variantis," referring to the variable scale</note>, along the established line of the principal height **a. b.**, I drew two oblique lines which cross each other in the middle of line **a. b.** Therefore, from the extension produced from the line of length **f. g.** above and below, they touch lines **c. f.** and **c. g.**, and from these points of contact the line of shortening **d. e.** is drawn. If a figure of this shortening is fashioned according to the principal height, it will become quite thick, as is achieved by this **Chooser** instrument. However, the height, width, and thickness of the head must be reduced if we wish it to correspond to the human measure. The space of the foot, meanwhile, will occupy one 6th <note>original: "vnam. 6."</note>. We have placed examples of these below, from which, following our teaching, anyone can do as they please, whether taller or shorter figures are preferred.

It emphasizes that simply scaling a figure requires subtle manual corrections to prevent the proportions from looking unnatural or ungraceful.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of human proportions. While the previous page listed the specific anatomical landmarks for a standard figure, this page moves into the geometric theory of how to modify those proportions—making a figure taller (elongated) or shorter (compressed) by manipulating the "cube" in which the head is inscribed.</meta>

## On the difference between elongation and shortness

It has seemed appropriate now to also treat the "head" described above in the first book, which was inscribed in a four-sided cube and divided by transverse <note>horizontal</note> and perpendicular <note>vertical</note> lines. That is, if that head is understood to be within a cube, we must explain by what method it can be extended—made taller by "stretching"—and how one can determine what is lost in the spaces of width when height is added, and conversely, what is gained in width when height is removed. These are commonly called <term>solid figures</term> <note>original: "στερεὰ σχήματα"</note>. For the sake of teaching, we distinguish between the "oblong" <note>tall</note> and the "prominent" <note>wide</note>. Let the teaching concerning these be as follows. Let a square cube be proposed; that is, conceive in your mind an equilateral four-sided solid, for the name "cube" properly belongs to this. But let its height be the space of the height of the head described above in the first book, which we call the "principal head." However, a few things must be explained regarding the cube itself separately before we arrive at the description of the head. Let it be left to the judgment of each person how much they wish to raise or lower it, or how much they wish to extend it.

<margin>
It should be known that every <term>hexahedron</term> <note>original: "εξάεδρον," a six-sided solid</note>, that is, a four-angled solid, is called a "cube" by the author.
</margin>

At the start, on the front line of the side of the proposed straight cube, you will mark the bottom with the letter **.a.** and the top with **.b.** You shall raise this very line as much as you please, and you will mark this newly made height with the notes **.a.c.** Afterward, you will place the line **.a.c.** horizontally, and upon it, you will place **.a.b.** perpendicularly, so that a "square" <note>a right angle</note> is described by this arrangement of lines, containing the angle **.a.** Soon, from point **.a.**, you will extend the line **.a.c.** backward; let the terminal point of this extension be **.d.** Thus, the perpendicular **.a.b.** placed upon the transverse **.d.a.c.** will appear at right angles on both sides. Then, you shall close the triangle above with an oblique <note>diagonal</note> line. Afterward, take a square <note>original: "gnomon," a carpenter's or geometer's set-square</note>—whether of brass or wood, provided it is not deceptive—and fit one side of it to the oblique line **.b.c.** so that the corner of the square touches point **.b.** Then, draw a normal <note>perpendicular</note> line from the corner of the square; where you see it intersect the transverse line **.d.a.c.** at a right angle, as I have said, place the mark **.e.** Once these things are perfected, know that you have now found the fitting—that is, the <term>proportional</term> <note>original: "ανάλογον"</note>—line to line **.a.b.** and line **.a.c.** That is line **.a.e.** But next, the line between **.a.b.** and **.a.e.** must be sought in this manner: Join both lines **.a.b.** and **.a.e.** so that they appear to describe one transverse line; let that be **.e.a.b.** Then, set one line perpendicularly at point **.a.**, fix the foot of a compass in the middle of line **.e.a.b.**, place the other foot at point **.e.**, and swing it around to point **.b.** so that a semicircle is described. Where the circumference of this circle cuts the line dropped perpendicularly at point **.a.**, place the mark **.f.** This will be the space for the sides of the width of a cube that has exceeded the principal height, which excess is designated by line **.a.c.**

You will do similarly if you are about to compress the principal cube so that it becomes somewhat wide <note>original: "prominentiusculus"</note>, except that everything proceeds differently from the previous steps. For just as the height of the cube was made taller above, it will now be made lower, so that **.a.b.** now describes the line of "shortening" <note>decurtationis</note>. But you will find the width in this way: Let the side **.a.b.** be fitted normally <note>at a right angle</note> to the transverse line of shortening **.a.c.** After that, beyond point **.a.**, let line **.a.** be drawn backward, which extension ends at point **.d.** Soon, let the oblique line **.b.c.** also be drawn. And finally, apply one side of the square to this same oblique line **.b.c.** so that the right angle touches point **.b.** From there, draw a normal line to the transverse line **.d.a.c.** Where you see it intersect this, there set point **.e.** Thus, the first line of correspondence will be found.

<summary>This page explains the geometric method for altering the proportions of a "principal head" by inscribing it in a cube and using a set-square and compass to calculate how width must change when height is increased or decreased.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, human proportion, cube, gnomon, elongation, compression, Albrecht Dürer, mathematical perspective, scaling</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric instructions for modifying the proportions of a human head. Having previously established the "principal head" within a standard cube, the author now demonstrates how to use a compass and ruler to find the exact dimensions for a "compressed" (wider and shorter) version of that head, ensuring the mathematical relationship between height and width remains consistent.</meta>

...between the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.**, and let that be **a. e.** But you shall seek the other line of agreement <note>or proportion</note> in this way: Join the two lines **a. b.** and **a. e.** so that they appear to form one transverse <note>horizontal</note> line **e. a. b.** After this, drop a perpendicular <note>vertical</note> line onto point **a.**, and fix one foot of a compass in the middle of line **e. a. b.** With the other foot, describe a semicircle starting from point **e.** And where you see the circumference of this semicircle cross the perpendicular line placed at point **a.**, mark that spot with the letter **f.** This very line will be the length of the prominence <note>the width</note> of the compressed cube, whose proposed height was line **a. c.** The figures for all these things individually, as well as the drawings of all parts of the head expressed within their own cubes, are placed below.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It should be known that in depicting the <term>front view</term> <note>original: "adversa facie," the face seen from the front</note> of the head, one-sixth <note>represented by the symbol ϛ</note> of its width must be removed, just as the system of <term>proportional measurement</term> <note>original: "commensuratio"</note> requires. You should certainly do it in this way: set out the width of the prominent and compressed cube eight times, and divide that total distance into ten equal spaces. One of these ten spaces will then be the width of the front-facing head. To this width, the <term>squareness</term> <note>original: "quadratura"</note> of the cube—from which the head was made elongated or prominent—will be contracted. And so you will distribute the lines of the form with the help of a diligent hand. You shall follow this same method through the variations across the entire body.

<summary>This page explains a geometric method using a semicircle and perpendicular lines to calculate the width of a 'compressed' head relative to its height. It also provides a specific ratio (8:10) for narrowing the head when drawing it from a front-facing perspective.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, head proportions, compressed cube, front view, commensuration, Albrecht Dürer, mathematical drawing, anatomical scaling</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric instructions for constructing the bounding boxes of the human head. Following the previous discussion on the "compressed" cube (wider and shorter), the author now presents the "principal" (standard) cube and the "oblong" (tall) cube, along with the geometric constructions used to derive their proportions.</meta>

# The standard and primary cube.

<margin>
**Line of extension a.c.**
<note>original: "Linea prolixitatis." This refers to the line used to increase a dimension.</note>
c
|
|

There are also other variations of the cube <note>original: "cubi variationes". Dürer treats the human head as if it were contained within a geometric box; by altering the shape of this box, he can systematically change the appearance of the face.</note>, from which a diversity in the faces of heads arises in this manner. First, if you establish the upper surface of the cube as square and widened <note>original: "dilatatam"</note>, and then at the bottom you subtract as much from the square surface as the upper part was widened. And this you can also afterwards invert, so that as much as the cube is wider at the bottom, it is narrower at the top <note>original: "angustior"</note>. 

Once these proportions are established, the lines of the facial parts will then be drawn in with the help of a diligent hand <note>original: "auxilio Diligentis". This likely refers to the skill of the artist in placing features like eyes and noses within the newly distorted grid.</note>, and subsequently the harmony of the form will follow. Thus, one face of a head will emerge wider at the top and narrower at the bottom. Another, however, will be narrower at the top and wider at the bottom. And the use of this same principle <note>original: "rationis", meaning the mathematical method or logic</note> may be applied throughout the whole body. 

Just as we have said that varying the surface of the cube in two ways—at the top and the bottom—creates different faces, so also the changed surface of the right and left sides yields different faces. For if the front surface—that is, the one to the right—is widened, and the back—that is, the one to the left—is contracted, and likewise conversely, if this one is contracted and that one widened, the facial parts...

<summary>Dürer explains how to create different facial types by tapering the geometric "head-box" (the cube) to be wider or narrower at the top, bottom, or sides.</summary>
<keywords>cubic variations, facial types, facial proportion, tapering, geometric distortion, Renaissance art theory, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Albrecht Dürer's "head-box" method. ...ple, the lines, and likewise the forms, may be congruently inscribed with the help of the <term>Diligent</term> <note>original: "diligentis". In Dürer's system, the "Diligent" or "Variator" refers to a specific coordinate-transfer method used to shift points from a standard grid to a distorted one.</note> method. In the first method, the face will be wider at the front (or from the forehead) and narrower at the back (from the rear); while in the second method, the face will be narrower at the front and wider at the back. All of which you may see in the examples provided below. But by this reasoning, you can either diminish from above or cut away as if <note>original: "recidere". Literally "to prune" or "cut back," referring here to tapering the geometric container of the head.</note> squaring the cube from below, just as we have depicted in the examples.

There is also another method if you make a <term>cubic rhombus</term> <note>original: "rhombum cubicum". A rhombus is a diamond shape; here, Dürer is describing a cube that has been pushed over or "skewed" so its faces are no longer right angles.</note> with two opposite acute angles and the other two opposite obtuse angles. This can be used in two ways. First, so that the acute angles are placed at the forehead and the nape of the neck <note>original: "ceruicem"</note>, with the back part of the head and the chin receiving the obtuse angles. Second, if this is inverted. Of all these things you have examples below. But the first face shows a sharp forehead, while the other shows a head sharp at its rear part. And these faces differ entirely from the primary model. Whatever has been described in the cube, all of it is changed when that cube is varied, provided that the appropriate distribution of parts is maintained through the <term>Diligent</term> or <term>Variate</term> <note>original: "Variantem". This is the mathematical process of shifting the proportions while keeping the features in the same relative "cells" of the grid.</note> method.

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page follows the visual demonstrations of distorted heads. It provides the theoretical explanation for how the artist can manipulate the internal grid lines—not just the outer bounding box—to create variety in human features.</meta>

The variations of the heads placed above were all derived from the variation of the cube itself, changed from the principal face, with all parts expressed appropriately through the service of careful attention. In the following parts, however, we will also demonstrate other variations within the cube of the principal head itself. This cube indeed remains the same and equal, but the positioning of its parts is made different by moving the **transverse lines** <note>horizontal grid lines</note> from their oblique positions, by which it is known that their heights are designated. This itself, however, can also be done within a varied <note>distorted</note> cube. This method opens the way most widely to expressing innumerable differences.

But to come to the matter: let the face of the principal head be proposed. In that face, let the transverse lines of the heights of the principal parts be marked first, which are three in number. One is **.k.**, which is at the top of the forehead. The second is **.l.**, which runs across the eyebrows and grazes the top of the ears. But the third, **.m.**, ends at the nose. Therefore, when these lines are moved from their place upward or downward, differences in the intervals arise, and the spaces become higher or lower. Whatever is lost from one space by this movement of the lines must necessarily be added to another. In this way, anyone will be able to raise or compress individual parts of the face.

What has been said regarding the movement of the three named lines is also to be understood for the others—namely **.f. .r. .q. .p. .o.**—whether their order is preserved, that is, so that they remain <term>parallel</term> <note>original Greek: "παράλληλοι"</note>, or whether they are slanted, which causes the spaces to become wider on one side and narrower on the other. According to this reasoning, all things can be varied that are understood differently by the terms length, shortness, extension, contraction, thickness, and thinness.

Each individual transverse line is typically moved from its place in three ways: **upward**, **downward**, or **obliquely**. It is also permitted to move all of them together upward or downward, or to move half of them upward and half downward, and indeed to move both the transverse and perpendicular lines in almost all directions at will. But if you change anything that you wish to be consistent with the "principal" <note>the original, undistorted model</note>—that is, the one from which it was changed—you must compose it with the help of careful attention. It is impossible to describe all the things that may occur, however wonderful they may be to those who engage with study and industry in the investigation of these matters.

Furthermore, for the face you have proposed to represent, you will describe straight lines by which a form suitable and corresponding to your will is designated. As we also said before, unequal intervals exist due to the movement of the transverse lines. From this, if you make those lines close together, the parts where this occurs will become somewhat short; if you make them more distant, these parts will become somewhat long. These effects, produced in either way along the perpendicular lines in an oblique face, bring about differences in thickness and thinness.

<summary>Dürer explains the mathematical method for altering facial proportions by shifting the horizontal grid lines (k, l, m) that mark features like the brow and nose. By moving these lines upward, downward, or at an angle, an artist can systematically generate an infinite variety of human facial types.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, facial anatomy, geometric grid, transverse lines, Albrecht Dürer, artistic theory, mathematical beauty, parallel lines</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for modifying the proportions of the human head. While the previous page introduced the concept of moving the horizontal grid lines (transverse lines) to create variety, this page provides specific examples of how to redistribute these lines to create heads with elongated or compressed features within a fixed vertical frame.</meta>

But whoever takes these matters in hand to work with them will also consider how much and what kind of change the nature of things allows him. Let this very thing that has been said serve as an example. Let the principal head be proposed, which we described in the first place of the first book. All the perpendicular lines of this head should be left unmoved, but with the **transverse lines** <note>the horizontal grid lines</note> you will act thus: distribute the three lines **.k. .l. .m.** within **.I. .n.** into four equal spaces, within which the principal parts are included. Afterward, by the help of **Diligence** <note>original Latin: "Diligentis"; Dürer often personifies "Diligence" as the careful attention or the assistant required to plot the secondary lines between the main markers</note>, you will appropriately replace the remaining lines, namely **.o. .l. .p. .q. .r. .s.** This form seemed elegant to me because of its symmetry, and we have placed it below, expressed as a tilted and front-facing head.

But it must be observed that the face in a head is in some places rather long, and in others indeed rather short. Therefore, you will know that if the face <note>original: "facies oris", literally "the face of the mouth," referring here to the features of the face from the forehead down</note> is very elongated, the **shell** <note>original: "testam", referring to the cranium or skull cap</note> of the head is made more compressed. Therefore, let such a height be placed in the face in the example that line **.f.** is placed upon line **.I.**, and that afterward, with the support of **Diligence**, the remaining lines within **.f.** and **.n.**—namely **.o. .l. .p. .q. .m. .r. .s.**—are appropriately distributed. You will see, therefore, through this raising, that the marking for the root of the hair is excluded.

Conversely, if you prefer the face to be rather short, you will see the skull-shell higher and coming out somewhat pointed. Therefore, in the nearby example of variation, let there be such shortness in the face that line **.f.** stands below **.I.** by one part out of three of the total height **.I. .n.** And let the remaining lines **.o. .l. .p. .q. .m. .r. .s.** be distributed with the support of **Diligence**, and you will see produced what appears in the subject example.

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how to manipulate the geometric grid to create variations in human facial features. The previous page discussed shifting horizontal (transverse) lines; here, Dürer introduces the concept of tapering or expanding the grid itself to create foreshortened or distorted profiles.</meta>

There is also such a variation of heads, where the **quadrilateral of the cube** <note>referring to the square grid units used as a base for the head's proportions</note>, in the front part—that is, to the right—is contracted, while in the back—that is, to the left—it is stretched out. This is done **proportionately** <note>original Greek: ἀναλόγως (*analogos*)</note> so that as much as is taken away from one place, just as much is added to the other. A similar reasoning applies to the opposite method: if you stretch the front but contract the back. And in these quadrilaterals varied in this manner, all the parts, if distributed appropriately as they ought to be, will represent such faces as those we have placed below on the two **flattened foundations** <note>original: "depressis fundamentis"; Dürer’s term for the underlying geometric diagrams or "ground plans" of the face</note>, as we call them.

---

At this point, it must also be known that all the **transverse lines** <note>horizontal grid lines</note> can be moved according to a certain method, which we will explain shortly. Thus, the spaces above may become somewhat elongated; this very process can also be inverted, and applied to either half of the face, or indeed to any of the transverse lines. These opposites are most evident, as when you now pull downward a line—whether transverse or perpendicular—which you previously moved upward. 

Now, therefore, I shall next describe several faces of heads, intending to show by examples how a work can be elegantly fashioned according to our instructions. By this very means, I trust that I will excite the interest and desire of many toward discovering other things, both greater and better than those we have demonstrated. But to the matter at hand. Let the first proposal be this: to change all the lines so that no part remains congruent <note>matching or identical</note> with the original. For instance, whether the forehead be long...

<meta>catchword: c</meta>

<summary>Dürer explains how to distort the standard proportions of the head by mathematically tapering or expanding the grid, allowing for a variety of facial types. He emphasizes that these geometric shifts must be done proportionately to maintain artistic elegance.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, geometry, facial variation, Albrecht Dürer, transverse lines, quadrilateral distortion, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical manual on human proportion. Having established the basic grid (the "foundations"), he now explains how to manipulate specific horizontal and vertical lines to generate an infinite variety of facial types—from those with high foreheads and short noses to those with elongated chins—while cautioning the artist to remain within the bounds of "human nature."</meta>

...or a short one, or whether we wish the nose to be long or short, and the chin likewise to be long or short. If, therefore, you move the **transverse line** <note>horizontal grid line</note> **l** far upward toward line **k**, a short space for the forehead will be designated, but the nose will be somewhat longer between **l** and **m**. Conversely, if you move line **l** downward toward line **m**, it will enclose a long forehead in the space **k** to **l**, but a somewhat shorter nose between **l** and **m**. If line **l** is left in place and line **m** is moved upward, the nose in the space **l** to **m** will indeed be shorter, but the **cheeks** <note>Latin: *bucca*</note> and chin will be long. But if line **m** is moved downward, the nose will be longer in the space **l** to **m**, while the cheeks and chin will be more constricted. 

Now, arrange it so that the two lines **k** and **m** are further apart from each other, such that **k** is placed near **l**, and **m** near **n**. Then the top of the head and the chin and cheeks will be shorter, but the forehead and nose will be quite long. Do the opposite so that the transverse lines **k** and **m** are less far apart from each other, and you will see the top of the head and the chin with the cheeks turn out somewhat longer, but the forehead and nose short. Again, let **k** and **l** be moved upward, and the top of the head with the forehead will be shorter. Afterward, when the remaining lines within **l** and **n** have been distributed with diligent labor, a long nose will result, and likewise a long chin with the cheeks. Invert this very thing and you will have a high top of head and a longer forehead, but a shorter nose as well as shorter cheeks and chin. Again, let there be an elevation: line **k** toward **l**, but line **m** lowered toward **n**, while **l** remains unmoved. By this will be designated a flattened head on top, a long forehead, and somewhat larger lips and chin. Let this itself be inverted, so that line **k** is lower toward **l** and line **m** is toward **n**, and there will be designated a steep top of the head, a short forehead, a longer nose, and somewhat shorter cheeks and chin. I could be quite copious in the explanation of these matters, but it seemed best to strive for brevity. 

You, who wish to employ these methods diligently, shall observe this: that you move the lines toward or away from each other in such a way that the power of imitating the nature of things is not violated, and so that the fashioned faces of men retain a **human measure** <note>Dürer warns that while the geometric system allows for extreme variation, the artist must not create monsters or non-human proportions</note>. But what we have said concerning the three lines **k**, **l**, and **m**, the same ought to be understood regarding the remaining lines **o**, **p**, **q**, **r**, and **s**. Furthermore, once these have been completed according to the mode of variation that pleases anyone, yet in such a way that all the lines remain **parallel** <note>original Greek: <term>παράλληλοι</term> (*parallēloi*)</note>, one may also vary the proposed faces by slanting these same lines, so that they decline either toward the front or toward the back, whether all of them or only some. In addition, it is even permitted to change the position of the **oblique line** <note>a slanted or diagonal line</note> which was described as a single line in the principal head from the root of the hair to the tip of the nose. It is also permitted that the transverse lines be **mutilated** <note>Dürer uses "mutilated" to mean "offset" or "broken," where a single line is split into segments that do not align</note> by moving small parts upward or downward, and the perpendicular lines moved forward or backward in the same way. This very thing can also be done in the oblique lines. But even after all these changes established according to anyone's judgment, let it be permitted to move the spaces within the small parts of the transverse lines—where the offsets were made—upward, downward, forward, or backward. These things, however, can never be stated clearly enough in words; wherefore everyone must approach the examples and the work itself.

Because I have said that lines are "mutilated," so that what I mean may be understood, let line **a–b** be proposed as an example, in which a point **c** is marked wherever one pleases. There, as if the pieces of a broken line were moved, let one be moved upward and the other downward. Thus there will be two parts of the **offset** <note>Latin: *mutilationis*</note> into which the proposed length was, as it were, broken. Likewise, let two points **c** and **d** be written on the proposed line, and let as much of the length as was occupied by these two points be cut away, and let that part, as if broken off, be moved upward or downward. We have placed an example of the transverse lines below, but the same are to be understood regarding the perpendicular and oblique lines as well, whose parts may similarly be moved forward or backward. With this example perceived, you will be able to look upon the faces described above, fashioned according to our description and intention.
