<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of Albrecht Dürer’s geometric system for facial variation. While the previous page focused on shifting single lines (like the brow or the nose base) to alter the profile, this page explains the effects of moving multiple lines simultaneously—such as raising both the hairline and the brow—or creating graduated spacings between the features to produce more complex anatomical variations.</meta>

This face has two lines, **k** and **l** <note>the hairline and the brow line</note>, raised very high, but **m** <note>the base of the nose</note> is placed in the middle between **l** and **n** <note>the brow and the chin</note>.

This one, however, has lines **l** and **m** positioned quite low, but **k** stands in the middle between **i** and **l** <note>the top of the head and the brow</note>.

In this face, lines **k** and **m** are moved upward.

In this one, by contrast, **k** and **m** are moved downward.

Beyond the aforementioned movements of these three lines—**k**, **l**, and **m**—there are these two additional variations: first, where the uppermost space within **i** and **k** <note>the distance from the top of the head to the hairline</note> is the largest, the next space within **k** and **l** <note>the forehead</note> is less high, the following space within **l** and **m** <note>the nose</note> is smaller still, and the bottom space within **m** and **n** <note>the chin</note> is the smallest of all. In the other face, these proportions are changed accordingly <note>reversed so the chin is the largest space and the crown the smallest</note>, as is demonstrated by the examples placed below.

<summary>Dürer explains how shifting multiple horizontal grid lines (hairline, brow, and nose) simultaneously creates diverse facial profiles. He also introduces graduated proportions, where the vertical segments of the face progressively increase or decrease in size from the crown to the chin.</summary>
<keywords>facial proportions, geometry, Albrecht Dürer, human anatomy, profile drawing, Renaissance art theory, grid systems</keywords>

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Dürer's "transfer" method. While the previous page looked at shifting specific horizontal segments (hairline, brow, nose) to create varied facial types, this page expands the geometric theory to include the distortion of the grid itself—tilting, breaking, or curving the lines to achieve a nearly infinite variety of human expressions and physiognomies.</meta>

The greatest variety arises, however, if the straight <term>parallel</term> <note>original: *παράλληλοι*</note> lines—both the horizontal and the vertical ones—are shortened or slanted. Therefore, when necessity requires, these shortened and slanted lines we mentioned should be carefully considered so they may be better understood. I will explain my meaning through the example of <term>slanting</term> <note>original: *obliquatio*—the act of tilting or making a line diagonal</note>. For individual horizontal lines can be raised or lowered on either side, and vertical lines can be moved forward or pulled back. Yet, they have not yet ceased to be straight lines. 

Therefore, as the subject requires, they may also be curved into upward or downward arcs, or bent into forward or backward arcs. There are even alternating, almost snake-like curves, without omitting the shortening of the lines themselves. Let us demonstrate this in the line of the forehead and the eyebrows. For those lines can be raised or lowered on either side. And this line, as much as it exists between the vertical boundaries, can be broken and moved up or down. It can even be shortened in the middle and thus bent up or down at the point of the break; for as the need arises, one may tilt the eye upward or downward in the portrait. 

But also, the line of the corners <note>referring to the corners of the eyes or mouth</note> can be drawn out as far as needed, and usually raised on one side and lowered on the other, so that it becomes slanted. Just as we see in small sundials, which they call <term>compasses</term> <note>original: *compassa*; referring to portable sundials that used a compass and slanted lines to indicate hours</note>, where each line departs obliquely from the <term>meridian line</term> <note>the central North-South axis</note>, and in this way, the inner or outer angles will be made more or less deep. If the line of the nose is broken, the nostrils' prominence will be raised or lowered; the same must be understood regarding the cheek and the jaws moving up or down at their rear part. 

There is also quite a difference in <term>slanted faces</term> <note>original: *facieb. obliquis*; faces shown in profile or three-quarters view</note> if the neck is moved closer to the chin or slanted by being drawn quite far back. The former seem to look down at the ground in a humble way, while the latter seem to look upward and straight ahead. Finally, there is the position of the ears, which can similarly be moved: up, down, forward, or backward. Likewise, they can be slanted in either direction according to one's judgment. One may also make this same area wider, narrower, shorter, longer, more stretched, or more contracted. For in human faces, one can see ears that are flattened against the temples in some places, and upright or protruding in others. 

From these points, I believe that studious readers will easily grasp my meaning regarding the changes of the subjects proposed. For when they see the methods by which straight and vertical lines can be curved, slanted, and made unequal, they will find without great effort the shortening of heights as well. And how to make thick things thin, and how to bend what is straight. Regarding these altered lines as we have shown, who does not understand that if a form is then drawn upon them, how great the difference will be between this and the one from which it was changed? 

Among these things, let everyone carefully remember the distinctions mentioned above and at the beginning, by which they will notice three main variations of appearance in the slanted face:
1. One is straight. 
2. The second is protruding and rounded or arched, so that all features are projected outward as if in a <term>rabbit's mouth</term> <note>original: *lepusculi ore*; a convex profile where the mid-face/mouth area pushes forward</note>. 
3. The third is curved inward, that is, hollowed out or turned up <note>a concave or "dish-faced" profile</note>. 

But these outward and inward curves can be indicated not only with curved lines, but also with straight ones, and not only with whole lines but also with shortened ones. This entire method revolves around addition and subtraction. And this three-fold variety, as has been said, will lead to infinite mutations. Now these faces themselves can be fashioned either more sharply and acutely, or conversely, more thickly and flatly. I have set forth these distinctions of the three-fold method in the examples placed below.

->c 3<-

<summary>Dürer explains how distorting the underlying geometric grid—by slanting, curving, or shortening lines—allows an artist to systematically create a vast array of facial types and expressions. He identifies three primary profile shapes: straight, convex (like a rabbit's mouth), and concave.</summary>
<keywords>geometry in art, facial variation, profile types, rabbit mouth profile, slanting lines, Albrecht Dürer, human proportion</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the discussion of how Dürer modifies the facial grid. While the previous page focused on shifting individual horizontal lines (like the brow or nose) or curving them, this section introduces the use of non-rectangular grids—specifically the "rhombus"—to tilt the entire head forward or backward, allowing the artist to depict motion and perspective through geometric distortion.</meta>

### The straight face.
### The rounded or arched face.
### The hollowed or upturned face.

There are, moreover, two variations of the slanted face which are designed in the shape of a <term>rhombus</term> <note>a four-sided shape with opposite sides parallel, but with slanted rather than right angles</note> so that both the upper and lower horizontal lines remain <term>parallel</term> <note>original: παράλληλος</note>, but the two vertical sides are slanted, though they remain <term>parallel</term> <note>original: παραλλήλοις</note> to each other. This is done so that, in comparison with the slanted sides, the lines converge at the top and diverge at the bottom. Thus, in the first case, there will be an <term>acute angle</term> <note>an angle less than 90 degrees</note> at the top of the forehead and an <term>obtuse angle</term> <note>an angle greater than 90 degrees</note> at the bottom; at the back of the head, an obtuse angle at the top will correspond to an acute angle at the bottom. In the second variation, these angles will be reversed.

Once this is done, all the facial features must be distributed according to the horizontal lines. However, the lines dropped through these horizontals will not be <term>perpendicular</term> <note>perfectly vertical/at a right angle</note>; instead, they will all be slanted. In the first face, these lines will express a <term>forward-leaning</term> <note>original: proniusculam</note> form, and in the second, a <term>backward-leaning</term> <note>original: supinulam</note> form, as appears in the examples placed below.

### The forward-leaning face.
### The backward-leaning face.

<summary>This page explains how Albrecht Dürer uses slanted geometric grids (rhombuses) to systematically tilt the human face forward or backward. By distorting the coordinate system rather than just the drawing, he provides a mathematical method for artists to achieve consistent perspective and expression.</summary>
<keywords>rhombus, parallel lines, facial tilt, geometric transformation, Renaissance art theory, acute and obtuse angles, anatomical projection</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how to manipulate the facial grid. While the previous page discussed tilting the entire head using a rhombus shape, this section focuses on the "addition and subtraction" of specific parts of the face—shifting vertical lines to make features thicker, thinner, or more prominent—and discusses the aesthetic concept of "venustas" (beauty or grace) in relation to these proportions.</meta>

In these transformed faces, just as we have shown, the process is no different than what we passed down regarding the previous examples. All the horizontal lines, and the straight lines drawn through them either perpendicularly or obliquely, can be moved from their positions. It now remains to be explained in sequence how the movements of those lines—which are drawn perpendicularly or obliquely through the horizontals—show the spaces of the parts in terms of width and depth.

For when, in the proposed quadrilateral of the profile, the front side is marked with the letter **a** and the rear side with the letter **b**, and between these the distribution of parts is divided into seven equal spaces by six lines marked with the letters **c, d, e, f, g,** and **h**, any of these lines may be pushed forward or pulled back at the artist's discretion—either all of them or just a few. The individual spaces enclosed by them can also be moved entirely forward or backward. In this way, variations of width and density will exist, so that the face may appear thicker, more slender, more stretched, or more contracted, just as the parts were made longer or shorter using the horizontal lines. 

Therefore, this method of adding and subtracting follows a similar logic. However, it should be understood that whatever is taught concerning straight and perpendicular lines ought to be applied to curved and oblique lines as well. These matters require no small amount of attention. For instance, we shall begin here: whatever is taken away from the curvature and protrusion of the forehead must be added to the prominence of the nose. The logic for the inversion of features will be the same. 

These features could be designated by curved lines and arcs with their ends turned this way and that, and likewise by shortened straight lines. You will be able to do this for all parts of the slanted face. From this, it is apparent that in the transformation of a figure, opposites always meet. For when you push one part forward, it is necessary that another nearby part, either above or below it, be pushed back. From this, the distinctions of the parts are created according to thickness or stretching, and slenderness or contraction. 

Now, indeed, once the parts of the face have been varied—just as in the whole face—by perpendiculars or diagonals drawn through the horizontal lines, it must not be ignored that these same distinctions we explained for the whole face are used in every single part, even the smallest. That is, whether a part is straight, or rounded, or hollowed out. For example, if you wish the forehead to be straight (that is, flat), rounded, or hollowed. The same applies to the nose, lips, cheek, chin, and similarly all other parts. 

It should also be known that the line by which the roundness of the slanted head is designated can be drawn in various ways. For it is apparent in human figures that one person's head is somewhat flat, another's is more pointed, and someone else's is almost <term>rugged</term> <note>original: scruposum; meaning uneven or "pebbly" in texture</note>. In nature’s great zeal for making all things different, there are infinite varieties of even this kind. 

Next, let us speak of the oblique line which we showed is drawn for the purpose of designating the nose. It can be leaned back toward the perpendicular line **d**, so that it is <term>diagonal</term> <note>original: διαγώνιος (diagōnios)</note> to the horizontal line **m**. It can also be drawn somewhat upright from the middle of **a** and **c**, or it can be leaned forward toward the perpendicular line **a**. 

In such arrangements, I especially approve of that which embraces <term>beauty</term> <note>original: venustatem; the classical aesthetic of grace and harmony</note>, since everything that is strange, steep, unusual, or startling may seem out of place. Thus, for example, we may say that a face is not beautiful when the crown of the head is either too high or too pressed down—the former having a head that is too pointed, the latter too flat. Therefore, what remains is a head of moderate roundness in the middle, which everyone perceives to be beautiful. 

As I have said, this is posited by us as an example of our teaching, not as a proof that all "middle" or average things are necessarily the best. However, this is rightly said for now. Just as it is unattractive in a whole face for it to be too long or too short, so one can gather many distinctions of deformity in the individual parts. For instance, a forehead being too long, too short, sinuous, or as if it were rocky. Indeed, in those noses of ours, we see long, knotty, and hanging noses.

->c 4<-

<summary>This page explains how to manipulate the vertical grid lines of the face to change the thickness and prominence of features like the nose and forehead. Dürer emphasizes that while nature provides infinite variety (including "deformities"), the artist should aim for "venustas" (beauty), which usually resides in a balanced, moderate roundness of the head.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Albrecht Dürer's system of human proportion. Following the discussion of how to shift the vertical and horizontal lines of a facial grid, the author now describes the vast diversity of human features and how these can be mathematically represented by altering the grid lines. It concludes with a significant aesthetic theory: that the artist must understand deformity to truly recognize beauty.</meta>

On the other hand, some have noses that are short, twisted, <term>snub-nosed<note>original: "simis"</note></term>, hooked, or bulbous; some are depressed at the top, while others are prominent. As for the eyes, some are tiny and seemingly hidden, while others are large and bulging. Some look out with a half-closed, swine-like gaze, blinking more with the upper lid than the lower. Others look about with eyes opened very wide. Some people have high eyebrows, some have brows that seem to weigh down upon the eyelids, and some even have drooping eyebrows; these may be thin in some people and thick in others. Furthermore, there are thick, fleshy, large, and lumped lips. In other people, the lips are sharp and slender. In some, the upper lip stands out more than the lower; in others, it is the reverse, and often one is thicker than the other. Frequently there is a somewhat high space between the nose and the lip, while in other places the nose seems to sit directly upon the lip. Furthermore, there are cases where the chin is blunt and large; conversely, where it is sharp and small. Sometimes the chin is far from the neck, and sometimes it is as if it were rubbed against the neck; in some places it is long, in others curved. These variations are designated by their own <term>transverse lines<note>Horizontal grid lines</note></term>, as has been indicated. There are also some faces that are unequal in the manner of a staircase, and this in two ways: for they either project most at the top and retract more and more toward the bottom, or, projecting at the bottom, they recede step by step as they climb upward. These are designated by <term>mutilated lines<note>Refers to broken or shortened grid lines used to indicate slants or missing proportions.</note></term>. Although much could be handed down regarding these—how they might be employed even in the smallest parts—diligent students will easily discover these things for themselves through practice.

At this point, it should be understood that anyone is permitted to create more or fewer transverse, perpendicular, and oblique lines than we have made, whether for the sake of greater accuracy or to avoid labor. It is also permitted for anyone to curve straight lines according to their own judgment, if the matter seems to require it. So that all these things may be better and more correctly understood—namely, how they have been prescribed according to my mind’s intent—it was decided to include various examples of the figures we have mentioned. The diversities of these figures appear designated by changed perpendicular, transverse, oblique, and certain mutilated and curved lines, to which the harmony of the form was later expressed. These will be of great use to those who could not easily have understood our writing without an example. 

However, the logic of this variation is infinite. For features can be transferred from one form into another, and everything can be mixed from the first to the last; it is altogether difficult to say what degree of liberty is granted to the work in these matters. Indeed, the work itself ought to be occupied with frequent mutations, just as we, in our examples, have moved all the perpendiculars and transversals randomly, and likewise slanted them as boldly as our whim dictated. Then we expressed the corresponding form, from which one may observe the result. But it must not be forgotten: if anything is taken away from the thickness of the <term>profile face<note>original: "oblique faciei". In Dürer's geometry, the "oblique" view refers to the side profile.</note></term>, it must be added to the width of the <term>front view<note>original: "latitudini aduersae"</note></term>. And conversely, if anything is added to the one, it must be taken away from the other. Furthermore, it should not be ignored that these variations serve to show distinctions between people rather than the <term>beauty of form<note>original: "venustati formae". Dürer distinguishes between character studies (which can be "ugly") and the search for ideal beauty.</note></term>; yet it is very profitable to design many of these for the sake of practice. For a beautiful form cannot be known through itself and without comparison to an unlovely one. Therefore, he who would know what is proper must also, by necessity, know what is deformed.

<summary>This page catalogs the vast diversity of human facial features—noses, eyes, lips, and chins—and explains how an artist can manipulate a grid of lines to represent these variations. Dürer concludes that studying these "deformities" is essential for an artist to truly understand and define beauty.</summary>

It remains for us to speak about the transformation of frontal faces <note>original: "aduersarum facierum"—literally "faces turned toward us."</note> and their various types. However, because there is a single method for transferring the horizontal lines <note>original: "transuersas lineas"</note> from all slanted profiles into frontal views—by the helpful hand of the person doing the transferring—it would be unnecessary to speak further about the variations of horizontal lines at this time. I will only advise this: the horizontal lines in a frontal face can also be slanted, either all of them or just a few. By doing this, the faces are distorted in the same way as when one side of a whole cube is lowered while the other is raised.

Let us proceed, then, to the treatment of the vertical lines <term>perpendiculars<note>In this context, these are the vertical grid lines that determine the width and spacing of facial features.</note></term>. These are found between the sides marked **a** and **b**, and in our system of teaching, they are labeled with these letters: **i, g, c, d, e, f, h, k**. These lines designate the principal parts of the face, such as the forehead, eyes, nose, ears, cheek, and chin. It should be understood that these lines can be moved from their positions individually, several at once, or even all together. Thus, the further they are spaced apart, the greater the width of those parts will be; the closer they are together, the smaller the width. Furthermore, they can be drawn so that they seem to converge <note>original: "concurrant"—meaning to meet at a point.</note> at the top and spread apart at the bottom, or vice-versa.

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how to manipulate facial features using a grid system, moving from the theory of horizontal lines to the practical application of vertical and curved lines to create diverse human expressions and types.</meta>

They are also described as curved; then they cease to fall perpendicularly, but become either oblique or curved. By this reasoning, in the depicted form, you will see the forehead expanded or contracted, small or large spaces between the corners of the eyes, and larger or smaller intervals between them. The eyes, eyelids, and eyebrows can also be designated with unequal size and position. You will see the nose expanded here and contracted there, above or below; it may be straight or hooked, rounded or sharp, smooth or knotty, narrower than the nostrils themselves, or the nostrils may be wide. Or, conversely, an expansion above the nostrils with the nostrils themselves contracted. We may say the same regarding the expansion or contraction of the cheek, chin, and jaws. The same applies to individual parts that are contracted above and expanded below, or vice-versa. By this path of unequal distribution, hunched, distorted, and strange faces are created in many ways. For example, with a narrow or wide mouth; with curved, thick, small, or large lips, or even unequal ones. With jaws expanded, contracted, sharp, or blunt, with the joint appearing more or less. The studious and experienced reader must investigate all these and similar diversities, from which he can extract many wonders <note>original: "admirabilia"—things worthy of wonder or technical marvels.</note> that lie hidden beneath them. 

Indeed, nothing is more certain than this: those who understand deformity <note>original: "deformitatis"</note> and ugliness <note>original: "turpitudinis"</note> easily perceive what must be avoided in a work intended for beauty <note>original: "venustatem"</note>. The further one recedes from depravity <note>original: "prauitate"—here meaning aesthetic defect or distortion</note>, the closer one must necessarily approach beauty. To whom these things are manifest, he will easily and clearly understand what differs from what and by what reasoning. This must happen not only in paintings, but also in those works produced with the burin <term>graver<note>original: "caelo"—the primary tool of the engraver, essential to Dürer's own fame.</note></term> and the chisel <term>scalpel<note>original: "scalpello"—used for carving or sculpting stone and wood.</note></term>. He who attempts these things while ignoring this reasoning, even if he expresses something harmonious and equal, it is certain that it was achieved by chance and not by art.

But let us return to the purpose. A face appears one way when placed on a long and slender neck, and another way on a short and thick one. Furthermore, one must observe the features of the face <note>original: "lineamenta oris"</note>, which in some places are large and full in the eyes, nose, and chin, and in others thin and pressed. Faces also appear different if the head is hairy or shaved, with curly or straight hair, thick or thin, long, shorn, frizzed, glossy, moist, or dry. If the face is bearded or beardless. And regarding the beard, the same things we said about the hair should be understood. We have declared some of these things in the examples placed below. Otherwise, the method for changing the male and female head is the same; only this must be observed, that each reflects the nature of its own sex. By such changes, the whole body can be traversed, and all its parts examined. But as I have warned, care must be taken that the male and female sex are not confused. The image of a man should not be changed so as to effeminate masculinity itself, nor the female image changed so that it passes from that kind into the masculine; for even without this, the path of transformation is very wide.

Moreover, since all living creatures are found to be similar within their own kind, yet the distinction of sex is almost always evident, as in the race of humans and of beasts. Furthermore, effort must be given so that these sexual distinctions in the images of men retain a human measure. Just as we see all beasts retain a certain measure of their own nature. For a lion is never so unlike other lions that it resembles a donkey, nor a fox so that it seems to be a wolf. Therefore, the property of genus and nature must be preserved everywhere.

<vocab>face, nose, eye, beauty, deformity, painting, chisel/scalpel, sex, beast, features/outlines</vocab>

<summary>Dürer explains how distorting grid lines allows an artist to depict a vast range of facial types, emphasizing that a master must understand deformity to achieve true beauty. He also cautions artists to maintain the distinct natural characteristics of gender and species, ensuring men look masculine, women feminine, and humans distinct from animals.</summary>
<keywords>facial proportions, aesthetic theory, artistic anatomy, deformity vs beauty, gender distinction in art, Albrecht Dürer, grid systems, Physiognomy</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer's discussion on the limits of artistic variation, moving from the technical manipulation of facial features to the philosophical and physiognomic distinctions between human character and animal-like traits.</meta>

What then do they mean—those who speak in such a way as to say that someone has a lion-like or bear-like look, or that one is a wolf, a little fox, or a dog? Surely they do not mean that the person is actually a four-legged animal <note>original: "quadrupedem"</note>. Nor do they say they have a similar body or similar limbs. Instead, they are noting a certain similarity of spirits <note>original: "animorum"</note>. They mean that these people behave in such a way that they reflect the natures of certain animals through their own characters <note>original: "ingenijs"</note>. This itself has very little to do with the physical state of the limbs, and these two things should not be confused. 

However, it cannot be denied that there is the greatest possible difference <note>original: "dissimilitudo"</note> among humans themselves. Yet, this is so that the likeness of the species <note>original: "generis"</note> remains consistent. This same principle is apparent even in the breed of dogs. For some are large, others are small, and they differ from one another in six hundred ways—smooth-haired, hairy, or of various colors. But they are not so different from one another that they lose the likeness of their species, or that they appear to be foxes or wolves. From this it is clear that changes and varieties in things do not immediately overturn or confuse their underlying natures; thus, it is easy to distinguish the shape of a dog from the shape of a wild beast. 

Regarding this difference—lest I be more wordy than is proper—I will make an end of the discussion. But I return to those things I warned of at the beginning, from which these ideas arose: that confusion of the sexes must be avoided—that is, masculinity <note>original: "virilitatem"</note> of form in a woman, and femininity <note>original: "muliebritatem"</note> in a man.

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page follows Dürer's discussion on human variety and the limits of physical traits. Having established that humans can vary wildly in appearance while remaining within their species, he now shifts to the technical geometric methods an artist can use to "stretch" or "compress" these figures mathematically.</meta>

<sig>d</sig>

Now I will show another way to achieve a new measurement for a chosen image, should one wish to make it thicker or thinner, or stretched or contracted. This is managed by two lines through which the change is made—similar to how we showed length and shortness before, but in a different manner, as follows. If you have the height of the image you want to use as a model to create a new one that is wider or narrower across, or more stretched or contracted, then the line for height is already known. You must then establish another line for the measure of thickness <note>original: "densitatis," literally density or stoutness</note> and width. If you want the image to come out larger, this line will be longer; if narrower, it will be shorter. You will divide this line into its main parts by means of the <term>Variant</term> <note>Dürer’s "Variant" is a specialized proportional scale or transferring tool used to shift dimensions from one scale to another</note>, and thus you will adapt the measurements to the changes of the proposed image. In this way, all parts will come out thicker and more stretched from a longer line, and thinner and more contracted from a shorter line. We have illustrated the figure of these two lines below, and we have given them the name <term>Gemini</term> <note>Latin for "The Twins," referring to the paired axes used for these transformations</note> because of the way these two lines are used together for a single measurement. 

This description is as follows. First, set a straight line perpendicular to the known height, which shall be **a.b.**; this line must be clearly marked with the distinctions of all its parts. Then, join a transverse line at a right angle below it, which will be shorter or longer according to your choice; let these marks be **b.c.** Thus you will have a right-angled three-sided figure <note>Essentially a coordinate system or an L-shaped ruler</note> designated as **a.b.c.** Once this transverse line has been newly marked out by means of the <term>Variant</term>—or indeed by the dividing method explained in the first book—to correspond with the height line, the figure of the <term>Gemini</term> will be complete. You can then use it to measure the thickness and width of all parts.

The use of the <term>Gemini</term> also applies to measuring height. For if the upright line **a.b.** is made longer and marked out anew through its height using the <term>Variant</term>, you will then have the measure for a taller but thinner image. Therefore, whenever either of these two lines is changed, it will cause a variation in the measurement of the image. So, if you want the height of the image to stay the same but make it thinner or thicker, use the transverse line changed to your liking. But if you want to keep the thickness and width the same but make the stature taller or shorter, you will use the height line changed in the manner described. Thus you see that both sides of the <term>Gemini</term>—the vertical and the horizontal—receive their own specific divisions, according to the method of division explained at the very beginning of the first book. At this point, be careful not to forget that these small parts <unclear>should not be too few</unclear>.

In this section, I would like the studious reader to be advised—as will easily become clear through frequent use—that wonderful measurements can be achieved from the shortness of one line and the length of another. This is even more the case if one or the other is moved point by point during the measuring, and most of all if both are moved. What I am saying will be clear to a skilled architect, and difficulties that seem miraculous will be very easily explained by this method.

<summary>Dürer introduces the "Gemini" (The Twins) method, a geometric system using two perpendicular axes to independently adjust the height and width of a human figure. This tool allows an artist to mathematically thicken, thin, or stretch a figure while maintaining consistent proportions.</summary>
<keywords>Gemini, Variant scale, human proportions, geometry in art, Albrecht Dürer, coordinate systems, thickness and width, architectural drafting methods</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the technical demonstration of the "Gemini" lines, which Dürer uses to scale human proportions. ## D 2

Let us now provide a practical example of this method. Suppose we take the female figure from the first book—specifically the one depicted after the first male figure. Our goal is to use the help of the "Gemini" <note>Dürer’s "Gemini" or "twins" refer to a pair of scales or lines used to mathematically expand or contract the dimensions of a drawing.</note> to make this figure first more slender in both its depth and width, and then more stout.

However, it must be understood that if these mathematical changes cause the human figure to stray too far from a natural appearance or lose its proper harmony—such as when parts become excessively thick or thin through scaling—then the distorted figure must be restored through correction. This is done by adding or subtracting from the form until it looks right, a process which I trust is clear from my previous explanations.

Therefore, we have adjusted that original figure to achieve a perfect balance of slenderness and thickness. First, we reduced the height of the head slightly. We did not do this by shortening the top, but by contracting it from the bottom upwards, so that the height from the crown of the head to the bottom of the chin becomes 2/15ths <note>original: "duarum. 15." — Dürer uses a fractional system where the body is divided into units, here implying the head is 2 units out of a 15-unit scale.</note> of the total height. Within this height, we distributed the features of the face evenly. We shaped the shoulders <term>scapulae</term> <note>the shoulder blades and the surrounding upper back/shoulder area</note> appropriately, extending them from the line of the chin toward the shoulders. However, because the foot had become too short when scaled by the square method, we extended it to a length of 1/13th plus 1/14th of the body. We also refined the thigh <term>femur</term> and the lower leg <term>crus</term> <note>In Latin anatomy, the *crus* refers specifically to the part of the leg between the knee and the ankle.</note> with more careful adjustment.

In the example of the thicker figure, we kept almost the same proportions as the original model. However, we added a slightly larger "double chin" <term>palearculum</term> <note>Literally a "dewlap"; a fold of flesh under the chin used to indicate a heavier set figure.</note> and drew the shoulders upward from the arms toward the neck. We also rounded the buttocks <term>nates</term> slightly below their guide-line to ensure they did not appear more lean than is proper. We also corrected the legs in the profile view <note>original: "obliqua"</note> and likewise corrected the front view near the ankles <term>talos</term>. The length of the foot in this thicker version measures 1/6th of the total height.

Furthermore, you can apply the same method to the head that we recently demonstrated for the male figure. We have provided the drawings for both types—the slender and the thick—below.

<margin>
Let it not trouble anyone that more horizontal measurement lines <note>Latin: *transversas*</note> are named in this example than in the main one because of its height, even though these points could have been expressed more elegantly.
</margin>

I shall also provide another example of slenderness. Let the main male figure be that one presented in the fourth place in the first book. In relation to the height and its accompanying horizontal lines, make the face one-sixth <gloss>than</gloss> shorter than that model, and thus divide it anew by means of the "variant" <note>Dürer’s *Verkehrer*, a proportional tool or mathematical method used to distort or "invert" dimensions to create different body types.</note>. Indeed, the parts of the height distinguished by the horizontal lines remain as they were designated; only the armpits shall be drawn somewhat higher, so that the shaping of the shoulders is harmonious and not overly thick. The lower line of the chest shall also be omitted, and the nipples <note>Latin: *mammae*</note> shall be drawn on the original line of the armpits, so that they are placed slightly higher up. You will also lower the horizontal lines above the foot slightly, so that the instep <note>literally "little mountain"</note> does not appear higher than it should. And so you will assign to the image its thickness and width from the "Gemini" <note>original: *de Geminis*; this refers to Dürer’s specialized proportional scales or "twin" measuring sticks</note>, and you will fashion it slender and thin. If you wish for the figure to be ten of its own heads tall, you may do so, but in such a way that the forehead line is moved far upward, and you distribute the rest of this height equally. With the head moved up in this way, the figure will have a very long neck; the length of the foot should be one-seventh <note>presumably 1/7th of the total body height</note>. So that anyone might more easily achieve this, we have placed the illustrations below, rendered according to our teaching. In the female figure, the movement and omission of horizontal lines will be done similarly to what we have shown for the male.

<note>This passage demonstrates Dürer’s "modular" approach to the human body. He treats the figure like a piece of architecture that can be stretched or compressed by moving specific horizontal markers (transversals), such as the nipple line or the top of the foot, to achieve different aesthetic "characters" like slenderness.</note>

# I
->3<-

In the following, I will show another way of changing a given image, by which its height is altered according to a certain ratio and method. By this means, the measurements in individual parts are raised or narrowed above or below the heights according to one's will, while the spaces of thickness and width along the transverse <note>meaning horizontal</note> lines remain unchanged. However, an image that had been made taller could also be thinned and contracted in a suitable way, just as one that had been made narrower could be thickened and stretched.

We will first explain how this is to be done. Let a transverse line be drawn, which is **a. b.** Below this, let a point **c.** be placed. If you wish to greatly dilate <note>expand or widen</note> the parts above, make point **c.** closer to line **a. b.** If you wish to dilate them moderately, make it further away. You should know that line **a. b.** represents the base <note>literally "the sole"</note> of the image. Then you shall draw another transverse <term>parallel line</term><gloss>original: παράλληλον (parallēlon)</gloss>, as far removed from that lower line **a. b.** as you want the height of the image to be; let that be **f. g.** From this, a line shall be dropped perpendicularly to the lower line, in which the division of the height's parts is established; let this be **h. i.**

After this, you will make a transverse line pass through this perpendicular, which will be halfway between **f. g.** and **a. b.**; let this be **k. l.** Once these things are done, the transverse lines of the parts shall be drawn through all the points of division on the perpendicular **h. i.** Then, from point **c.**, which stands below the perpendicular, you shall draw two lines upward as far as you see the matter requires. These must be arranged so that, being fixed at point **c.**, they move away from each other as they go upward; for they ought to be movable <note>Dürer is describing a geometric instrument or a "transfer" diagram where lines act like a pair of compasses or a fan</note>. By these, the measure of width and narrowness will be contained; let these be marked **d. e.**

Therefore, for a proposed part that is to be fashioned wider or narrower, you shall do this: place the transverse line of thickness or width of that part to be changed, wherever it may be located, onto the middle line **k. l.**, so that the perpendicular **h. i.** cuts that transverse line of the part to be changed in the middle. Afterward, take those two movable lines which we said must be fixed at point **c.** and move away from each other; you shall apply one of them on each side to the end of the transverse line of the part to be changed, lying as was said on line **k. l.** When, therefore, those movable lines touch this transverse line on both sides, they will indicate the dilation of the part in that place which will be closer to this transverse line whose part you wish to change. In this way, all parts grow larger or smaller above and below line **k. l.** There can also be a use for these things inverted, then of course all things will be both different and opposite, according to the diagram of this description of ours which we have added below, to which we have given the name "The Indicator" <note>original: *Indici*</note>.

<summary>This page describes a geometric method for altering the proportions of a human figure, specifically how to stretch or compress heights and widths using a system of movable lines and a central axis. This technical "Indicator" tool allowed Renaissance artists to mathematically derive varied body types from a single ideal model.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, artistic proportion, Albrecht Dürer, perspective, dilation, measurement, The Indicator, Renaissance technique</keywords>

# I
->3<-

In the following, I will show another way of changing a given image, by which its height is altered according to a certain ratio and method. By this means, the measurements in individual parts are raised or narrowed above or below the heights according to one's will, while the spaces of thickness and width along the transverse <note>horizontal</note> lines remain unchanged. However, an image that had been made taller could also be thinned and contracted in a suitable way, just as one that had been made narrower could be thickened and stretched.

We will first explain how this is to be done. Let a transverse line be drawn, which is **a. b.** <note>marked as A. B. in the provided diagram</note>. Below this, let a point **c.** be placed. If you wish to greatly dilate <note>expand or widen the proportions</note> the parts above, make point **c.** closer to line **a. b.** If you wish to dilate them moderately, make it further away. You should know that line **a. b.** represents the base <note>literally "the sole" of the feet</note> of the image. Then you shall draw another transverse <term>parallel line</term><gloss>original: παράλληλον (parallēlon)</gloss>, as far removed from that lower line **a. b.** as you want the height of the image to be; let that be **f. g.** From this, a line shall be dropped perpendicularly to the lower line, in which the division of the height's parts is established; let this be **h. i.**

After this, you will make a transverse line pass through this perpendicular, which will be halfway between **f. g.** and **a. b.**; let this be **k. l.** Once these things are done, the transverse lines of the parts...

<summary>This page details a geometric method for adjusting the height and width of a figure independently. By using a projection point and a series of parallel horizontal lines, the artist can mathematically "stretch" or "compress" a drawing while maintaining structural consistency.</summary>

<keywords>geometric scaling, proportions, transverse lines, image distortion, perspective, height adjustment, mathematical drawing</keywords>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical geometric instructions for artistic anatomy. Having previously defined the standard measurements for male and female figures, the author now introduces a specific geometric tool or "instrument" used to distort those measurements to create different physical types (such as stocky or elongated figures).</meta>

But to return to the distortion <note>original: *perversionem*; here meaning a mathematical transformation or warping of proportions</note> of the proposed image, for which the figure and the calculation of the Index have been explained, its practical use is now required. Let us take, for example, the male figure and the accompanying female figure shown in the fifth place of the second book. <margin>Check whether it is more accurately in the sixth place</margin> The height line of both of these figures should be marked with the points of their distinct parts. Let these marks be **a.** and **b.** Afterward, a perpendicular line <note>a line at a right angle</note> should be extended from point **b.**, at the end of which point **c.** is placed. Finally, let the sides be joined by the slanted line **a. c.** Then, from all the points of division on the main height line, you will draw straight lines to point **c.**, where all of them must converge. Thus, you will have a perfect instrument for the distortion of images, to be stretched or narrowed either above or below. We have chosen to call this the **Distorter** <note>original: *pervertentis*</note>. For by means of this tool, using a hanging line, measurements are typically transformed. The use of it is as follows:

Take another height line, and let it be **d. e.** This should be placed lower down, with its point **e.** on the horizontal line **b. c.** Point **e.** should be removed from the corner **b.** by one-eighth part of the height of the figure to be made; above, however, its point **d.** must rest upon the slanted line **a. c.** Thus, you will see this new line is itself appropriately divided anew by the horizontal lines drawn from the points of the distinct height on the main line. However, it will be distorted, with the parts above being elongated and the parts below being constricted. Therefore, using this newly divided line, let horizontal lines of the original thickness and width be drawn through each point of division, and let the form be drawn. The change of which we speak will then appear. We have illustrated this "Distorter" instrument in two ways below: the first according to the method just shown, and the second in reverse. For if someone wishes to make a transformation opposite to the one described, they must use the instrument in a position opposite to the first, while doing everything else similarly to what has been shown. We have also included examples of the transformed male and female images, though we have not made them wider or narrower at the top or bottom; we taught how that is done when we composed the diagram of the Index. One is permitted to follow their own will in this. If anyone uses the Distorter with focused and singular diligence—the logic of which contains all the necessary signs of the differences explained above for discovering the secrets of this art—they will experience a success in these wonderful matters that they will not regret. But in a work so far removed from the truth <note>referring to intentional artistic distortion rather than naturalistic "truth"</note>, no one should depart too far with additions or subtractions, as has been done in the examples. Now, someone may even measure these distorted figures anew with measuring tools and describe them almost as we did above. However, any deformity that arises from figures being pulled too far from the truth must be corrected everywhere. For these things were not proposed by us as if they *must* be done this way, but rather for the sake of teaching, so that everything so vastly different from the truth might be more noticeable. On the whole, it must be understood that the use of this entire doctrine should be applied for the benefit of necessity, not for the sake of creating depravities <note>ugly or nonsensical distortions</note>. You will take care, therefore, not to move that single hanging line of novelty—whether as we described in the first instance or in the reverse—too far from the corner **b.**, lest such distortion give birth to monsters. Just as the necessary use of this logic can bring great utility, so too will unnecessary curiosity inject a corresponding foulness into the work when one does not take care to avoid doing something "too much" without cause. It should be known that this logic of the Distorter is used as much for individual parts of the body as for the whole. Furthermore, through such transformations of the human image, the diversities of nature—Strength, Weakness, Speed, Slowness, Alacrity, and Sluggishness—are perceived. Therefore, who can doubt that these things should be known by students of this art? Especially since they extend immensely further than what has been explained in this place.

The author emphasizes that while these transformations allow artists to depict different natural temperaments, they must be used carefully to avoid creating "monsters."</summary>
<keywords>geometric distortion, artistic proportions, the Distorter, male figure, female figure, anatomical measurements, artistic theory, temperament</keywords>

<margin>a</margin>

If anyone should wish, perhaps for the sake of novelty, to employ a **distorted male image** <note>original: *virilem perversam imaginem*; referring to an anamorphic or proportionally altered figure</note> along with its female counterpart, he must—for those parts shifted toward the top—greatly compress the top of the head and make the **instep of the foot** <term>monticulum pedis|literally "the little mountain of the foot," referring to the arch or the top of the foot</term> somewhat taller. However, for those parts extended toward the bottom, he must make the shin and the instep shorter. Then, through the benefit of the **Variant** <note>original: *Variantis*; likely a reference to a specific scale or tool used for varying proportions</note>, he should distribute all the parts harmoniously. We, however, have left everything in our examples exactly as they resulted from this method of calculation.

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous anatomical catalogs to explain the practical application of these measurements through geometric transformation, specifically focusing on how to distort or "pervert" the human figure for use on curved surfaces like vaults or ceilings.</meta>

Having demonstrated above the methods for distorting human images, we will now add one more method by which the height of a proposed image may be varied. However, the change in its density and width will be derived directly from the "Diligent" figure. <note>original: "ex Diligente." This refers to a specific reference figure or standardized set of proportions established earlier in Dürer’s work, often termed "The Diligent" or "The Carefully Crafted" figure.</note> Therefore, there can be a universal use for this distortion, which is twofold: one involves a segment of a circle where the measurements are gathered on its convex side, and the other where they are gathered in its hollow <note>concave</note> side. In either path, anyone may progress as far as they wish. Furthermore, he who perceives this doctrine will easily recognize their use. From this is also revealed the reason why, in a convex mirror, a straight ruler appears curved, being somewhat wider in its middle and more contracted at its extreme bends; for it follows the circle, just as other things do. It is necessary that anyone who wishes to decorate vaults and coffered ceilings with appropriate paintings understands these things. Although this does not yet belong fully to the science of perspective, it nevertheless approaches it closely when considered in a mirror. But we are making these transformations through measurements themselves. For this distortion <term>perversio</term> <note>original: "perversio." In this context, it refers to anamorphosis or geometric transformation rather than a moral failing.</note> which we have set out to explain encompasses the measurement of a straight line against a convex or concave segment of a circle. When you observe what is produced by this method, the cause will immediately appear and reveal itself in the work itself. These things, although they may seem marvelous in their design and description, are adapted for use; nevertheless, a knowledge of such things is necessary for the artist, who must select from a multifaceted science what is apt and suitable for his purpose and will, even though the unskillful might mock these and perhaps even more excellent things. But now, let the method of this distortion be explained in turn; if someone uses it skillfully, they will be able to improve many things from a certain principal figure. Therefore, diligent effort shall be given in this part as well, so that nothing is done in excess. <note>original: "Ne quid nimis." A famous Latin adage meaning "nothing in excess," counseling moderation.</note> Furthermore, the fact that our examples—expressed according to the prescriptions of this doctrine—were not larger (which would be excessive) was done not only because of the smallness of the paper, but also so that the difference in the mutation would be more evident. Therefore, in a large work, he who wishes to employ this should maintain proper proportion. But to the matter at hand.

<margin>This is stated unclearly.</margin>
In the beginning, let a line for the height of the principal image be proposed, distinguished by its points; let this be **r.** at the top and **y.** at the bottom. Let it be cut at the middle point **c.** From this, let a transverse line be drawn at equal angles, the end of which is marked with the letter **s.** After this, let an <term>isosceles</term> <note>original Greek: "ἰσοσκελὲς." A triangle with two sides of equal length.</note> triangle be closed between **r.**, **s.**, and **y.**, the apex of which is **s.** Soon you will draw lines from all the points of line **r. y.** toward point **s.**, extended as far as necessary. Then you will establish a <term>parallel</term> <note>original Greek: "παράλληλον." Lines that are always the same distance apart.</note> line after line **r. y.** toward point **c.**, as long as the use requires; for it will be much [longer] than that former one, that is, higher than the base of the triangle. After these steps, fix one foot of the compasses <note>original: "cercinusculi."</note> at point **s.** and with the other describe an arc that grazes the middle point of the line of the principal height of the triangle and curves toward the <term>parallel</term> line which we ordered to be drawn far behind that line **r. y.** Where the horns of this arc join themselves to that line, place the mark **G.** at the top and **t.** at the bottom. Where that same arc cuts and, as it were, prunes the lines drawn from the points of the principal height toward point **s.**, from all those points of intersection you shall draw all the transverse <term>parallel</term> lines through line **f. t.** In this way, it will be newly distinguished from the principal line, but differently. For it becomes both shorter and has the circle as the author of its distinction. Thus, everything is shortened more and more from the middle point upward and downward. Once these tasks are completed, you will apply certain marks of their own to all the points of the new distinction. First, at the top, thus: for the top point of the transverse, **a.**; for the next, **b.**; then **c.**; and so on, **d. e. f. g. x.** up to the middle point. From there, indeed, use marks of numbers all the way to the lowest transverse. Let that lowest one be **1.**, and from there upward **2. 3. 4. 5. x.** This, therefore, will be the newly distinguished line of height **a. 1.** You shall propose this twice: once for the profile view and the other for the frontal view, just as we have often instructed to be done, and so through the distinction of all points, their own transverses [will be drawn].

<summary>This page provides technical instructions for using geometric "distortion" (anamorphosis) to project human proportions onto curved surfaces like vaults and ceilings. It details the construction of an isosceles triangle and circular arcs to recalculate anatomical measurement points for artistic application.</summary>
<keywords>anamorphosis, perspective, geometry, human proportion, vault painting, Albrecht Dürer, isosceles triangle, architectural decoration</keywords>

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for applying geometric distortion to the human figure, moving from determining height to calculating width and thickness using Dürer's "Diligent" reference figure. It describes the specific construction of lines and arcs required to project a flat image onto a curved or distorted plane.</meta>

...you will draw out. But once the divisions of height are found, there remains to be sought the width and density <note>In this context, "density" refers to the depth or thickness of the body parts in a three-dimensional sense.</note>, which you will establish by the benefit of the "Diligent" <note>original: "Deligentis." This is a specific reference figure or standardized diagram of proportions used by Dürer as a baseline for his transformations.</note> from its movable line. You will employ this line on both sides so that the corresponding upper and lower parts can be designated in this manner:

First, let a transverse line be drawn, ending at points **a. a.** Into the middle of this, drop another line perpendicularly; let this be **b.** below and **g.** above. Then, you will accommodate the movable line **a. c.** on both sides. You will use them as follows: Take from the prescribed distortion <term>pervertente</term> <note>Dürer uses "perversion" to mean a geometric transformation or anamorphosis.</note> the middle line which, as was said, tends straight toward point **z.**, as great as its maximum length is within the line drawn against point **z.** and the arc. Therefore, you will establish this maximum length, as I have said, upright upon the transverse line **a. a.** on the perpendicular **b. g.**, and at its summit apply the movable lines **a. c.** on both sides so they are spread out there in a lattice-like fashion. 

After this, you will take all the lengths of the upper part of the transverse lines within the arc and the straight line, and you will carefully note which letters were inscribed on each. You will set these upright on the transverse line of the "Diligent" **a. a.** so that all reach the imposed line **a. c.** as <term>parallel lines</term> <note>original Greek: "παραλλήλας."</note> above. Thus it will happen that all the short lines tend toward the point of the "Diligent" **a.**, just as they were previously near point **S.** in the line of the arc. 

But the intervening spaces do not remain the same because of the difference between the straight and the curved. Furthermore, just as he instructed that the lengths of the parts marked with letters (which were the upper parts) be placed on one side of the "Diligent," in the same way you will establish the lower parts (which I ordered to be marked with numbers) on the other side of the same. Then, one by one, you will place the width of each individual part of the principal image upright on the middle line which is raised on the "Diligent" marked with letters **g. b.**, so that it stands upon the transverse line **a. a.** below. 

Then, move the movable line or thread **a. c.** toward this so that it touches above it. Thus it will happen that all the widths of the other parts are defined in this sequence according to order. And whichever principal width you place upon this, its measurement will be found in its own place marked with letters or numbers, corresponding to its change, defined by the mentioned placement on the transverse line **a. a.** and the adjustment of the line or thread **a. c.** I have also placed below a very clear description of these things. And according to this method, I have fashioned two male and likewise two female images, whose measurements were set forth above at the end of the second book. Only in the arms and feet have I departed from this, following the harmony of the form; for this distortion <note>perversio</note> in the highest and lowest parts involves an excessive and distracting pulling away.

This instrument of distortion, even when you have inverted it, will produce an image that is more extended in the lower and upper parts and more contracted in the middle, as was seen in the previous example. Let this inversion be done thus: Let the line of height distinction be taken up again, and let the proposed extension **S. t.** be added as before. Afterward, establish point **z.** and join the two lines **r. z.** and **z. y.** Soon, draw lines from all points of distinction of line **x. y.** toward point **z.** 

Afterward, mark point **c.** corresponding on the left to point **z.** Then, fix one foot of a long compass before line **s. t.** so that a segment of a circle is described which touches points **s. t.** and is arched between **x. y.** and point **c.** In those places where you see the arc cross the lines drawn to point **z.**, <term>parallel lines</term> <note>original Greek: "παραλλήλοι."</note> shall be drawn from them. These lines, drawn through those points after the arc toward **z.**, will explain the new and entirely freshly prepared distinction. When these are finished, the widths and densities are to be sought by the benefit of the "Diligent" by the method demonstrated above. Let anyone who wishes fashion an image according to this distortion; for us, it was enough to have expressed the instrument itself.

<summary>This page details the geometric process of using the "Diligent" reference figure to calculate the distorted widths and thicknesses of a human figure for anamorphic art. It concludes by explaining how inverting the instrument results in different types of bodily elongation and contraction.</summary>
<keywords>anamorphosis, geometric transformation, Dürer, proportion, Diligent figure, parallel lines, architectural painting, perspective instruments</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Albrecht Dürer's system of human proportion. Following the previous discussion on calculating widths and thicknesses via the "Diligent" figure, this page provides a specific catalog of anatomical markers for a "long and slender" male figure. These points serve as the vertical coordinates that will be subjected to the geometric "perversion" (anamorphic distortion) mentioned on the preceding page.</meta>

Measurements of the
long and slender
