--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific anatomical measurements provided on the previous page to a theoretical discussion on how an artist should apply those measurements. It addresses the potential for artistic variation, the danger of creating "monstrous" proportions through error, and the necessity of using live models to ensure the work remains grounded in natural truth.</meta>

❧

And indeed, these variations are as we have described. It is further permitted to vary these same effects—whether produced through a varying or a diligent method, using or altering the **Twin Index** <note>original: "Geminos Indicem," a reference to Dürer’s specialized proportional rulers or scales used to calculate dimensions.</note>—by adapting them either to entire figures or to specific parts of them. Indeed, one may even mix the proportions of different images, by which method the artist may most correctly judge what will be pleasing to himself and to others. 

However, monstrous things will result from such mixtures unless one is attentive; therefore, great care and focus will be needed when the form is shaped into measured parts of **height, width, and thickness.** <note>original: "altitudinis latitudinisqz ac densitatis." In this context, "densitas" (thickness) refers to the depth or volume of the body in the third dimension.</note> For in the final marking of the height, and likewise through the transverse indices that define the width and thickness, all things obtain a certain and fixed description. But within these boundaries, errors are easily made, insofar as the inner or outer lines may be expressed more or less than is proper. From this follows an undue thickness or thinness, or a stretching or contraction of the parts; these matters have not been fully explained in our description here—as we are striving for brevity—though they could be demonstrated by manual guidance.

It has occurred to us, therefore, that some unskilled persons, attempting to enter the path we have shown and wandering astray in their work, will attribute the fault to us and find flaw with our teaching. And I fully understand that to those wishing to employ this method of ours, all things will at first seem difficult. Truly, I believe such people must place a **living body** before their eyes—one as suitable as possible to the measurements of the intended likeness—and thus, by contemplating the work of nature, they should strive to express the very core of art according to truth, to the best of their strength and understanding. 

For those images are praised in which life itself is recognized, and where the likeness shines forth from the model, especially if **beauty** <note>original: "venustas," a term implying grace, charm, and aesthetic harmony.</note> is also present; for in this way, copies are most highly praised and valued as they deserve. But in those names of distinctions which we have set forth above, let each man occupy himself as he pleases. For one may become accustomed to following the art that embraces truth, or one may use all things without that skill, with a certain liberty of straying; such labor is ridiculous to the learned. On the contrary, works well and diligently made tend to be an honor to God himself, useful to men, and in themselves pleasing and delightful. 

Truly, that contemptible labor of perversity in art is to be blamed; it is found to be harmful and hateful in both small and great works. Therefore, it is right that those things which one intends to bring to light should be perfected with care and reason. One must see to it that nothing intolerable is either taken away from or added to **natural truth.** You may even find those who constrain their variations and changes so tightly that they are scarcely visible; since this escapes the senses, it is to be disapproved no less than when one errs by excess. 

The **middle path,** therefore, is always best. We have set forth such great contrasts in our examples for the purpose of making the evidence greater and more illustrious in small things; however, it would not be right to wish to imitate this same thing in large works. In those, that rigidity must be corrected and the lines must be softened, so that harshness is avoided and true artistry appears. For who would notice differences that are designed poorly and without art? 

Furthermore, let no one wonder that so many and such diverse things can come to the mind of a skilled artist; a whole lifetime would not suffice to work them all out, and the brevity of life compels many to be omitted. For those things which occur to the mind are innumerable, and the soul of the artist is filled with **images**; <note>original: "simulacris," here meaning mental concepts, forms, or "inner ideas" that the artist envisions before creating.</note> all of which—previously unknown in the depiction of humans and other things—he might bring forth daily, if by chance a life of many centuries, and the talent, zeal, and practice of this art were granted to him by divine providence. For by divine favor, those skilled in this and other arts are capable of much. But since we have spoken of diversities and variations...

<summary>In this passage, Dürer provides a theoretical justification for his system of proportions, warning that while artists have the freedom to vary forms, they must remain grounded in "natural truth" and use live models to avoid creating monstrous or distorted figures.</summary>

<keywords>art theory, human proportion, Twin Index, natural truth, artistic beauty, anatomical variation, Renaissance art pedagogy, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific anatomical measurements of the human body to a theoretical discussion on artistic variation. Dürer explains that while his system provides a mathematical foundation, no two objects in nature or art are ever perfectly identical, and the artist must use judgment to vary proportions—within the bounds of nature—to achieve beauty.</meta>

...we might use many words. However, it is established and well-known that no work, nor any thing at all made by humans, can possess an invariable likeness to another work or thing without them naturally differing; so that no **artist** <note>original: "artifex"</note> lives who is so perfect that he can produce two works similar in every respect, such that they cannot be distinguished from one another. For nothing of our making can reach such a great likeness, however much we strive. Indeed, we see this if we print one sheet of paper from the same type, and then another, or cast bronze in the same clay mold; those things which come out have marks of difference in them by which they can be distinguished for many reasons. Now, if this occurs even where there is such certainty, what must we think happens in other cases?

But we are not speaking of such accidental differences at present. Rather, I want a difference to be understood which someone has studiously proposed to express, and all the previous points should be taken in this regard. For whenever someone has conceived a work in the mind, images of difference immediately arise—not of that inevitable and necessary kind, but of this kind by which beautiful and ugly things are distinguished. This follows from those terms of difference explained above. When the work is finished, the judgment of the mind follows the contemplation of the eyes. In this, the opinions of different people are almost always different.

Therefore, in our own rules, I want so much to be granted to everyone that, if it seems right, they may change and move everything of ours, about which we shall speak more clearly shortly. In this way, care should be taken that the work is not brought to an excessively foreign and unnatural manner, unless perhaps the intention is to fashion monsters and a kind of race from dreams, where the natures of all things are confused.

But to return to our purpose. Let an **Image** <note>original: "Effigies"</note> first be established, divided by its transverse lines into spaces of height. Those transverse lines, then, are included by us in a fixed number. But everyone is permitted to describe more or fewer. Making more lines serves a more exquisite precision, which is the mark of a diligent artist. He who makes fewer will certainly reduce the labor and trouble, but he will not be able to reach exceptional precision. Now, those who are very experienced may not even draw lines, but only use points at the divisions, if this is sufficient for their understanding.

And this is one linear variation of our method. Another similar one is the moving of lines through the whole body, as demonstrated by us in the variations of faces in heads. For in the same way, those transverse lines of the whole body will either be moved closer together or moved further apart. Where the former produces shorter parts, the latter produces taller ones, and all this belongs to the difference of shortness and length.

However, the difference of **thickness** <note>original: "densitas," referring here to the depth or volume of the body</note> and **width** <note>original: "latitudo"</note> must not be neglected, which itself varies within the transverse lines. For if you make the transverse lines longer, the width of frontal bodies and the thickness of profile bodies will come out increased. But if you make them shorter, both will be diminished. And the same thing will clearly happen within the parts between them, so that they become larger or smaller. But at this point I warn again: see to it, through all the terms of difference, that there is **Nothing in excess.** <note>original: "Nequid nimis," a Latin translation of the Greek Delphic maxim "Mēdèn ágan," a core principle of classical and Renaissance aesthetics advising moderation and balance.</note>

If an ingenious reader and practitioner happens upon these our precepts, he will achieve wonderful changes and variations of figures. And from these come such results as broad shoulders, narrow hips, and contracted thighs, or the opposite; or a somewhat short torso with long legs, or other such variations. Likewise, straight bodies with arms and legs, or distorted ones. Entirely through **measurement** <note>original: "mensuratio"</note>, the nature of men is revealed. Therefore, from this, the reason for beauty and deformity is sought, and whether a work becomes graceful or ungraceful. Thus, it must be decided and deliberated by the artist before he applies his hand to the panel, what he wishes to fashion. So that when fame is sought through the work, with exquisite...

<summary>Dürer argues that while perfect duplication is impossible, artists should use his proportional systems as a flexible foundation to create variety and beauty, warning against extreme proportions that result in "monstrous" forms.</summary>
<keywords>artistic variation, human proportion, aesthetics, moderation, measurement, Renaissance art theory, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the variation of form, moving from the mechanical application of rules to the role of the artist’s innate talent and the elusive nature of absolute beauty.</meta>

<lang>English</lang>
<sig>f 4</sig>

...and uses those means which are most suitable for completing it. And if a skilled and practiced **artist** <note>original: "artifex"</note> demonstrates what his **talent** <note>original: "ingenium"; Dürer refers to the innate creative power or genius of the artist</note> and art can do more and better in a very simple and small work than another in a large and elaborate one. These things, unusual and new to others, are easily understood only by artists powerful in both intellect and hand. From this, it happens that in a single day's time, things dashed onto paper with a small pen, or carved into a wooden block with a chisel, may be more praiseworthy and better than another person's large work of a year's labor. This must truly be considered a unique gift of God. For often it happens to someone that their talent and the success of their work are such that neither in their own age nor in many following centuries is there a peer. This can be understood from the monuments of the ancients, whose fragments are still being unearthed today. In these, there is so much art that nothing of our own can be compared to them. But if anyone asks what the reason for **beauty** <note>original: "pulchritudo"</note> in images is: some might say, those things which human judgment approves. Others will not grant this. Nor does it seem so to me, if judgment is allowed to unskilled men. But who shall define what skill is sufficient?

For I am of the opinion that no one lives who can fully grasp in their mind the supreme perfection of even the lowliest living creature; much less would I think anyone could do this for man, whom God fashioned with singular counsel and manner, wishing him to rule over all other creatures. I would not deny that a more elegant image is often conceived in the mind and expressed by the hand by one person than another, which can be demonstrated with the best reasons. But for that reason, one would not have reached that maximum perfection than which nothing better can be made. For this is not grasped by the powers of the human intellect. The Divine Mind knows, which alone could instruct the human mind with such knowledge. For this is the rule of **truth** <note>original: "veritas"</note>: what form and what **measure** <note>original: "mensura"</note> is most beautiful and most fitting, to which one single thing—not many—must correspond. But to humans seeking it, many diverse things occur, and it happens that we pursue beauty by more than one path, yet ugliness offers itself more readily everywhere. And in these great errors of ours, I find no way to demonstrate an absolute beauty of true measure, as I desire nothing so much as that crudeness and deformity be cut away from the works of our people (unless perhaps they are sought on purpose), and if I can bring any help, I would do so studiously.

But now I return to that human judgment I spoke of; to them, whatever image seems most beautiful now, they soon prefer another. That artist, however, deserves praise who can complete a **figure** <note>original: "effigies"</note> approved by the judgment of individuals, for which much use and experience is required. Nor could anything better happen to him than to have grasped the measure of truth in his mind, so that he understands the correct and appropriate measurement for his purpose, which must be single and simple. If anyone professes to hold this, they would perform a great feat if they could demonstrate it. Which seems to me hardly possible in these darknesses and gropings of ours. However, whatever work has been explained and confirmed by **geometric reason** <note>original: "geometrica ratione"; the application of mathematical principles to art</note> possesses such certainty that no room is left for blame, nor can it be contradicted. Such an artist all will deservedly revere as divine; those explanations are pleasant to hear, but the work is much more pleasant to behold. But someone will say: "Why then, in this state of despair regarding the best things which you have introduced, should we apply ourselves to the study of science?" This would be inhumane; nor would it be right, when good and bad things are set before us, not to grasp at the better things, even if the best are denied to us. And so that we may demonstrate what is better in our field—that is, in some figure—we must...

<summary>Dürer discusses the "ingenium" (innate talent) of the artist, arguing that while absolute beauty is known only to God, artists must use geometry and experience to strive for the best possible proportions.</summary>
<keywords>artist, talent, beauty, truth, measure, geometric reason, human figure, perfection, divine mind</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on human proportion, moving from the philosophical nature of beauty to the practical application of measurements and the necessity of anatomical consistency across different ages and body types.</meta>

...while before all things, a certain and suitable **measurement** <note>original: "mensura"; here Dürer refers to a standardized system of proportions used to map the body</note> of all parts is sought out, which, once established in its order and manner, allows the individual parts—both the smallest and the largest—to be polished with great care and diligence, so that perhaps we may truly find something of **beauty** <note>original: "pulchritudo"</note> and reach closer to the goal. Since, however, as has been said, the **human body** <note>original: "humanum corpus"</note> is gathered and composed of many parts, each of which has its own certain manner of **uprightness** <note>original: "rectitudo"</note>, we must strive greatly to understand what is contrary to that very manner. This is so that we may better avoid it and be granted to approach as closely as possible the natural constitution, toward which we strive with all our strength as if on a straight path.
<margin>*</margin> ...that we may make the work conspicuous for its art and grace, all of which demand no small amount of care and more than ordinary attention. For first, let someone observe that separate description of the face in the heads set out above; certainly one will discover a marvelous subtlety of curved and straight lines and other notations which cannot be described by the mere drawing of a ruler. Nor will one who is lazy understand those roundings and, as it were, the little globes or hollows of the forehead, cheeks, nose, eyes, mouth, and chin, unless he is careful not to omit the smallest details, treating them with the same care as the largest parts. Nor should he apply less diligence in shaping the individual parts than in the composition and, as it were, the joining together of the whole. The neck follows; this should fit the head, not deformed by shortness or height, thickness or thinness. And so consequently it must be seen that in the outlines of the chest, belly, back, buttocks, likewise of the legs and feet, arms and hands, everything is formed diligently and articulately. These things themselves, and every smallest detail within them, must be perfected in all their proportions with the greatest diligence; for we must not listen to those who say that because they are in a hurry, they are not accustomed to linger over the work. Unless it is a matter of necessity where the thing itself or the desire of those asking for it does not allow for delay, this should not be permitted. If this should happen by chance, then the haste must be forgiven, provided that even in this, the true method is preserved and the knowledge of the art shines through. Thus the entire appearance of the **image** <note>original: "effigies" or "imaginis"</note> from the crown of the head to the heel shall be consistent, in whatever manner it is established. Whether it be rigid or supple, fleshy or thin, let it not seem to overflow with flesh in one part and truly be lacking in another. As for example, if the arms are thin, the legs should not be swollen. Whether from the front everything is full, but from the back very little, or the opposite; for we wish there to be a harmony among the parts, and that they not be heaped together randomly or perversely. For those things which possess **suitability** <note>original: "conuenientia"; the pleasing harmony between parts of a whole</note> also usually seem beautiful. Wherefore the form of age shall also be maintained: neither the head of a youth should be joined to the chest of a decrepit man, nor should the hands and feet of a man of advanced age be made; nor should a youth be expressed from the front and an old man from the back, or the reverse. For those things which do not correspond to nature are judged to be perverse. Therefore, it is right in all ages, constitutions, and manners to preserve the harmony of parts: in the likeness of a youth, a decrepit man, a man of advanced age, the thin, the fleshy, the rigid, and the soft. Therefore, the image of **youth** <note>original: "adolescentia"</note> shall be bright, supple, and smooth. **Old age** <note>original: "senecta"</note>, however, shall be wrinkled, distorted, uneven, with the flesh consumed. To achieve these things with praise, it is expedient before they are fully worked out with strokes and lines to describe everything according to measurement, so that it can be known if anything by chance is lacking in correctness. He who observes this with attention and diligence will not easily be followed by regret for his work. Regarding this matter, an exact knowledge of **outlines** <note>original: "delineatio"</note> is required from the artist, for this is of the greatest importance to the art. Because even if the most truthful measurement has been designated, nothing is more quickly done than the ruining of the work by the hand of some unlearned person's sketching, wandering randomly through the height, width, and thickness of the Image.

<summary>Dürer emphasizes the necessity of consistent anatomical proportions and warns against mixing body parts from different ages or physical types. He argues that true beauty arises from a disciplined harmony of parts, requiring the artist to master both precise measurement and the subtle, irregular curves of the human form.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, anatomy, artistic discipline, youth and old age, consistency, measurement, beauty, sketching technique</keywords>

--- Page 235 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of precise measurements in art, emphasizing that beauty is found by observing nature rather than following one's own imagination. It includes a specific comparison of different human types, including European and African bodies, and concludes that true artistic skill is the result of diligent study of God's creation.</meta>

...measurements. But one skilled in these things, having obtained a good <gloss>and</gloss> true measurement, will have made the object better even in the expression of its form. Moreover, so that we might attain some truth of measurement and be able to induce as much beauty as possible into our works, I find nothing more helpful than to place the living bodies of many people before one's eyes and to collect the measurement of all parts from the judgment of those who are considered beautiful. For one who is truly knowledgeable in art will not find a lack of harmony among parts even in a great multitude of people. But who could point to any single person in whom everything is perfect "to the fingernail" <note>original: *ad unguem*; a Roman idiom referring to a sculptor checking the smoothness of a joint with their nail, meaning "perfectly" or "to a hair's breadth"</note>, as the saying goes, so that nothing is lacking in any part? But remember this also in this part: do not confuse the modes of nature. Instead, ensure everything is congruent—which we said shortly before ought to be done. For images of the young, use the observation of youthful bodies; similarly, use elderly bodies for images of the old. The same is to be understood for advanced age, just as you should compare similar natures in living subjects for the thin, the fleshy, the rigid, the soft, the robust, and the weak. There is no doubt that to those studying this method, more things will offer themselves through individual parts than they even sought, and they will be able to work them out. For art can scarcely ever express the full beauty of things. I do not speak here of extraordinary and perfect beauty, but even that which we have explored, yet which bursts beyond the powers of our intellect and escapes the skill of our hands.

Since, as we have said, different natures of living bodies must be sought for different images, two types of diversity among men also present themselves. I mean our own people who are white, and the Southern people who are black, called "Ethiopians" <note>In the 16th century, "Ethiopian" was a general term for people from Sub-Saharan Africa.</note> by the Greeks. Their faces are different from one another, nor are the bodies of the Ethiopians as pleasing to the sight as ours, because of the great flatness of their noses and the thickness of their lips; likewise, their hands, knees, shins, and feet are considered deformed by knobbiness. But I have seen their bodies in all other parts to be of such great harmony, and polished with such care by nature, that I judged nothing more perfect could be made. Truly, many diversities of form offer themselves within their own kind, which are useful for observation in images according to the variety of constitutions. Thus, in the robust, everything in the torso is large and, as it were, "leonine" <note>lion-like in strength or appearance</note>. But the weak have these parts slight, nor are they muscular like the others. Therefore, the features of softness should by no means be applied to the image of a robust man, nor should the lines of rigidity be given to a weaker one. And although one must sometimes cater to thinness or fleshiness, a balance of this rigidity and softness can be maintained according to each person's judgment. The truth of these things is revealed in nature and life. By dwelling on these and fixing your eyes upon them, do not depart from there toward your own opinions, as if you were going to achieve something better; for know that you will be deceived. For art is truly deeply embedded in nature; if you can draw it out, you will have avoided many errors in your work once you have grasped it. You will also be able to demonstrate the uprightness of your work largely through <term>geometric principles</term> <note>original: *geometricis rationibus*; the use of mathematical proportions and compass-and-ruler constructions to define the human figure</note>. But where these do not reach, it must be left to the common judgment and opinion of men, yet in such a way that we know experience counts for a great deal. Nothing is more certain than this: the closer your work comes to the likeness of life, the greater praise for correctness it will find. Therefore, let the false opinion be far from you that you might make anything better than what God has bestowed upon His own work, which He Himself fashioned. For compared to these, your efforts will be found lacking in strength. From this it can be gathered that no one can ever express beauty solely from their own sense and imagination. But it is necessary that whatever beauty one draws from one's mind must have first been stored there through study and diligent, careful imitation. This should not be considered one's own property, but rather a skill acquired and prepared through study and diligence.

<summary>The author argues that true artistic beauty is discovered through the diligent observation and measurement of diverse living bodies rather than individual imagination. He emphasizes that "art is embedded in nature" and that the artist's goal is to extract this natural perfection through study and geometric principles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of artistic theory, transitioning from the observation of external nature to the "hidden treasure" of the artist's mind. It emphasizes the importance of internalizing rules of proportion to allow for the creation of diverse human types and characters.</meta>

<sig>c</sig>

...what counsel the mind has brought forth in its fruits. And from here, that treasure hidden in the mind and the ideas conceived within are produced into a work. For this reason, practiced artists <term>artifices</term> (skilled masters or creators) do not always need to have living, breathing models placed before their eyes to express their images. For by long use and study, so much has flowed into their minds that much can be drawn from there. To them, everything is ready at hand, but very few attain such great understanding. On the other hand, there are many who work with great diligence but produce only faults.

But those practiced artists I mentioned, refined by art and meditation, can easily produce a brilliant work even when looking at no model—though, to be sure, reference to life and nature makes everything better. Truly, the unlearned and inexperienced must despair of such success. For nothing in these matters happens by chance. Thus, one occasionally finds someone who, by their own inner intelligence alone, looking at no living model, still shapes more excellent images than others who observe life and nature before them with diligence and care. Knowledge of art is what these others lack.

One seeking praise must take care above all that the work is not entangled in any deformity; therefore, he will prune away anything superfluous from those images he wishes to be beautiful, for such things disgrace them. You may understand it thus: blindness, weakness, wasting away, lameness, and distortion are deformities because of the failure of natural use. But it would be no less shameful to see someone with three eyes or as many hands and feet—not because of a deficiency, but because of a superfluity. Therefore, the more you avoid such things and instead shape what is necessary, upright, conspicuous, and healthy—which are pleasing and approved by all—the greater praise you will win for your work.

Beauty itself, hidden in nature, often confounds our judgment. One can find two people, both beautiful and charming, yet neither shares anything with the other, neither in measurement nor constitution; nor is it clear which ought to be called the more beautiful. Such are the shadows of our intellect. Who, then, shall pronounce the truth regarding the excellence of form? But although it is not apparent to us, it is likely that one of those equally beautiful people surpasses the other through the perfection of certain parts.

From this, it follows that the gifted artist must beware of following only one single type in paintings, but rather it pertains to him to be practiced in manifold and diverse ways. From this, it follows that he can express images of men of any constitution. Such as the angry and the mild, or any others, all of which are approved when skillfully made in their own kind. Thus, if someone comes and asks for an image of a man who is deceitful and "Saturnine" <note>melancholic or gloomy, influenced by the planet Saturn</note> or "Martial" <note>warlike or fierce, influenced by the planet Mars</note>, or one more "Venereal" <note>charming or amorous, influenced by the planet Venus</note>, lovely and flattering, what will be easier for the practiced artist than to find, from the rules set out above, the appropriate and true <term>imaginum mensura</term> (measurement of images) for each of those figures? And, once found, to illustrate it with a fitting form? For the natures of all can be shaped thus—whether they be fiery, airy, or earthy <note>references to the classical elements and the temperaments: choleric, sanguine, and melancholic</note>—with the power of art governing the work.

Nor, indeed, does the praise and glory of a work reveal itself only to those who gaze upon it for a long time; it shows itself to skilled artists in the first moment and draws the knowledgeable into an incredible love for it. To those who are well-versed in these matters, what I say is clear, and they possess the <term>scientia veri usus</term> (knowledge of true use). Knowledge <note>original: *Scientia*</note> always embraces truth, whereas opinions frequently wander from it. Therefore, no one should trust themselves too much, lest they happen to be led astray in their work by this. And therefore, it is necessary...

<meta>This page continues the artist’s advice on the intersection of theory and practice. Having previously discussed the "hidden treasure" of the mind, the author now addresses the need for critical observation, the danger of empty talk versus actual skill, and how the discipline of measurement eventually leads to creative freedom.</meta>

<sig>g</sig>

...has frequently contemplated and imitated by drawing, and has heard craftsmen <term>artifices</term> <note>skilled masters or creators</note> disputing their principles. Yet I would not want anyone to think that even in a praiseworthy work, errors should be ignored if they happen to be present; it is right for such things to be both recognized and corrected. Nor should anyone persuade you to restrict your mind to one specific type and style of images, which you have noticed some particular artist usually produces. For nature brings it about that a work is generally such as the judgment and reasoning of the mind. But I command you to contemplate various things and to choose the best from many. For the falsity of opinions troubles almost everyone. And who would dare to say that any work is so perfect that nothing better could be made, since no one can declare—however beautiful a man they have seen—that a more beautiful one could not be found? It is best to enter upon the safest path, whether through the teaching of others or through your own diligent inquiry into the living model. But I do not approve of those masters who, while they are accustomed to speak without end about art, have no praiseworthy work of their own to produce; I know I have seen several of this kind. For if you follow these men, you will fall into error. Even if I were silent, the poor quality of their own works shouts this. Indeed, these are two separate things: to talk much about a work, and to actually make it. Nevertheless, I do not therefore forbid anyone from accepting instructions even from the unlearned; for it can happen that simple peasants might point out some fault in your work, even if they are unable to teach you the method by which you might correct it.

At this point, I would wish to warn those ignorant of this art who have set out to learn from our teaching: let them diligently learn what has been handed down by us, and first set themselves a few things to learn and execute; once these are understood, only then should they proceed to others. For knowledge <term>scientia</term> must grow together with practice <term>usus</term>, so that the hand serves the will and the understanding, from which finally a great certainty of art and practice arises. These two must be joined together; separated, they are worth nothing. Now, although the unlearned and the common people might seem to be able to distinguish better things from worse, yet no one is able to judge truly except a skilled artist <term>peritus artifex</term>, who has himself often attained praise for his own works.

But some will say: "Is so much labor and effort to be put into these measurements <term>mensurationes</term>? Is so much time to be spent that all images are individually required to follow this method? Yet many things often must be made in a short time." We do not even prescribe this. But I teach this: a certain "certainty" must be prepared through study and diligence, which consists solely in these principles <term>rationes</term>. Once those who have attained this have an infallible freedom in their hands, no one will require the measurements of all bodies or things. Rather, eyes instructed in art begin to serve as a rule, and the hand is obedient to the art, which produces confidence and, by its power, drives away errors and excludes falsehood from the work. Then, speed is not lacking, nor—being full of knowledge—will you search long for what you should do, nor will you cast a line or a point at random. Such works merit the praise of art, which appear both lovable and free, and not at all anxious, but "right." Such works are approved by everyone because they contain a goodness diffused through all their parts. But the unlearned and those ignorant of art will never produce anything good and right, however much they have attained a freedom of the hand which may be very great. Indeed, that freedom should then be considered a prison, since it binds one to errors. Therefore, nothing can be done without art, nor will art achieve much without practice; these must therefore be joined, as we have said. From this it is manifest to those studious of these things that the method of measurements <term>mensurationum rationem</term> must be learned; once understood, admirable successes follow. For a human figure <note>original: "humanam effigiem"</note> is not measured by a single rule or compass...

<summary>The author argues that artistic mastery requires a balance of theoretical knowledge and constant practice, warning against "masters" who talk but cannot create. He explains that rigorous study of proportion eventually grants the artist an "infallible freedom" where the eye and hand work instinctively.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Book 3 of Albrecht Dürer’s treatise on human proportion. Dürer (via his translator Camerarius) is finishing his argument that the geometric principles of proportion are universal and scaleable, defending his system against critics who might claim it only works for small-scale drawings.</meta>

...to be designated by the stroke of the hand, for which reason it is necessary that it be described point by point through its individual parts, as we have shown. For nothing that lacks the correctness of <term>measurement</term> <note>Latin: *mensurationis*. Dürer emphasizes that without a mathematical basis, art lacks objective quality.</note> can be approved.

I see that it can happen that some, having followed these our precepts in large images, might go astray through their own lack of skill, but the blame is then transferred to me, as if small things might be correctly finished by these rules, but large ones not likewise. But this cannot happen. For if you praise the small things, why would you find fault with the large? If indeed the former were improper, I would easily grant that the large ones should be rejected, for these things do not allow for a distinction between goodness and badness based on scale alone. 

Just as a very small circle preserves its roundness no less than a massive one, and a square is similarly enclosed correctly by both short and long lines. For the <term>ratios of proportions</term> <note>Latin: *proportionum rationes*</note> agree in large things no differently than in small ones. It is just as in music: for that which they call an <term>octave</term> <note>Latin: *octauam*. The musical interval of an eighth, which maintains the same mathematical ratio (2:1) regardless of the pitch.</note> consists of a certain single tone, whether the voice be ringing and high or deeper and more muffled.

Finally, it must be known that the demonstrated images can furthermore be varied in many ways, but in such a way that the measurement remains entirely the same and undisturbed.

### **The end of the Third Book.**

<summary>Dürer concludes the third book by defending the scalability of his proportional system, using analogies from geometry and music to show that mathematical ratios remain constant regardless of size. He asserts that errors in large-scale works stem from the artist's lack of skill rather than a failure of the underlying theory.</summary>

<meta>This page begins Book 4 of Dürer’s treatise. Having established the "ideal" static proportions of various human types in the previous books, he now moves into kinematics—how to make these mathematical models move, bend, and express emotion without losing their proportional integrity.</meta>

# Albrecht Dürer’s Fourth Book on Painting, concerning the bending and gestures of depicted figures.

Having demonstrated in the previous three books several methods for drawing figures according to fixed and required proportions, and for varying them in diverse ways, it remains to point out the way to <term>bend</term> <note>original: *flectendi*</note> them according to one's will, and to turn them at the joints of the limbs and the connections of the members, in such a way that it appears natural. For what use or beauty would there be in those straight and stiff forms, as they were designated by our teaching in the previous books? Moreover, however skillful or excellent a certain limb of the body may be, it is often disgraced by an ugly gesture, just as it is usually commended by an elegant composition that makes it appear more noble. Now, a formal treatise on the limbs—how they are wonderfully joined and woven together by nature—is not the purpose of this work. Leaving that to those <term>anatomists</term> <note>original: *anatomicis*. Dürer acknowledges that his work is for artists, not doctors, though he studied anatomy closely.</note>, I shall never touch upon it unless perhaps compelled by necessity.

It should be known, therefore, that figures made in the way we have taught, which are seen from the side, are usually bent along the whole <term>spine</term> <note>original: *spina*</note> from the neck down to the hip, through and within all the lines drawn crosswise. However, you should make them more prone <note>leaning forward</note> than supine <note>leaning backward</note>. This entire bending ought to be marked in the last part of the <term>fourth thickness</term> <note>original: *4. densitatis*. This refers to Dürer's coordinate system where he divides the body's depth into segments to track movement in three-dimensional space.</note> toward the back, through and within all the transverse lines, as we have said, except where the bending of the hips is, in the middle line of the upper thigh. The knee is bent on its own line, the foot near the ankle line, and if there is need, in the middle of the foot itself, the toes are bent at their joints. You shall bend the arm at the shoulder, the connection of the elbow, and the joint of the hand. The fingers, moreover, are bent at each of their joints or knuckles. 

But a figure facing forward is usually bent toward either side from the neck through the entire spine of the back; so that these may be more easily noticed, we have placed marks of these <term>bendings</term> <note>original: *flexionum*</note> in the straight figures in their proper places by the shape of small triangles or circles. Because the logic of bending is not simple, so that everything might be explained more clearly, we have decided to propose several <term>categories</term> <note>original: *differentias*</note> of them, concerning which we shall set forth this entire teaching according to our understanding. It must also be observed with great care what the stance and the face of the figure should be—whether severe or pleasant—of which the former ought to be more harsh and rough, and the latter more lovely and sweet. These are generally the names for the different types of bending:

| | |
|---|---|
| <gloss>Bent inward</gloss> <note>original: *Inflexum*</note> | <gloss>Straightened</gloss> <note>original: *Directum*</note> |
| <gloss>Curved</gloss> <note>original: *Curuatum*</note> | <gloss>Twisted</gloss> <note>original: *Contortum*</note> |
| <gloss>Turned</gloss> <note>original: *Versum*</note> | <gloss>Displaced</gloss> <note>original: *Dimotum*</note> |
| <gloss>Intertwined</gloss> <note>original: *Implexum*</note> | |

According to these differences, which we have called by six names <note>Although the table lists seven terms, Dürer likely considers "Straightened" as the neutral state or groups two terms together.</note>, the human body is bent, more or fewer parts moving at once according to its motion. I shall declare my thoughts on these matters using lines in the following pages.

<margin>g 2</margin>

<summary>This page introduces Book 4 of Dürer's treatise, shifting focus from static proportions to the mechanics of bodily movement and gesture. Dürer outlines the anatomical points where a figure should bend (spine, joints, knuckles) and establishes a vocabulary for different types of motion.</summary>
<keywords>flexion, joints, anatomy for artists, spine movement, proportions in motion, artistic gesture, kinetics</keywords>

--- Page 240 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Book 4, where Dürer establishes the geometric definitions necessary to describe the movement of the human body. He focuses here on the distinction between angular bending, smooth curving, and rotation.</meta>

First, therefore, it should be known that I have understood that something is said to be <term>inflected</term> <note>original: *Inflexum*. In this context, Dürer refers to an angular bend, like a joint, rather than a smooth curve.</note> in such a manner. Let there be a straight line **a-b**, and on it let two points **c-d** be noted. If you bend the line from its straightness at these points in any direction, yet in such a way that the segments between the designated points are not curved, but rather the bending occurs at these points as if at joints, know that you have created an "inflection" according to our understanding.

Second, "to be <term>curved</term>" <note>original: *curuari*</note> should be understood as something that has entirely lost its straightness in any way whatsoever, for this term has a broad application. For example, let line **a-b** be a segment of a circle, or [curved] above and below, or above, in the middle, and below, entirely according to anyone's judgment.

Third, we call something <term>turned</term> <note>original: *versum*. This refers to a pivoting motion or intersection.</note> in this way: Let there be line **a-b** and line **c-d**, one of which is placed upon the other, so that both seem to be a single line. Afterward, let a point **e** be noted in their middle, and let them be turned while remaining joined at that point, so that they <term>diverge</term> <note>original: *diuaricentur*</note> above and below.

<vocab>Inflected (Inflexum), to be curved (curuari), turned (versum), straight line (linea recta), points (puncta), segment of a circle (circuli segmentum), they diverge (diuaricentur)</vocab>

<summary>Dürer defines three fundamental geometric movements—inflection (angular bending at joints), curvature (smooth bending), and turning (rotation at a pivot point)—to provide a mathematical framework for drawing the moving human figure.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, kinematics, joints, Albrecht Dürer, inflection, curvature, rotation, articulation</keywords>

--- Page 241 ---
<meta>Continuing the geometric definitions for bodily movement from the previous page, Dürer now describes more complex spatial transformations: twisting, lengthening or shortening, and the separation of overlapping elements.</meta>

<sig>g 3</sig>

Fourth, I call it **intertwined**, as we have described the intertwined lines **a, b, c, d** as if <term>in a spiral fashion</term> <note>original: *terebellatim*. This term is derived from the Latin for a "gimlet" or small drill, describing a corkscrew-like or helical path.</note>.

Fifth, I call this **straightened** and **contracted**, when a line is either unfolded and extended, or as if folded and drawn in, so that it becomes either longer or shorter; I could not demonstrate this in any way other than by using two lines, one longer and the other shorter.

Sixth, we understand the term **to be moved apart** <note>original: *Dimoueri*</note> when two lines that were placed upon one another so that they appeared to be a single line are then separated in such a way that neither touches the other. This can be done in various ways. For it can happen with parallel lines in any direction, or with one being slanted, or with curved lines; and the separation can be established according to anyone's preference. We have placed an example of this in the designated lines **a-b** and **c-d**.

<vocab>Intertwined (Implexum), spiral fashion (terebellatim), straightened (Directum), contracted (contortum), to be moved apart (Dimoueri), parallel (paralelis), lines (lineae)</vocab>

<summary>Dürer continues his geometric foundations by defining intertwined spirals, the expansion and contraction of lines, and the separation of overlapping lines. These definitions provide a mathematical vocabulary for describing how the human body moves and changes appearance in space.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, anatomy, spiral motion, contraction, separation, Albrecht Dürer, spatial perspective, kinematics</keywords>

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>Dürer transitions from the abstract geometry of lines to the practical application of these movements in the human anatomy, explaining how joints, muscles, and skin react to different poses.</meta>

But remember that just as this is demonstrated in lines, it also occurs in the surface—which we understand to be flat—and in the soft bodies—which we understand to have width and thickness. These are similarly inclined to be bent, curved, turned, intertwined, straightened, twisted, and moved apart, just like the lines that contain them. I will now teach how these are used individually in the human body.

The first, which we called **bending**, is found in the joints of the body. For in these, the limbs are bent, yet they retain their own straightness; if you tried to bend the bones, you would break them. Therefore, we showed this using lines consisting of straight parts joined at specific points.

Second, the <term>sinews</term> <note>original: *nerui*. In Renaissance anatomy, this term often referred to tendons and ligaments as well as nerves.</note> and flesh in the human body are **curved**. For when the body is bent to the side, or forward and backward, the sinews and flesh are also curved, so that a small hollow appears in one place and a small bulge in another.

Third, we named the **turning** of the limbs, such as when the head is turned, and similarly the arms, feet, the torso itself, and indeed almost all the members in the body.

Fourth, you should know that **intertwining** occurs in the sinews and flesh during the turning of the body, when the soft parts become interwoven, as in the neck, torso, arm, and foot.

Fifth, you will note that **straightening** and **contraction** are very often used in the human body; the former thins the part, while the latter thickens it. Here is an example: if you extend your arm so it is straight, the sinews are stretched and they thin the flesh lengthwise. But if you draw it in so it is contracted, it is twisted and compressed, and the flesh thickens. This also occurs in other parts of the body.

Sixth, we say that one part is **moved apart** from another in the body when they seem to separate or move away to the side, or forward and backward. Likewise, in curves and bends, the flesh is moved apart and recedes from itself, outward, inward, or to the side.

Furthermore, these six differences can all occur at once in the movement of the body, such that the limbs are said to be bent, turned, and separated. Meanwhile, the flesh and soft parts of the body are curved, intertwined, and contracted, and we see the parts naturally pressing against one another.

From these things demonstrated here, anyone will be able to compose their own figures in whatever gesture they wish, as the subject requires. But care must be taken not to depart from the natural constitution of the body. Whatever is forced will necessarily be deformed. In this matter, one should also avoid "too much" and "too little," which can make an image look either <term>frenzied</term> <note>original: *furiosa*</note> or <term>lethargic</term> <note>original: *somnolenta*</note>—which is a fault, unless you perhaps intended to make the image in that specific way. It should be understood that these principles apply to figures of men and women, as well as to animals like horses and other living creatures.

It must also be known that the heights of the figures described above do not remain the same during the bending of all parts, nor do the measurements of width and thickness remain equal. For in movement, it appears that some space is lost in one part and added to another; thus, a change follows in the work. To execute this correctly, students of these matters must work hard on many examples that express the likeness of living bodies. In this way, they will easily grasp the proper measure of all parts, which must be very well known so that no error is made in the work.

<summary>Dürer applies his six geometric principles of movement—bending, curving, turning, intertwining, straightening, and moving apart—to the muscles and joints of the human body. He emphasizes that movement changes the thickness and length of limbs, requiring artists to study life to maintain natural proportions.</summary>

<keywords>anatomy, movement, joints, musculature, artistic theory, Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, kinetics</keywords>

--- Page 243 ---
<meta>Dürer continues his instruction on how the human form is modified by movement. Having established that lines, surfaces, and solids all bend and twist, he now introduces a specific geometric method—using rectangular grids—to help artists accurately map these changes in perspective.</meta>

<sig>g 4</sig>

Furthermore, for investigating the effects of **flexions** <note>bends or movements of the joints</note>, there is one method among many that is particularly useful for **sculptors**: if, in the drawings, the principal parts of the figure are enclosed within transverse lines and rectangular frames. This is done effectively in the initial sketches from the very start of the work. In the same way, the space of the head is usually encompassed, as are the neck and, piece by piece, the entire **torso** <note>original: *truncus*</note> down to the belt. Then from there to the top of the thigh, and from here to the knee, and consequently the shins and heels. The same is to be understood for the arms. For all things in every part of the body can be most aptly declared by these angular figures, line by line and point by point, so that the position of each part may be understood by means of parallel lines, both vertical and transverse. However, not all of these can be made perfectly square or rectangular; they must be adapted with their sides and angles to the underlying parts of the body so that they may truly assist the understanding.

But so that I may carry out the instructions for these flexions, I will follow my usual habit and explain with examples. Let us take, then, the **Male Figure** <note>original: *Effigies virilis*</note> described in the first book and first section. Let us follow the first head. This head, indeed, should be turned from the straight **plumb line** <note>original: *perpendiculum*; a lead weight on a string used to find a perfectly vertical line</note> toward the left. You shall set that "depressed" <note>seen from above or flattened in projection</note> face described above so that it faces you, but by turning it, you will place it almost at an angle—yet not so much that it stands directly on the corner. Once this is done, when you see the corners marked with their numerical signs (1, 2, 3, 4), you shall draw **parallel** <note>original: *παραλλήλους* (parallēlous)</note> lines upward from each of them, as high as seems necessary. In this way, a description of the width of the **quadrilateral** <note>a four-sided shape</note> will exist, in which the turned face is enclosed. Afterward, you will place a long transverse line marked with the points *r* and *y* beneath those vertical lines. Upon this, you will place the front-facing face toward the upper right, and by the benefit of the transferrer, draw all the transverse lines of the front-facing face through those four vertical lines, as we have taught. Now, in this division of the new quadrilateral, you will have all the locations marked: the forehead, eyebrows, eyes, nose, ears, cheek, and chin. Afterward, draw out from all the corners of the "depressed" face the lines for each individual part, and draw those **parallels** upward through the transverse lines already laid down. In this way, the nose, eyes, cheek, chin, ears, forehead, neck, and the roundness of the head—or **cranium**—will be marked out by these. It will then appear in which areas and within which boundaries the individual parts must be expressed. From this expression, a shape suitable for a turned face, as if looking back, will be produced. But this method, if error is to be avoided, requires singular diligence. To demonstrate all these things more certainly, we have placed the example of the drawing itself below.

<summary>Dürer explains a technical method for artists to draw the human body in motion by enclosing body parts in adjustable geometric boxes. He provides a specific step-by-step guide for rotating a face in perspective using a grid of parallel and transverse lines.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, geometric drawing, human proportion, anatomy for artists, quadrilaterals, head rotation, artistic technique</keywords>

--- Page 244 ---
<meta>Dürer continues his instruction on how the human form is modified by movement. Having established that lines, surfaces, and solids all bend and twist, he now introduces a specific geometric method—using rectangular grids—to help artists accurately map these changes in perspective.</meta>

In the same way that we turned the **principal face** <note>the standard, straight-on view of the head</note> so that it looked back, as it were, I will now next show the method by which, with the top of the head tilted back and the chin raised, the head is positioned so that it looks up, as it were. Let the method be as follows. 

Using the **square** <note>the geometric grid frame</note> of the **profile face** <note>original: *obliquæ faciei*; the side view</note> constructed according to the teachings written above, you shall place the lower angle of the back side upon a somewhat long **transverse line** <note>a horizontal line</note> **x.y.**; however, you shall raise the lower angle of the front side where the chin is. Afterward, you shall place another somewhat long transverse line **parallel** <note>original: *παραλληλως*</note> to line **x.y.** above so that it touches the protruding angle of the square; let this be **k.k.** 

To this, you shall then add the **frontal face** looking upward in this manner. Between those two long transverse lines, you will mark out the space for the total width of the frontal face with two **vertical lines** <note>original: *perpendicularibus*</note> **a.b.** And within these, you will also distribute the remaining lines according to their markers, as they are placed in the principal face, namely **c. d. e. f. g. h. i. k.**, by which the parts of the width are designated. Then, by means of a transfer <note>likely using a straightedge or compass to carry heights across from one drawing to the other</note>, draw all the transverse lines from the profile face into the **quadrilateral** <note>four-sided frame</note> of the frontal face placed next to it, starting from the highest...

<summary>This page explains a geometric method for drawing a human head tilted upward by projecting lines from a profile view onto a frontal grid. It demonstrates Dürer’s use of coordinate-like systems to maintain anatomical proportion during complex perspective shifts.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, perspective, geometry, anatomical drawing, foreshortening, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 245 ---
<meta>Dürer continues his explanation of how to map the features of the head onto a grid. In the previous section, he established the primary horizontal and vertical lines for a face looking upward. He now concludes the list of specific features and explains that the same geometric principles apply to a face looking downward and, eventually, to the entire human body.</meta>

...of the head, forehead, eyebrows, nose, ears, cheeks, chin, neck, and any others that may be required. Thus, these **lattices** <note>original: *cancelli*; the grid lines used for measurement</note> will easily show you in which places you ought to position the parts of the upward-looking face. However, it will be a matter for your own diligence in the expression of the form to notice without fail which features fall into which specific location. Moreover, just as an upward-looking face is fashioned by this method, so too a downward-looking face may be drawn if you treat the **square of the profile face** <note>original: *quadratum obliquæ faciei*; the side-view grid</note> as though it were looking downward. To make these things clearer, we have placed below our teaching both an upward-looking and a downward-looking face, fully drawn.

In both types of face, it will also be permitted to employ a **turning** <term>versionem: a rotation or "version" of the head where it looks back or to the side</term>—that is, to make them as if they are looking back while in this pose. This very act of changing them appears marvelous in a finished work. For this method in either direction, **projections** <term>depressiones: the geometric technique of dropping points from a 3D-angled form onto a 2D baseline</term> will serve, whether they belong to upward-looking or downward-looking faces. In such a variation of projections, it is wonderful to say how much **skill** <note>original: *atis*, likely a shorthand for *artis*</note> is mastered, and how much certainty in all things becomes apparent. Nor should it be thought that these methods have a place only in faces, since they can be adapted to the entire body as well. For just as we have taught the face to be projected, so, if the matter requires it, we shall perform the projection of the whole body. But before this is undertaken, the gesture of the image must be established, and all the bends and flexions composed according to the artist's will; and then, from individual points and parts, lines shall be dropped **perpendicularly** for the projection.

--- Page 246 ---
<meta>Dürer continues his technical instruction on how to manipulate the human figure using a geometric grid. Having previously explained how to map the features of a static head, he now moves into "flexions"—the art of posing the body by bending and rotating joints within the coordinate system. This page bridges the gap between static proportion and dynamic figure drawing.</meta>

So that our teaching on **flexions** <term>flexionum: the bending or articulation of the body's joints to create a natural pose</term> may be more correctly understood, and to show what kind of images result from them, I will now proceed to present the primary **male figure** <note>original: *imaginem virilem*</note>. This figure is seven head-lengths tall and is to be bent in those places where bending is appropriate. Therefore, let the described **profile face looking downward** <note>original: *facies despiciens obliqua*</note> be taken and placed upon the torso of the **frontal figure** <note>original: *aduersæ effigiei*</note>. He will then appear to be looking toward his own left side. Conversely, you shall place the frontal downward-looking face upon the torso of the profile figure; for the primary figure must be prepared in two versions. After this, you shall bend the body above the loins toward the left side, and an elevation will appear: the right shoulder will be higher than the left. Furthermore, you shall bend along the spine through certain **transverse lines** <note>original: *transuersis*; the horizontal grid lines used for measurement</note>, so that the body is not inclined in a single straight line. And you shall direct the left arm so that it appears to be extended. Below the loins, you shall bend the hip slightly toward the left side. And you shall fix the left foot in a position corresponding to the head, so that he appears to stand upon it. You shall direct the right foot, however, so that it extends a little toward the right side. And you shall bend the shin itself below the knee as if drawing it back, so that in this part he appears to stand on his toes.

When the frontal image has been outlined in this manner, the profile image is then adapted, with all the transverse lines being transferred by the skill of the draftsman. By these lines, the locations of all parts are clearly demonstrated. Following these, a matching form must be expressed anew.

You shall follow a similar method in the bending of the **female figure** <note>original: *muliebris imaginis*</note> adapted to the male one above, but we have shown a contrary gesture in our example. That is, a face looking upward. Let this face also look toward the left side, just like that of the man. Just as we ordered his body to be bent toward that same side, so we instruct that this female body be bent in the opposite direction—that is, to the right—while still preserving the character of the female physique. Once this is done, you shall resort to the aid of the transferrer in drawing the lines, and you shall act just as we showed should be done with the male figure. We have expressed examples of these things clearly below, by the contemplation of which anyone may be instructed. But it should be understood that these principles do not pertain only to this specific method of use; they also show a way to create other and very different poses, as anyone may please. Furthermore, it should be known that individual bent parts on the transverse lines can also be **rotated** <note>original: *verti*; turned on an axis</note> according to what is appropriate for the subject. For example: if the left male shoulder is bent toward the hip, you can also turn that same shoulder and, as it were, rotate it backward, and you can move the right shoulder forward, as if that part of the body were moving as one and following the gesture. You may do this everywhere, and it will be permitted to use all these techniques more extensively than this writing describes.

<summary>This page explains how to use a coordinate grid to depict a human figure in motion, specifically detailing how to "flex" or bend the male and female forms at the waist, spine, and limbs while maintaining correct proportions.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, anatomy, flexion, movement, coordinate grid, male figure, female figure, Renaissance drawing techniques, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 247 ---
<meta>Dürer has just concluded his explanation of how to bend a frontal figure (one facing the viewer) to the left or right. He now transitions into "profile flexions"—the art of posing the body from a side-view, specifically focusing on forward and backward movements using his "slender" 10-head-tall model.</meta>

But now that we have run through the method of **flexion** <term>flexionis: the bending or articulation of the joints to create a pose</term> for the frontal image to either side, I will next set forth the manner of bending forward and backward in the **profile image** <note>original: *obliqua imagine*; literally an "oblique" or side-view image</note>, which must first be established. 

Just as we used the primary figure described in the first book and first section above, let the **male and female figures** described in the same book but in the final section now be taken for bending—specifically those figures whose height is completed by ten of their own head-lengths. This contrast will be very evident when comparing the extreme shortness and thickness of the previous figures with the extreme height and **slenderness** <note>original: *gracilitatis*; Dürer's "slender" type is often 10 heads tall, whereas his "sturdy" type is 7 or 8</note> of these two.

At the start, you shall make this primary figure stand as if on the right foot, but you shall pull back the left, with which the buttocks also follow; the knee being bent, the shin shall be drawn back so that in this part it appears to stand on the toes. After this, starting from the line of the navel, you shall begin to bend the **spine** backward, curving it somewhat, and above near the collarbones, you shall extend the curve beyond the straight vertical line that marks the height, so that this flexion clearly takes the shape of a segment of a circle. Then you shall curve the neck well, so that the head is lowered and the face looks downward; however, so that the pose remains balanced and as if poised, let it be in line with the left foot and the right arm extended backward.

You shall vary the flexion of the male image in some places for its female counterpart in this manner. From the waist you shall bend backward through the whole spine up to the neck, and you shall direct the right arm backward. Afterward, from the waist down to the top of the thigh, the buttocks shall be bent backward. In the right hip, you shall bend it so that the right thigh and knee pass beyond the fixed line of height. But below the knee, you shall draw back the shin. Thus you will have balanced the body, with the left foot appearing to stand on the toes. 

Then, with a **transfer-tool** <note>original: *transferente*; likely a pair of dividers or a compass used to map points from one drawing to another</note>, you shall carry across all the **transverse lines** <term>transuersas lineas: the horizontal grid lines that Dürer uses to ensure consistent proportions</term> where the beginnings and ends of heights are marked, from the frontal image into the profile. And you shall adapt the previously described width and thickness, and turn the hips slightly toward the right side; and you will have this new image sketched in a gesture, which you shall complete by drawing in the form, just as you will see I have done. 

I advise again that these are proposed by me in examples as a general doctrine, lest anyone think they are bound by me to this one single method. For anyone will be able to use flexions of images at their own discretion, expressing them as sitting or lying down, and in all gestures appropriate to a living body, in all ways and kinds. Likewise, as I have shown certain flexions in two proposed images—one of shortness and thickness, the other of length and slenderness—so it will be permitted to do in all other images, whose **measurements** <term>commensurationes: the mathematical proportions or ratios of the body parts</term> were demonstrated by us in the previous books.

<summary>Dürer explains how to pose "slender" (10-head-tall) male and female figures in profile, detailing how to curve the spine and balance the weight. He emphasizes that these geometric methods are a flexible "general doctrine" rather than rigid rules, applicable to any pose or body type.</summary>
<keywords>flexion, profile view, body proportion, spinal curve, balance, Albrecht Dürer, anatomical drawing, artistic theory</keywords>

# The Third Book on the Proportion of the Male Figure

<note>original: "Das dritt Buch von der proportion der mannlicher figur." In this third volume of his work, Albrecht Dürer moves beyond static, standing figures to explore "kinetics"—the study of how the human body changes its appearance and mathematical relationships while in motion, bending, or twisting.</note>

->[Anatomical woodcut illustrations of a male figure: on the left, a profile view of a man leaning or walking forward; on the right, a corresponding frontal view. The figure on the right holds a pair of drafting dividers. Horizontal measurement lines extend across both figures to map anatomical points.]<-

<note>The illustration demonstrates the application of geometric principles to the body. By using horizontal lines to connect the profile and frontal views, Dürer shows how the "height" of specific anatomical markers (like the knees or shoulders) shifts when the figure leans forward. The presence of the dividers—a tool for precise measurement—emphasizes the scientific nature of his artistic method.</note>
