--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page transitions from specific anatomical measurements of the female form to the underlying geometric theory of movement. It introduces Dürer’s method of using stacked square blocks to explain how body parts—such as the torso or limbs—rotate, twist, and bend in three-dimensional space.</meta>

In the following pages, I shall take up the method of **bending** <note>original Latin: *curuandi*</note> figures. Likewise, I will address **turning** <note>original: *uersionis*; referring to rotation</note>, **entwining** <note>original: *implexionis*; the way limbs cross or interweave</note>, **aligning** <note>original: *directionis*</note>, and **twisting** <note>original: *contorsionis*</note>. I intend to show some use for these, just as we did previously with inflections and turnings. I will explain these concepts using two **bodies** <note>In this context, Dürer uses "bodies" (*corporibus*) to mean three-dimensional geometric solids, specifically cubes or blocks.</note>; for I trust that in this way the matter will be understood most correctly. These will be **square blocks** <note>original: *quadrata corpora*</note> placed one upon the other.

However, let the readers remember that in this section, a **surface** <note>a flat, 2D plane</note> is one thing, and a **body** <note>a solid, 3D volume</note> is another. Now, therefore, consider—if you understand these bodies to be soft—how they might be drawn out, aligned, or twisted, and shaped into the other aforementioned modes. Let the demonstration of these things be as follows.

Let two square blocks be established in the place of some limb. You shall mark the touching surface of each block with certain points so that the distinction between them is clear. For example, mark the four corners of the lower surface of the **upper block** with the letters **a, b, c, d**. Then, mark the four corners of the upper surface of the **lower block** with the numbers **1, 2, 3, 4**. Let a center point, **o**, be placed in the middle of both. In all their movements, these blocks should always remain joined at this point and never move away from it.

After this, you shall place beneath these a square that serves as a **foundation** <note>original: *fundamentum*</note>—this is that "depression of the figure" <note>This refers to the ground plan or a flattened projection of the object on the page.</note> of which we have often spoken. You shall mark its corners with letters and numbers as the logic of the drawn straight lines requires, using the benefit of the **transfer tool** <note>original: *transferentis*; likely referring to a ruler or a method of geometric projection used to move points from a ground plan to a perspective drawing</note>. Thus, one corner will receive the marks **a, 1**; another **b, 2**; the third **c, 3**; and the fourth **d, 4**. We have set all of these out below, expressed with their own lines and marks.

Now, let the goal be to **turn the upper block to the side**. By this rotation, all its corners are moved away from the corners of the square beneath it. Therefore, in the square of the foundation (or the "depression"), let this be done: let corner **a** appear to move away from corner **1**, **b** from **2**, **c** from **3**, and **d** from **4**. If you then project lines upward from these displaced corners using the transfer tool, the **turning of the upper square block** will immediately appear. We have clearly illustrated this alongside the blocks themselves and the underlying foundation.

The logic of this **turning** <note>original: *uersionis*</note> has a place in all the transverse lines that define the parts of the human body, in order to express any natural gestures. It is from this very method that the description of a **turned face** seen from above was derived.

->[Four technical geometric diagrams illustrating the rotation and projection of square blocks, used to explain the movement and twisting of human body parts in art.]<-

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric principles of movement by using stacked cubes as models for body parts, demonstrating how rotation can be mapped from a ground plan to a three-dimensional form.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, rotation, twisting, anatomical movement, square blocks, projection, perspective, artistic theory</keywords>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric explanation of how three-dimensional "blocks" (representing parts of the human body) can be rotated and tilted. It follows the introduction of the basic square blocks and the central point "o" from the previous page.</meta>

<sig>i 2</sig>

But once the upper square has been turned so that the corners are shifted, the student of these matters should also experiment with **tilting** <note>original Latin: *inclinationem*</note>, so that the sides are made oblique; and shortly after, he should also attempt a **projection** <term>projection<gloss>depressionem; in Renaissance geometry, this refers to the "footprint" or ground plan of a three-dimensional object projected onto a flat surface</gloss></term> on the underlying **foundation** <note>original: *fundamento*; the base plane or drawing surface</note>. To ensure that all corners are clearly marked in this projection, let certain letters also be assigned to the upper surface of the upper square; let these be **e, f, g, h**, while the corners of the lower surface are **a, b, c, d**. If you complete these steps correctly, the resulting figure of the whole will be such as we have placed below, explained on the foundation by the variation of straight lines, which are usually produced through the skill of the **transferrer** <term>transferrer<gloss>transferentis; the person or tool using geometric lines to move points from one view—like a side view—to another, such as a ground plan</gloss></term>.

Next, with the upper square turned in two directions and tilted to the side as we have shown, a third method of **leaning forward** <note>original: *proclinationis*</note> of the upper square should be attempted. However, the point **o**, which we established as the center, must always remain unmoved, unless perhaps some necessary use requires it to be moved. Thus, that square will be rotated and bent both to the side and forward simultaneously. When the projection on the foundation emerges through the skill of the transferrer, the figure will then be just as the example placed below contains.

->[Diagram showing a tilted cube with diagonal internal bracing. It is labeled with letters e, f, g, h on the top surface, a, b, c, d on the bottom, and numbers 1, 2, 3, 4 on the midsection lines.]<-

->[Diagram showing the same cube in a more extreme compound tilt, leaning both forward and to the side, labeled with the same alphabetic and numeric system.]<-

<summary>This page explains how to project the "footprint" of a tilted or rotated cube onto a flat plane, a fundamental technique for drawing the human body in complex poses. Dürer demonstrates how to track the movement of corners using a lettered coordinate system (a-h) and a fixed central axis (point o).</summary>
<keywords>perspective, geometric projection, ground plan, spatial rotation, coordinate system, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance drawing techniques</keywords>

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric instruction on how to manipulate three-dimensional blocks. While the previous page focused on the rotation and tilting of an upper block upon a stationary base, this page explains that both the upper and lower blocks can be moved simultaneously to model more complex physical movements.</meta>

Just as from our previous teaching—where two squares were placed upon one another—the method of **turning** and **bending** <note>original: *versionis flexionisqȝ*</note> forward, backward, and to the side could be understood, so it should be known that the same handling must be applied to the lower square. And just as one can be moved by the other, it is also permitted to move both together, according to what the specific use and subject matter require. 

But once someone has sketched these, a marvelous "discourse" of sides, angles, and surfaces appears within these stationary and **compressed** <term>compressed<gloss>depressis; literally "pushed down," referring to the perspective projection of a 3D object onto a flat ground plan</gloss></term> bodies, as you can see. For in some places the bodies are, as it were, folded into themselves; in others, they seem to be pulled apart and unfolded. When these things happen in **soft bodies** <note>original: *mollibus*; this likely refers to how human flesh and muscle behave when the underlying "blocks" of the skeleton move</note>, the directions and **twists** <term>twists<gloss>contorsiones; the spiraling or torsional movement of flesh around a rigid axis</gloss></term> are observed on the opposite side. From this it follows that what is taken away from one part is added to another, so that what you might think has disappeared is actually filling out <note>original: *expleantur*; meaning the mass of the body is conserved but redistributed during movement</note> a different area.

Furthermore, it should not be overlooked that the same principle of **bending** and **turning** <note>original: *flexionis et verſionis*</note> that we demonstrated with straight lines can also be applied to a **soft body** with lines, sides, and **surfaces** <note>original: *superficiebus*</note> curved according to one’s preference. To make these matters clear, I will add **curvatures** <note>original: *curuationes*</note> in the sides and surfaces to some of the previous bends and turns, so that nothing may hinder anyone’s understanding.

<summary>This page explains how to move both upper and lower geometric blocks simultaneously to simulate complex body movements, noting how "soft" flesh redistributes its mass when the underlying structure bends or twists.</summary>
<keywords>flexion, version, geometric blocks, anatomy, soft bodies, curvatures, perspective projection, body mass redistribution</keywords>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric instruction on how to manipulate three-dimensional blocks to represent the human form. While the previous page discussed the redistribution of mass when blocks are moved, this page introduces more complex distortions like twisting and the use of ground plans (projections) to achieve realistic postures.</meta>

<sig>f 3</sig>

But even once these differences in **bending** <term>bending<gloss>flexus; the tilting of a block on its axis</gloss></term> and **turning** <term>turning<gloss>versio; the rotation of a block</gloss></term> have been understood and demonstrated in those squared bodies, there still remains **interweaving** <term>interweaving<gloss>implexio; a complex twisting where the surfaces of the block are distorted diagonally</gloss></term>, the knowledge of which must not be neglected. This **interweaving** occurs when lateral lines are drawn from corner to corner, which causes the surface itself to be "entangled" at the same time. This logic is not different from that of the **curves** <term>curves<gloss>curuatio</gloss></term> indicated above, except that interweaving mixes the lines together, whereas those curves are drawn, as has been said, in a "screw-like" <note>original: *terebellatim*; referring to the spiraling motion of an auger or gimlet tool</note> fashion. Furthermore, there is a free use of interweaving by which it is brought about that nothing seems to retain its original shape. Likewise, in a **flattened projection** <term>flattened projection<gloss>depressio; the 2D ground plan or "footprint" of the 3D shape as seen from above</gloss></term>, something that has been interwoven appears to have a foundation far different from its original one, as can be seen in our provided illustrations. This happens by necessity, as a change in the figure produces a different **projection**. Here it must be known that things which are rotated must be rotated in the projection so that the figure may be raised up from it. However, things that are bent must be bent within the figure itself, so that they may be lowered into the projection.

Now, as to why I have used two squared bodies in the demonstration of these matters: let no one think that these things do not also happen to other shapes. For these boxes do not always have to be square, but can sometimes be raised and **elongated** <term>elongated<gloss>prolongatæ; stretched out</gloss></term>—that is, longer in any other part—and even many-sided or **polygonal** <term>polygonal<gloss>polygona</gloss></term>. Depending on the height of the part and the form to be designed, it may happen that neither the sides, nor the surfaces, nor the angles retain equality or straightness, nor does anything appear similar to itself in any part of the body. If anyone takes these things in hand to practice them, they will understand that I have not made mention of this in vain. Moreover, the logic for bending and turning a figure that has been changed in any way is clearly the same as that which we explained regarding the squared bodies. Furthermore, a person following these principles will be able to enclose the entire **human body** within an angular figure, though one in which there is the greatest inequality. The same use will be found in many other things. For you will be able to turn and bend all natural things, once they are sketched in their proper state, according to the transverse lines that signify the parts or limbs—either to the side, or forward, or backward—and designate their locations with square or angular shapes, modeling the gesture of the image at your will: standing, sitting, or lying down.

Thus, in a proposed image, you may design all the parts with square or angular shapes, and afterward, at your own discretion, seek out the bends and turns. In this way, those differences mentioned above—of directing, twisting, curving, and interweaving—will be found, by which the logic and path of the work are opened. Nothing more useful than this can be shown to those students of the art of **carving marble or wood** <note>original: *marmora aut ligna scalpandi*; this confirms Dürer's method was intended for sculptors as much as painters to ensure three-dimensional accuracy</note> to achieve certainty.

<summary>Dürer explains the advanced geometric manipulation of "blocks" to model the human body, introducing "interweaving" (torsion) and explaining how 3D movements relate to 2D ground plans (projections). He emphasizes that these principles apply to any shape and are essential for sculptors to master realistic poses like sitting or reclining.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, geometry in art, perspective projection, torsion, sculpture techniques, anatomical modeling, Albrecht Dürer, 16th-century art theory</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how to use geometric blocks to map the human body. It transitions from the theory of "interweaving" on the previous page to the practical application of these blocks on a male figure, introducing cross-sections and the "Transferrer" method.</meta>

From this method, one can identify on every **angular surface** <term>angular surface<gloss>superficie angulari; the flat facets of the geometric blocks used to "box in" the body</gloss></term> those parts that ought to be carved away, so that neither too much nor too little remains. However, skilled and practiced craftsmen do not usually occupy themselves with these calculations except in grand and singular works. They are accustomed to marking the curves with points on a drawn image, and in this way, they soon fashion the varied and changed form anew. 

Regarding these matters, it does not escape me how difficult it is to encompass this teaching in writing, and how it might cause weariness for the reader and student because of the great number of points and marks, and the wordiness of the repetitions. Truly, there are many **shortcuts** <note>original: *compendia*; Dürer often uses this to refer to simplified methods or geometric summaries</note> which those following these instructions might use to feel less fatigued and easily reach the end, without which the difficulty of this art would seem insurmountable. Therefore, whoever wishes to exercise themselves much in the use and experience of these things will daily find something more expedient and easier. For this work, nothing would be more desirable than a **good teacher**; those things which must be learned without an interpreter usually produce manifold more labor and exhaustion.

We have placed below an example of these things we have discussed, modeled on a **male figure** <term>male figure<gloss>imagine virili</gloss></term>. That image will be the one described above in the second book, whose height was indeed nine lengths of its own head, but since the head was made slightly taller, it did not strictly maintain the measure of nine heads. Therefore, we have enclosed the profile and front-facing form of this image on all sides with squared and angular shapes; these themselves emerge as higher or lower from the **transverse divisions** <term>transverse divisions<gloss>transuersis distinctionibus; horizontal cross-sections of the body</gloss></term> and become unequal because of the shape of the parts to which they are fitted. 

We have also divided the height of this image into ten angular figures by nine sections. These are the names of the sections:
*   The transverse line of the **Chin**
*   Of the **Shoulders**
*   Of the **Collarbones** <note>original: *Iugulorum*; the hollow of the throat or collarbone area</note>
*   Of the **Breasts**
*   Of the **Waist** <gloss>cinctus</gloss>
*   Of the **Hips**
*   Of the **Top of the Thigh**
*   Of the **Knee**
*   Of the **Ankle below the shin** <note>original: *Imitali sub tibia*; likely a printer's error for *initiali* or *infima*, referring to the base of the leg</note>

But for the sake of greater clarity, we have set out the entire form of the image enclosed on all sides by the quadrangular figures of the lines, both in profile and facing forward, standing upright and erect without a bend, with the right arm extended and the left hanging down. In the other section, however, you will see only the quadrangular figures of the lines standing upon one another, with the modeling of the form omitted. Afterward, you will see these same quadrangles placed upon themselves and enclosing the image, flattened below in the **ground plan** <term>ground plan<gloss>fundamento; the "footprint" or 2D projection of the 3D shape on the floor</gloss></term> by means of the **Transferrer** <note>original: *beneficio transferentis*; referring to Dürer's specific geometric method or instrument for projecting 3D coordinates onto a 2D plane</note>, just as you saw done separately for the head above. 

Afterward, we have described the individual **roundings** <gloss>rotundationes; the circular or elliptical cross-sections of the body</gloss> of those sections separately, corresponding to their quadrangles, which must be sought out in both the front-facing and profile figure. From this, it appears what ought to be removed from the quadrangular surface in each individual section. At this point, it should be noted that when one quadrangular surface in the same section belongs to the upper body and another to the lower body, they are placed upon one another. Then it is understood that both have one and the same **contour** <gloss>circumductionem</gloss>. 

We have also marked the quadrangles of the sections, set out separately, with **grid-like lines** <gloss>cancellatim</gloss>, so that if any part is turned or bent, it can be properly shaped from these. For in some places it happens that the quadrangles are elongated so that they are compressed, and in other places **rhombuses** <note>original: *Rhombos*; diamond shapes created when a square block is viewed at an angle or twisted</note> are created; then, therefore, it will be necessary to properly round the parts according to these forms. When the quadrangles become rhombuses, the lines drawn in a grid-like fashion consequently fall according to that figure. Furthermore, when something that was wide is made narrow, it is shortened by the aid of the **Variator** <note>original: *Variant*; another of Dürer's geometric tools/methods for altering proportions</note>, the diagram of which we expressed in the earlier book. Afterward, we have as if piled together all these sections, flattened in the ground plan, with the quadrangles omitted, so that it could be seen how they fall upon one another, and to what extent one exceeds the other.

<summary>This page explains the practical application of geometric "boxing" to a male figure, detailing how to divide the body into ten sections and use ground plans to map its three-dimensional volume. It introduces technical tools like the "Transferrer" and the "Variator" to help artists handle complex poses and foreshortening.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometry in art, cross-sections, ground plan, Transferrer, Variator, male figure, anthropometry</keywords>

# Proportions of the Male Figure

->**Frontal and Profile Diagrams with Geometric Construction**<-

By using the **Transferrer** <note>original: *Exempler*; Dürer’s specific term for a tool or method used to transfer measurements from a drawing into a three-dimensional form, such as a woodcarving or stone statue</note>, one may locate every point of the body within a three-dimensional space. ### The Geometric Method
By reducing the organic curves of the human form into these **stereometric blocks** <term>stereometry<gloss>the science of measuring volume and three-dimensional bodies</gloss></term>, the artist can maintain perfect proportions even when the figure is foreshortened or viewed from difficult angles. Each block corresponds to a specific anatomical segment—from the crown of the head to the sole of the foot—allowing for a mathematical reconstruction of the human "type" described in our earlier chapters.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion." The previous page was a blank recto; we now move into the descriptive text accompanying his geometric and anatomical diagrams, specifically focusing on how to represent the human body's segments as three-dimensional blocks that can be tilted and rotated.</meta>

### Head. &emsp; Chin. &emsp; Shoulder blades. &emsp; Throat.

### Breasts. &emsp; Waist. &emsp; Hip. &emsp; Thigh.

### Knee. &emsp; Bottom of the ankle. &emsp; Sole.

After this, it is pleasing to investigate what the difference in appearance of these sections might be when they no longer stand straight upon one another on their quadrangular surfaces, but have been moved as if by an impulse. For the sake of clarifying these things, let an example be proposed of a <term>quadrangular section<note>Dürer visualizes the body as a series of stacked blocks or "sections" to simplify complex anatomy into geometry.</note></term> that occurs between the breasts and the waist. Let this solid quadrangle be placed first upon an oblique straight form on a <term>transverse line<note>A horizontal reference line used in Renaissance drafting to align different views of an object.</note></term>, and you will mark the upper angles where it was cut from the breasts with letters: on the front part, **a. b.**; on the back part, **c. d.** But where it was cut from the waist, you will mark the front **1. 2.** and the back **3. 4.** From here, you will place beneath it its <term>projection<note>original: *depressio*; in this context, it refers to the "ground plan" or the flat geometric footprint of the three-dimensional block.</note></term>, marked itself in a similar way with the same signs of letters and numbers, so that all things may correspond to one another.

Then, the quadrangle placed upon the oblique straight form on the transverse line shall be bent forward according to the marks of the spine. And its projection shall also be placed beneath it, and each of the angles shall be marked with their own letters and numbers. Thus, this leaning body will have received a new projection. Then, let this projection in the front part where the chest is be turned directly facing the viewer and squarely to the side beneath the aforementioned transverse line. Once this is done, let lines be drawn upward from all the marks of letters and numbers beyond the transverse line. Soon, across these upright lines (as we said) from their letters and numbers, you will draw transverse lines from the quadrangle of the oblique form placed on the transverse line, as was said at the beginning; and this will be achieved through the benefit of a <term>transfer<note>Likely referring to the use of a proportional compass or "transferring" measurements from one view to another to maintain scale.</note></term>. Let each of the letters and numbers be noted, and all things will be clearly established; and so the lines of the sides shall be described, and that version seen from the side will appear. And now this part will be bent forward and turned to the side. Then afterwards, this very part—so bent and turned—shall be bent toward the right side upon the transverse lines through the marks of the spine; and from all the letters and numbers of the angles, of this straight part and the part bent forward, and likewise the part turned and

...bent to the side, you will draw down perpendicular lines, and through those, you will draw transverse lines from all the letters and numbers of the <term>true projection<note>original: *depressionis veræ*; this refers to the flat ground plan or footprint of the body part.</note></term> with the help of a transfer tool. Everywhere these lines will be seen to intersect one another at their corresponding letters and numbers; in those places, draw lateral boundary lines, and you will find that everything matches. Within these <term>grids<note>original: *cancellos*; literally "lattices." Dürer uses a coordinate grid system to map 3D shapes onto a 2D surface.</note></term>, you will distribute the newly formed quadrangles of the oblique and front-facing sections, according to the width and thickness of the upper and lower parts; this shall be achieved by the help of the <term>shifter<note>original: *varians*; Dürer’s specific term for a geometric guide or scale used to calculate how dimensions change when an object is tilted or rotated.</note></term>. Finally, you will draw the resulting shape through the grids in both the upright and the projected parts, just as it ought to be drawn, and as you see we have done. Just as I have taught how this specific part can be handled, so may anyone do for all parts of the body. It is necessary to have these preparations ready in order to capture figures in their natural poses, especially when one wishes to represent them as if they were receding from the viewer’s sight.

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s geometric instructions on how to manipulate body parts in three-dimensional space using a system of blocks or "quadrangles." The previous page concluded with the general principle of using a "shifter" and a coordinate grid to find the correct proportions for tilted or foreshortened figures.</meta>

<margin>f</margin>
<meta>catchword: which</meta>

But now that this method has been demonstrated through the example of the larger part of the proposed human figure—specifically the section between the breasts and the waist—one can understand from this how all the other parts connected to it should be handled when their position is altered from a straight line. Therefore, this same method will now be applied in turn to the tilts of all the parts, each enclosed within its own <term>quadrangles<note>original: *quadrangulis*; Dürer uses these boxes as a simplified geometric scaffold to help artists rotate the human form in perspective.</note></term>. We shall set their position according to our choice and vary them, leaving everyone the freedom to act as they wish. And regarding these matters, if anything suitable or useful was explained previously, we shall revisit it now. 

Furthermore, as we have warned before, diligence is needed here, because when all the parts are moved within their quadrangles, their shapes in the descriptions—both in the upright and the <term>projected view<note>original: *depressarum*; refers to the "ground plan" or top-down footprint of the body part on the paper.</note></term>—become different from what they were before. First, a part is usually tilted forward or backward; then it is turned to the side, and soon after it is also tilted to the side—all of which can be appropriately derived using this method. 

If anything previously stated is found repeated in what follows, know that it was done out of necessity, so that the subsequent teaching might harmonize with what went before. For a short while, I even considered being content with that single example, but I feared that the remaining points might be poorly understood from it. I will propose at the beginning the starting part of the figure—that is, the <term>quadrangle of the head<note>original: *quadrangulum capitis*; Dürer treats the head as a cube to simplify the complex geometry of its rotation.</note></term>, the treatment of which will be similar to the treatments of the other parts. 

First, the four upper corners are marked with the letters **a, b, c, d**. The lower ones with the numbers **1, 2, 3, 4**. Thus, **a** corresponds to **1**, **b** to **2**, **c** to **3**, and **d** to **4**. Afterward, looking at the <term>oblique view<note>original: *obliquam faciem*; a profile or three-quarter view of the head.</note></term>, once it is moved forward from the height-line of the figure, I tilt it forward so that it looks down; and from this, a projection must be made, so that it may be known how much further the upper parts protrude than the lower ones. These are marked with the letter **A**. 

Afterward, I turn the projection toward me so that it becomes a front-facing view looking at the viewer, and I turn it slightly from the front toward the right side. And so I raise vertical lines from all the points of the letters and numbers. Then, from the letters and numbers of the downward-looking oblique view (marked **A**) placed nearby, I draw transverse lines across those vertical lines with the help of the <term>transferrer<note>original: *transferentis*; a geometric guide or secondary drawing used to move measurements from one view to another.</note></term>. Where the sections from the letters and numbers intersect, the corners are designated—the letters above and the numbers below. 

When I have joined the marks with their sides, the front view appears, derived from that oblique one tilted forward and turned to the side, showing both the upright form and the projection. These themselves are marked with the letter **B**, but furthermore, it must also be understood how this part **B** appears in the <term>representations<note>original: *effictionibus*; finished drawings or depictions of the body.</note></term> when it is both slanted and upright. For it is necessary to know what form anything takes in a representation, whether in profile or from the front. 

Now, therefore, this part **B**, tilted forward in the front view and turned to the side, will then be slanted in this way: You shall turn back the projection that was facing me (to make it look forward), as I said, evenly so that it becomes oblique. Soon you will raise lines from the letters and numbers of this projection using the transferrer; and wherever the front-facing, tilted, and turned view is nearby, you will bring across transverse lines from all the points of the numbers and letters using the transferrer, passing through those lines raised from the projection. And so where you see the lines crossing one another, you will place the marks of the letters and numbers and join the sides. Thus you will have the projected and upright form of this part, to which you will add the label of letter **C**. 

From these, next, this same part must be found tilted forward, turned to the side, and also tilted to the side—a method we touched on somewhat above, but now you shall proceed as follows: You will tilt the upright part **B** toward the right side (having already turned it toward that same side), and since you will have left its projection placed beneath it, you will afterward make that projection match this tilt. For it must be varied so that the upper corners **a, b, c, d** seem to fall toward the right side, while the lower sides **1, 2** and **3, 4** are narrowed...

<summary>This page details Dürer's rigorous geometric method for rotating the human head in space by treating it as a labeled quadrangle (cube). He explains how to derive front, profile, and tilted views using vertical and transverse lines transferred from a ground plan.</summary>
<keywords>quadrangles, geometric perspective, head anatomy, projection, transferrer, Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, three-dimensional drawing</keywords>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Albrecht Dürer’s geometric method of foreshortening. The previous page ended with the introduction of how to use a "transferrer" to shift coordinates between different planes to represent a body part in a tilted or turned position.</meta>

exactly as with the upper parts, a, b, and c, d. This will be done in this manner. By using the <term>transferrer<note>original: *transferente*; a coordinate-shifting tool or ruler Dürer used to map points from one view to another.</note></term>, you shall lead perpendicular lines from the letters of the upper angles a, b, c, and d into the <term>depression view<note>original: *depressionem*; in Dürer's geometry, this is the "ground plan" or horizontal projection—like looking at the top of the block from above.</note></term>, and the <term>foreshortening<note>original: *abscissio*; literally a "cutting off," referring to how a line appears shorter when tilted away from the viewer.</note></term> and lengthening of the lines a, b, and c, d will immediately appear. For they are revealed by the lines of the letters dropped from above into the depression view. In a similar way, lines are also dropped from the points of the numbers. By these same means, it is shown how much narrower the sides 1, 2, and 3, 4 become in this depression than they had been before. And thus you will see what kind of bend and, as it were, <term>backward tilt<note>original: *reiectio*; the "throwing back" or receding of a plane in perspective.</note></term> exists in the depression view as compared to the upright part. To this, you shall add the mark of the letter **D.** Because it will be necessary to have understood this work—specifically how it appears in an oblique view—you shall achieve it thus: you will establish the depression of this part **D** obliquely, just as was said above regarding part **C.** Once this is done, you shall raise lines from all the points of the letters and numbers by the benefit of the transferrer; afterwards, from the part marked with the letter **D**, you shall lead transverse lines across the upright ones. And in those places where you see the lines intersect, you shall place their corresponding marks of numbers and letters. Once the sides are drawn in, you will have the oblique form of this part, to which the mark of the letter **E** should be applied. And thus, at last, you will have the oblique and <term>facing<note>original: *aduersa*; the front-facing or "elevation" view.</note></term> versions of this part completed, which will be of use in the work.

After this, set out the <term>quadrangle<note>original: *quadrangulum*; the geometric box or "block" that Dürer uses to simplify the complex anatomy of the body.</note></term> of the neck, and treat it similarly to that method used for the head. But you shall bend it forward a little less, and turn it to the side a little less, and bend it into itself a little less. You shall also mark its angles with the letters a, b, c, d and the numbers 1, 2, 3, 4. And in this way, the forms of the upright and depressed parts will appear, especially in the subsequent calculation of marks **D** and **E**, which are for use in the work. You shall observe these same rules in the other following parts of the image, but you shall bend and turn each of the lower parts less, so that order and consistency are maintained in all things. And from this will appear the twisting of the body toward the side, and that inclination which occurs both forward and to the side.

When, however, by proceeding in this way you reach the part marked by the section of the <term>breasts<note>original: *mammae*.</note></term> and the <term>waist<note>original: *cinctus*; the "girdle" or midsection of the torso.</note></term>, you will leave that part standing upright, but turn it a little toward the right side, and you will express those things which are necessary. Only a triple representation is required: the oblique view on account of the first depression which must be turned and raised, and a third in which the version is made oblique. And you will mark these depressions with the letters **C, D, E**, and not in sequence from **A**, so that **D** and **E** are always coupled. You shall also know this: if you wish to turn a facing depression, you will never be deceived if you first enclose that part which you are about to turn in a rectangular quadrilateral, and thus turn the enclosed part together with the quadrilateral. Or, if you seek the version according to a straight perpendicular line, noticing whether the angles of that part—whether obtuse or acute—go out to the line; however, use the oblique lines as has been shown for the transverse straight lines. The last will be the quadrangular body between the section of the waist and the <term>navel<note>original: *umbilici*.</note></term>, which you shall move forward or bend forward at the section of the navel. And you shall place beneath it its own depression view which, because it is the latter one, you will mark with the letter **E.** The facing part, however, you will mark with the letter **D.** This part is expressed in only two ways <note>original: *bifariam*</note>, because it is bent only once. And indeed, in the facing view, when it has been drawn out by the lines raised from its depression view and the transversals brought in through the transferrer, everything will appear clearly and correctly.

<summary>This page details Dürer's geometric technique for rotating and tilting body segments (the neck, breasts, and waist) using a system of coordinate blocks and "depression" (plan) views. He emphasizes using a bounding box ("rectangular quadrilateral") to ensure accuracy when shifting these parts in three-dimensional space.</summary>
<keywords>foreshortening, perspective, human proportion, geometry, coordinate transfer, anatomy, technical drawing, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

<meta>This page provides the visual diagrams (A-E) that correspond to the geometric transformation techniques described on the previous page. It demonstrates how to visualize the human head as a simplified rectangular solid to master perspective.</meta>

### These rectangular shapes pertain to the head.

->A &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; B &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; C<-

<note>The letters A through E refer to geometric diagrams in the original manuscript. These drawings show how a simple <term>rectangular solid<note>original: *quadrangulata*; four-angled blocks used as anatomical scaffolding.</note></term>—representing the basic mass of the human <term>head<note>original: *caput*.</note></term>—can be tilted, turned, and manipulated according to the rules of <term>perspective<note>original: *perspectiva*; the mathematical method of representing three dimensions on a flat plane.</note></term> and <term>proportion<note>original: *proportio*.</note></term>.</note>

<summary>This page features five geometric diagrams (A-E) that apply Dürer's coordinate-shifting method to the human head, modeled as a rectangular prism. These drawings serve as the foundational scaffolding for accurately drawing the head in complex, tilted positions.</summary>

### These are of the neck. <note>original: *Hæc sunt colli*. Here, the author transitions from modeling the head to modeling the neck as a series of geometric prisms.</note>

-># **A** &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; **B** &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; **C**<-

<note>Diagrams A, B, and C represent the neck from various elevations. These wireframe drawings use <term>perspective<note>The mathematical method of representing three-dimensional objects on a two-dimensional surface.</note></term> to show how the "block" of the neck connects to the base of the skull. The labels (a, b, c, 1, 2, 3) serve as coordinate points for the artist to maintain consistent <term>proportions<note>original: *proportio*.</note></term> when the figure is seen from different angles.</note>

-># **D** &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; **E**<-

<note>Diagrams D and E demonstrate more extreme tilts. In these figures, we see the application of <term>stereometry<note>The science of measuring and representing solid figures; in art, this involves breaking complex organic shapes into simple polyhedra or prisms.</note></term> to solve the problem of <term>foreshortening<note>The visual compression of an object as it recedes in space, which is particularly difficult when drawing the neck and throat area.</note></term>.</note>

<summary>This page presents five geometric diagrams (A-E) that model the human neck as a rectangular prism. These studies allow an artist to calculate the complex movements and rotations of the neck using the laws of perspective.</summary>

<keywords>neck proportions, perspective, stereometry, prisms, polyhedra, Renaissance art theory, geometric modeling, foreshortening</keywords>

## These pertain to the tops of the shoulders. <note>original: *Haec debentur summis Scapulis*. The author is demonstrating how to apply geometric "blocking" to the upper back and shoulder region.</note>

->### **A** &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; **B** &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; **C**<-

<note>Diagrams A, B, and C illustrate the <term>projection<note>The mathematical process of mapping a three-dimensional object onto a two-dimensional surface using geometric rules.</note></term> of the shoulder region from different lateral and frontal rotations. By simplifying the <term>scapulae<note>The shoulder blades; here modeled as rectangular volumes.</note></term> into these rigid forms, the artist can precisely determine how the light falls and how the body occupies space.</note>

->### **D** &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; **E**<-

<note>In diagrams D and E, the artist demonstrates the <term>perspective<note>The technique of representing depth and volume on a flat plane.</note></term> of the shoulders during more complex movements. The alphanumeric labels (1–4 and a–d) indicate the <term>vertices<note>The corner points where the edges of the geometric blocks meet.</note></term>, allowing for a rigorous calculation of <term>geometry<note>The branch of mathematics concerned with the properties and relations of points, lines, and solids.</note></term> in the human figure.</note>

### These pertain to the upper Thorax. <note>original: "Hæc sunt superioris Thoracis." In this context, the <term>thorax<note>The part of the body between the neck and the abdomen; the chest.</note></term> is being treated as a complex volume that can be simplified into geometric shapes.</note>

->**A &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; B &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; C**<-

### These are to be applied below the line. <note>original: "Haec sub lineam applicentur." In the study of <term>linear perspective<note>A system of creating an illusion of depth on a flat surface using a horizon line and vanishing points.</note></term>, "the line" usually refers to the horizon or the ground line. This instruction tells the artist to draw these specific views as if they are situated below the viewer's eye level.</note>

--- Page 280 ---
<meta>This page concludes Book IV of the treatise on human proportion. Having finished the geometric and perspective instructions for the torso, the text transitions to a personal concluding statement followed by memorial poems for the author, Albrecht Dürer.</meta>

If anyone takes this teaching of ours into their hands to be put to use, let them first explain the images piece by piece correctly by measurement and fashion them with suitable stature. Then let them bend them appropriately, and subject them to their own depressions <note>The "depressions" refers to the concave areas of the body or the receding parts of the geometric volumes used in the sketches.</note>, and raise them from there in service to the principles of the **art of perspective**, and strive to produce a praiseworthy and approved work from these. He will easily perceive what fruit and utility is to be hoped for from this; nor do I doubt that he himself, by following these things, will seek out more and even better methods. 

Nor does it escape me that these things will seem difficult to understand and use in many places, but study and diligence will easily assist in this. For the obscurity of writers is always accustomed to be overcome by assiduous reading and care. And at this point, it has pleased me to finish my teaching on these matters; but if God breathes favor upon my endeavors, I will later explain other and more numerous principles of painting in my writings. This is so that our native <note>original: *nostrates*. Refers to German artists, as Dürer was deeply concerned with elevating German art to the standards of the Italian Renaissance.</note> painters may be seen to be strong not only in the use of their hands, but also in the true <term>precepts of art<note>Formal rules and theoretical foundations, as opposed to mere manual skill.</term>, in which matter I think I could do nothing more glorious for God nor more pleasing for our people.

<insert>of</insert> <insert>9</insert>
->End of the Fourth Book.<-

# ELEGY OF WILLIBALD PIRCKHEIMER
### on the death of Albrecht Dürer.
<note>Willibald Pirckheimer (1470–1530) was a prominent Nuremberg humanist and Dürer's closest friend. This poem expresses the deep personal loss felt by the intellectual community at Dürer's passing in 1528.</note>

Albrecht, you who had been most closely joined to me for so many years,
And who were the greatest part of my soul,
With whom I could share sweet conversations
In safety, and pour out my words into a faithful breast.
Why, unhappy one, do you suddenly leave your mourning friend,
And hasten with a swift foot, never to return?
I was not permitted to touch your beloved head, nor your right hand,
Nor to say the final word, "farewell," in sadness.
But you had scarcely surrendered your weary limbs to the bed,
When death, hurrying, suddenly snatched you away.
Alas, deceitful hopes, and a mind ignorant of evils!
How suddenly all things fall in a swift decline.
Favorable fortune had given everything to Dürer:
Genius, beauty, and faith along with integrity.
But death took all things away again with a swift daring;
Yet that wicked power was not able to take away his praises.
For the virtue of Dürer remains, and his renowned fame;
They shall shine as long as the bright stars in the sky.
Go, honor—go, not the least glory of our people,
And seek the celestial kingdoms with CHRIST as your leader.
There you will always rejoice in no vain honor,
Bearing worthy rewards, happy for your merits.
While we, fragile ones, wander in the shadow of death,
And wretchedly glide in an unstable boat,
The grace of Christ will be present to preserve us;
With Him as our leader, we shall enter the journey happily.
Meanwhile, sorrowing, let us pour out tears for our friend,
Than which nothing can be sweeter for those afflicted.
And prayers shall be added, which may appease the high Thunderer <note>original: *tonantem*. A classical poetic epithet for Jupiter, here used by the humanist Pirckheimer to refer to the Christian God.</note>,
If pious vows have any power at all.
And lest anything be missing from the tomb, we shall scatter scents:
Narcissus, violets, lilies, garlands, and roses.
Happy one, meanwhile rest in blessed sleep;
For a good man sleeps in Christ—he does not die.

## EPITAPH FOR THE SAME.

After Albrecht had adorned the world by painting,
And all things were filled with such elegant art,
"Now heaven remains," he said, "so that the heights may be painted."
Soon, leaving the earth, he sought the bright stars.

### ANOTHER.

Genius, integrity, sincerity, prudence, virtue,
Art, piety, and faith: here they lie buried.

### ANOTHER BY THE SAME.

If life could be returned through tears, Albrecht,
Your body would not be covered by this cheap earth.
But because the fates are not moved by tears or weeping,
We pay our funeral rites only through mourning.

<summary>This page concludes Dürer's Fourth Book on human proportion with a final exhortation to students, followed by a series of moving Latin poems written by his friend Willibald Pirckheimer to commemorate Dürer's death.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Willibald Pirckheimer, elegy, epitaph, Renaissance humanism, art theory, perspective, Nuremberg, German art</keywords>

--- Page 281 ---
<meta>This page follows Dürer’s own concluding remarks to his "Four Books on Human Proportion." Having finished his technical instructions, the book concludes with a series of elegiac tributes and epitaphs written by his close circle of humanist friends following his death in April 1528.</meta>

# EPITAPHS FOR ALBRECHT
## Dürer, by Eobanus Hessus. <note>Helius Eobanus Hessus (1488–1540) was a leading German Latin poet and a friend of Dürer.</note>

> In the same tomb where Albrecht Dürer is laid,
> the whole method and theory of painting lies buried.

### ANOTHER.

> If the fates were to wait upon the praise of art and genius,
> it is certain that Dürer could not have died.
> Therefore, he does not yet lie entirely within this tomb;
> while living, he carried the fame of his genius to the stars.

### ANOTHER.

> Why do you hurry? Stop a little while, traveler who reads this,
> here the bones of Dürer rest softly.
> It is worth the effort to behold such a ruin,
> with which all the honor of painting has collapsed.

### ANOTHER.

> What does it profit to have cultivated life through any arts,
> when black death snatches it away in a brief moment?
> No one among painters was equal to Albrecht, but the Fates
> could not long endure him remaining at the summit.
> Therefore death cut short the man’s life with a malignant scythe,
> and said as he died: "Even so, you shall not die."

# TO THE NOBLE ALBRECHT DÜRER,
## painter and Geometer in Nuremberg. He lived 57 years.
### By Joachim Camerarius. <note>Joachim Camerarius (1500–1574) was a great classical scholar and Dürer's first biographer.</note>

> When the Fate <note>original: *Parca*. One of the three Fates who spin and cut the thread of life.</note> was spinning your final thread, Albrecht,
> and was winding up a life full of work,
> Nature herself is said to have spoken thus for you,
> seeking a little more time for your sake:
> "Spare, O fatal sisters, to unroll this task;
> our glory is drawn from that distaff.
> I alone create the living forms of things;
> but this man is learned at fashioning things just like the truth.
> What if our own powers are surpassed by his art,
> and he can do what Mother Nature herself cannot?
> By him I am carried on continuously, and not divided;
> in the midst of winter, spring remains, and the spring of winter.
> He makes it so that those buried are seen among the living;
> through him, posterity beholds ancient ages.
> He makes it so the earth bears all things everywhere,
> and he creates both what people think will be, and what has been."
> Though the Fate wished to grant such things to the one pleading,
> Persephone <note>Queen of the Underworld; in Roman myth, she cuts a lock of hair from the dying to release their soul.</note> had already snatched away the sacred lock of hair.

# Joachim. <note>This is a Greek epigram translated into English.</note>

> To those for whom the hands of Apelles <note>The most famous painter of ancient Greece; Dürer was often called the "German Apelles."</note> once seemed marvelous,
> I would wish they could see these living works.
> As many monuments of art as were left behind by Homer's skill,
> the works of this good painter were as beautifully composed.
> Truly, seeing these, one would say that Apelles was a match
> not only for us, but for the ancients as well.

## To the Blessed Memory.

> To the memory of Albrecht Dürer, a most excellent man and the most accomplished painter of his age, who was the first among the Germans not only to enlarge and illuminate the art of painting and to restrict it to a stricter law <note>This refers to Dürer’s introduction of mathematical rules of proportion and perspective to German art.</note>, but also began to commend it to posterity through writings. For this reason, but especially for his disciplined character, singular prudence, and modesty, he was most dear to his fellow Nurembergers—indeed, to all foreigners—and most welcome to the Divine Emperors Maximilian and his grandson Charles, as well as to Ferdinand, King of Hungary and Bohemia, who supported him with a large annual stipend and followed him with the highest favor. He died, however, not without the great grief of his friends, on the 6th day before the Ides of April <note>April 6, 1528.</note>, in the year 1528, at the age of 57.

-> Willibald Pirckheimer <-
-> placed this for his most upright friend. <-
<note>Willibald Pirckheimer was a wealthy lawyer and humanist, and Dürer's lifelong best friend.</note>

<summary>This page contains a collection of funerary honors for Albrecht Dürer, including poems and a formal memorial inscription by his friends Eobanus Hessus, Joachim Camerarius, and Willibald Pirckheimer. These tributes emphasize Dürer's role as a pioneer who brought mathematical rigor and "divine" naturalism to German art.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Eobanus Hessus, Joachim Camerarius, Willibald Pirckheimer, Nuremberg, funerary epitaphs, Apelles, Holy Roman Empire, Renaissance art theory, memorial</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the "Errata" (list of corrections) and the colophon (publication statement) for the posthumous Latin edition of Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion" (De Symmetria), published in 1534. It follows the elegiac tributes to Dürer found on the previous page.</meta>

Whoever happens to apply themselves to reading these books, may they kindly correct any other errors they notice that were admitted during printing, and especially amend those noted by us. First and foremost, in the preface of the first book, the lines were swapped and the meaning confused during the printing of the work. It should be corrected in this manner: on the back of page a. 3, at line 23, read: "It did not escape me how much more difficult the labor would be with the author dead than it would have been while he was alive, and what would be most contrary, occupied etc." But at the last line and thereafter on the first line of the following sheet, delete these words: "The labor would be with the dead author than it would have been while alive, and what would be most contrary."

Later, in Book 3, on sheet a. 1, at the 2nd line from the bottom, read: *if any*.
On a. 2, line 8, place a period before *nothing*; at line 12, remove the period before *will leave behind*. Also, on the back of the page, at line 11 from the bottom, remove the period after *exit*.
On a. 3, line 3, delete *to*. At line 12, read: *distinguishes*. At line 15, read: *this whole calculation*. At line 24, read: *parallelly* <note>original: "παραλληλως" (parallēlos)</note>. At line 26, read: *of the shortening* <note>The Latin "decurtatio" refers to foreshortening in drawing.</note>. At line 5 from the bottom, read: *by shortening*. On the last "a" sheet, line 1, read: *with extensions*.
On sheet b. 1, on the back, next to the male figure, read thus: *Bottom of the knee. Bottom of the calf, outer side. Bottom of the same, inner side. Top of the foot.*
On b. 2, on the back, at line 7 from the bottom, read: *as a. c. described*.
On c. 2, on the top of the first side, read: *raised*.
On c. 3, on the back, in the writing between the heads, line 2, read: *but two*.
On c. 4, line 12, read: *this* (feminine) not *this* (ablative). At line 25, read: *which* (plural) not *which* (singular). At line 7 from the bottom, read: *more sharply*. On the back, line 6, read: *with thick lips* <note>original: "labeonibus"</note>.
On c. 6, line 20, read: *are worked out*. At line 25, read: *thin things*.
On e. 1, line 1, read: *of the proposed*. On e. 4, on the back, line 1, read: *of perverting*. At line 15, read: *unless suited for use*. At line 12 from the bottom, read: *to place near*.
To the "diversion of the perverter," <note>This likely refers to a specific technical diagram or section title in the book regarding the distortion of figures.</note> assign: *Measurement of the male figure last described in Book 2.*
On f. 3, line 19, read: *themselves*. On the back, line 9, read: *is it to be thought?* At line 21, delete *certain*. At line 29, read: *For in the same way also*.
On f. 4, on the back, line 17 from the bottom, read: *with thin things*. On f. 5, line 14 from the bottom, read: *by no means*.
In Book 3, on g. 2, on the back, line 5, read: *of the designated part*. On g. 3, line 10, read: *of the sinew*.
On g. 4, line 3, read: *or with angular figures*. At line 5 from the bottom, read: *the skull*. On the back, line 2, read: *those things*.
On g. 5, line 3 from the bottom, read: *let us approach*. On the back, line 12, read: *but directs the right*.
On j. 2, column 2, line 2, read: *into the side*. At line 6, read: *always*. On j. 5, line 16, read: *to others these figures*. At the last line, read: *of carving* <note>original: "scalpendi" – likely referring to engraving or woodcutting.</note>. On the back, line 7, read: *easily to the end*. At line 8, read: *it would be, difficult*. At line 11 from the bottom, read: *they are, then they understand*.
On k. 1, line 5 from the bottom, read: *as also in the side*. On the back, line 9, read: *obliquely*.
On k. 4, on the back, line 8, read: *by the diligence of reading*.

->[Front Cover]<-

<note>This page contains no text, as it is the decorative outer cover of the bound volume. The binding is a fine example of historical craftsmanship, featuring a <term>marbled</term> or "tree calf" leather finish. This distinctive mottled pattern was created by treating the leather with acidic solutions to produce a permanent stain resembling the grain of wood. The cover is further embellished with a <term>gold-tooled</term> border, where thin sheets of gold leaf were pressed into the leather using heated brass stamps. Such ornate decoration indicates that the contents within were highly valued by the collector or owner.</note>

<summary>The outer front cover of the manuscript, featuring brown mottled leather and an intricate gold-leaf border.</summary>

<keywords>front cover, bookbinding, marbled leather, tree calf, gold tooling, ornamentation, luxury manuscript</keywords>
